Pipeline v3
Lecture videos to a knowledge layer, activities and cited answers
Pipeline v3 · documentation · as of 2026-11-17

From lecture video to a course that answers back

Pipeline v3 reads a course's subtitles and builds a knowledge layer: the facts each lecture teaches, the concepts they name, how those concepts relate, what each lecture is for and which lectures need which. On top of it sit activities checked by a judge, and a question box that answers only from the course and cites the lecture and the minute. Every claim traces back to a sentence of a lecture.

63lectures of NPTEL Foundation for Virtual and Augmented Reality Systems
386segments, each one sub-goal
2,006facts, each citing its sentences
3,122concepts after the synonym merge
1,316with a written definition (the glossary)
7,427relationships between concepts
1,168activities of ten kinds; the six model-made kinds judged
4.5 smedian answer time (vLLM, 17 questions)

Every number and example on this page comes from a file in the repository, a commit message, or local run logs not in git (ec2_sync/, output/*/qa/), marked (local) where cited; the path is given next to it. The course is the VR course (pipeline/output/nptel_vr/), as rebuilt on 2026-11-17 (commit d3896b9). Where something was not measured, the page says so.

How to read this page

Part I gives the whole pipeline in five minutes, how it came to be (v1, v2, v3), how it compares with simpler approaches, and every argument for the design. Part II goes stage by stage: what each does, its prompt in full, a real input and output, its time and the changes made to it. Part III lists every evaluation set and what fails where. Part IV is how to run it and the reference (Python API, command lines, HTTP routes, configs, the demo). Part V is the known limits, the fixes coming next and future work; the Appendix indexes every prompt and schema and defines the terms. Select any text and press Highlight to keep it; your highlights are listed under ✎ Highlights, bottom right.

Part I · Overview and story

What the pipeline is, how it came to be this way, and why it is built as it is.

The pipeline in five minutes

A course goes in as subtitle files. It comes out as a folder, knowledge/, that a program can query, plus activities built from it. The heavy model work happens once, on a GPU server. Answering a learner's question later needs only a small model and the folder.

Every stage in one line

StageWhat it doesWhoOut (VR course)Time
0aSubtitle cues become numbered sentences with start and end timescode11,893 sentencesseconds
1aThe model cuts each lecture into segments, one sub-goal eachQwen429 proposed19 min
1bCode checks the cuts; segments under 2 or over 15 min go back to the modelcode + Qwen386 segments≈ 8 min

Times are wall time on one RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell (96 GB) on EC2, from ec2_sync/queue/queue.log (local) (the 2026-11-17 rebuild), pipeline/output/logs/qwen_vllm_1a.log and the notebook's run notes. * 2c ran on an L40S (46 GB) during the 2026-11-13 MVP run, with 32 parallel requests; it was not rerun on the larger GPU. † With the fact embeddings already cached. Time estimates has the details.

The idea in one paragraph

Do the expensive, careful work once: cut each lecture where its goals change, extract what it teaches as facts with evidence, name the concepts, join their names across lectures, write one definition per concept, and work out which lectures depend on which. Store it all with IDs that lead back to sentences and times. Then every later use (an answer, a quiz, a concept map, a list of prerequisites) is a small, checkable step over that store, and every claim it makes can be shown at the moment of the video where it was said.

One lecture as data

Every object has an ID that says where it lives, and every one traces down to sentences. This is lecture 30, Auditory Sense & Immersive Experience, followed from one sentence to the course goal.

ThingIDHoldsMade in
Sentence30/157text, start and end time (null when it starts inside a subtitle cue)0a
Segment30/S4first and last sentence, start, end, sub-goal1a–1c
Fact30/S4/F6text, evidence (sentence IDs)2a (2b)
Segment concept30/S4/C8name, aliases, facts, defined (true/false)2c
Segment relationship30/S4/R7subject, label, object (segment concepts), facts2c
ConceptC1574name, aliases, facts, lectures, segments, defined_in, how each segment concept was merged in, definition3, 4a
RelationshipR3343subject and object (concept IDs), label, facts, segments3c
Objective / lecture goal / course goal30/O2, 30/G, G4goal, achieves, its segments / objectives / lectures4b
Lecture link29 → 30the concepts lecture 30 uses that 29 defines; direct, indirect or weak4c

Why this pipeline

Three versions were built on the same course in three weeks. Each one failed in ways that could be read in its output, and each failure changed the design. This part tells that story with the real failed outputs and the prompts that produced them, then compares the design with simpler ways of doing the same job, and says plainly which claims are measured and which are argued.

How we got here: v1, v2, v3

v1: one LIR file per lecture, built on a 12 GB card

v1 turned each lecture into a guided sequence: recall questions, short clips, a quiz and a summary after each clip, a concept map and open questions at the end. It ran on one RTX 5070 with Gemma 4 E4B and a cheap API judge. It worked end to end, and its documentation (the page this one follows) is honest about its gaps. Three of them shaped v3.

The three failures, with v1's shipped output and its prompt registry merges · no fact store · topic-change segments

1. Concepts were merged by their names. v1's registry rule: “the same name or abbreviation means the same concept; otherwise embedding similarity decides (≥ 0.93 the same, 0.78–0.93 the model is asked yes or no)”. An abbreviation is not a name: “ER” is also error rates, empirical research and entity-relationship. One wrong merge then pulls in the next. This is v1's most central concept, as shipped:

v1 · course.json, C001
{
 "id": "C001",
 "name": "Extended reality",
 "aliases": [
  "XR",
  "ER",
  "Extended reality environment",
  "Error rates",
  "Empirical research",
  "Entity-Relationship",
  "Enhanced realism",
  "Extended reality domain"
 ],
 "definition": "Extended reality (XR) refers to extending reality by some means. Systematically, it involves using systems to either augment our reality or replace our perception of reality."
}
v3 · knowledge/concepts.json, C0001
{
 "id": "C0001",
 "name": "extended reality",
 "aliases": [
  "XR"
 ],
 "definition": "Extended reality (XR) is an umbrella term, not a specific technology, that covers all present or future immersive technologies, such as augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and mixed reality (MR).",
 "defined_in": [
  {
   "lecture": "1",
   "segment": "1/S1",
   "start": 698.411
  },
  {
   "lecture": "2",
   "segment": "2/S0",
   "start": 111.024
  }
 ]
}

v3 fixes this at three places. A concept's name is always the full term, and an acronym is only ever an alias (decision D8, prompts/concept_extraction.txt rule 4). Stage 3a never merges two entries because they share an acronym; a shared acronym only makes them candidates. And stage 3b asks the model to decide from the facts, not from the names alone (prompts/concept_same.txt), with the rule that a wrong merge costs more than a missed one.

2. There was no store of what a lecture says. v1 wrote key points and key terms per part and judged each one. A term the judge rejected left the registry with its links, which is why the course ended with 883 concepts. A question that no key point happened to cover had nothing to be answered from. v3 makes the fact the unit: one complete point of subject knowledge, with the sentences that state it. Concepts, relationships, definitions, activities and answers are all built from facts, so they all cite sentences.

3. Segments were cut at topic changes. v1 used the best prompt from a segmentation study (WindowDiff 0.09 against a human annotator on lecture 1, see the measured comparison). On the whole course it gave parts “4.7 minutes long on average; 16 are flagged as long”. The same prompt on the large v2 run gave “36 parts over 10 min and one of 28 min (L51/P04: a whole worked problem that the ‘stand alone’ rule would not cut)” (notebook, record of 2026-11-09). The prompt:

v1 prompt segment.standalone v3 lir-construction/pipeline/prompts.py on branch v1 
Below is the transcript of a lecture video, one sentence per line, each with its number in brackets.

Split the lecture into segments. Find the apt boundaries: the points where the instructor has completed an instructional goal or a topic and moves on to the next one.

Each segment must stand alone: a learner who starts watching at the first sentence of any segment, without having seen what came before, must be able to follow it. Cut only at a logically concluded point, after the instructor has finished the thought, never while he is in the middle of explaining something. A question and the discussion of that question belong in the same segment. Do not cut at every change of example or sentence-level aside; prefer fewer, complete segments over many fragments.

Answer with a JSON list of the sentence numbers that START a new segment (do not include sentence 1), in order, and nothing else. Example of the format: [14, 52, 97]

Transcript:
{transcript}

“Each segment must stand alone” sounds harmless. A capable model reads it literally: its thinking on lecture 51 in v3's run 3 says “'So, given this background…' … Therefore, Segment 2 and 3 must be merged.” (analysis/segmentation/report.md). v3 defines a segment by its sub-goal (what the learner can do after it), limits “stand alone” to real dependencies and says outright that connectives are not one, and moves the length rule into code that only says “looks too small / too big” (stage 1). Result: 386 segments, none over 15 minutes.

v2: addresses, judges everywhere, and a prompt that grew with the course

v2 (LIR 2.1) gave every item a sentence address, a judge for every item with one rewrite, and a decision log. It moved to vLLM on an RTX PRO 6000. The small setup (E4B writes, 12B judges) built all 63 lectures; its analysis reads: “The knowledge per part is as good as v1 or better, and the v1 registry problems are mostly gone. Two steps failed silently on the second half of the course: 27 of 63 lectures have no links, and 20 lectures have no open questions. The run took 2.7× the planned time, because the 12B judge did most of the work.”

The four failures, with v2's links prompt and what it produced links overflow · judge cost · fragmented registry · open questions

The links prompt. One call per lecture listed every relation between that lecture's concepts. Its input block carried, for each concept, what every earlier lecture taught about it. The prompt text is fine; the input grows with the course:

v2 prompt links.lecture v1 lir-construction/pipeline2/prompts.py 
Task: list every relation between two of these concepts that this lecture states or clearly implies. Relations, read as "a relation b":
- kind_of: a is a specific kind of the more general b
- part_of: a is a component or part of b
- depends_on: to understand a, a learner must first understand b
For each relation give:
- a, relation, b: the concept labels and the relation;
- label: a phrase of 2 to 6 words that sits between the two names, so that "<name of a> <label> <name of b>" reads as one true, natural sentence a student would understand, for example "provides the display for" or "is a type of"; specific to what the lecture says; without the concept names;
- evidence: the numbers of the sentences above that show the relation.
Leave out relations the sentences do not support. List each pair of concepts at most once.
What it produced

“Every links.lecture call for those lectures was cut at length (56 of 92 calls; 30 retries). The prompt grows with every earlier facet of every concept: up to 63.9k input tokens against a 65.5k context. Result: 57% of concepts have no link.” Lectures L21, L27, L31 and L37–L63 (except L43, L46, L48) got none.

v3 finds relationships inside one segment, from that segment's facts only (stage 2c), and joins them across lectures in code (stage 3c). A prompt then never grows with the course: the 779 calls of 2c averaged about 3,700 input tokens (2,890,550 in all, stage_2c_concepts/llm/calls.jsonl). Every lecture has relationships; the course has 7,427. The price is decision D4 in the plan: a relationship between concepts taught in different segments is never found. It is listed under future work.

Judges set the run time. “2,711 judge + 1,035 rewrite calls: 58% of requests, 12.1 M of 18.0 M output tokens, 90%+ of it thinking, p50 latency 400 s.” v3 judges only what a learner sees (activities), caps thinking at 6,144 tokens, and measured its fact judge before letting it change anything. That measurement switched it off (stage 2b).

Fragmented, noisy registry. 1,019 of v2's 1,395 concepts appeared once; some aliases were sentences (“A virtual reality system is an artificial environment which…”). v3 checks every alias against the facts (alias_not_written: an alias must be written in a fact), never takes a sentence as a name, and joins the names with an exact merge plus a model decision per cluster.

Open questions dropped by a check. “70 questions dropped by the code check ‘each key point needs 2 keywords word for word in the reference answer’.” v3 ties a reference answer to its key facts (at least two) instead of keywords; every lecture has an open-question activity.

v3: what changed inside the version

v3 was built “small first” (lectures 1, 4 and 16), with a report and a review at each gate, then run on the full course as an MVP on 2026-11-13 (git tag mvp-2026-11-13), then rebuilt on 2026-11-17 with the stages the MVP had skipped. The table lists the changes with their measured effect; each is told in full in its stage's section.

ChangeBeforeAfterWhere
Segment prompt by sub-goal; length check in code with retryrun 3: 12 segments over 15 min, longest 27.9 min0 over 15 min, longest 14.5 min1
A fact is “one complete point of subject knowledge”lecture 1: 65 facts, course talk included20 facts2a
Fact judge measured against gold, then switched offcaught 1 of 7 real failures, 15% of verdicts flipfacts pass through unchanged2b
Synonym merge (3b) runMVP: 3,384 concepts3,122 (262 model merges)3
Definitions written by the model from the factsMVP: a concept's first defining fact; about 26% read like a definition1,316 concepts defined4a
Activity prompts reviewed; code checks; judgecorrect quiz option the longest in 52% of questions14% (160 of 1,163)5
Recap judge: “judge the meaning, not the wording”1,465 judge failures (969 in recaps)1,086 (593 in recaps; 3 of the drop from the odd-one-out rerun)5
One rewrite on a judge fail285 quiz and 62 open-question items failed102 and 245
QA: thinking only in round 0, early exit, streamingmedian 5.4 s, max 16.2 s4.9 s, max 8.9 s (local); 4.5 s on vLLMQA
Viewer: lean routes, caching, paced videohome through an SSH tunnel 9.3 s1.1 sViewer

Against simpler approaches

Three simpler designs could answer a learner's question about a course. Here is how this pipeline differs from each, illustrated with real outputs. None of the three was run on this course yet, so this section is an argument from design; the experiment that measures it is described under Measured comparison, and a separate run is under way.

(a) Plain LLM

Ask a model, no course data

It answers from what it learned in training. It cannot say which lecture taught a thing or at what minute, it cannot say “this course does not cover that”, and its definitions are the textbook's, not the lecturer's.

(b) RAG over transcripts

Search the lecture text, answer from chunks

It can cite lectures. But a chunk of speech is full of “this”, “it”, “as we saw”, acronyms and asides; a chunk that mentions a term is retrieved as readily as the one that defines it; and no chunk holds “what do I need before lecture 9”.

(c) GraphRAG alone

An entity graph built from chunks

It adds entities and relations, often with community summaries. Without facts that cite sentences, a course order, a lecture graph and goals, it can relate concepts but not tell a learner where one is taught or what comes first.

What the knowledge layer gives that these do not, each with a real example:

  • Every claim leads to the video. An answer cites facts; each fact cites sentences; sentences have times. The learner sees “(lecture 8, 26:40)” and clicks it. From the vLLM benchmark (analysis/qa/bench_vllm_shipped.jsonl, B09): 
    {
 "question": "In which lecture is stereoscopy taught?",
 "seconds": 2.53,
 "steps": [
  {
   "tool": "check_concept",
   "args": {
    "name": "stereoscopy"
   },
   "result_summary": "card of stereoscopy; 2 facts"
  }
 ],
 "covered": true
}

Stereoscopy is defined in lecture 28, "Visual Perception & Displays II" (lecture 28, 14:25). It is also used in lecture 14 during the discussion of head-mounted displays (lecture 14, 12:31).
  • “Not covered” instead of a made-up answer. Vergence-accommodation conflict is a real VR topic this course does not teach. The answer says so, then offers what the course does say (B02): 
    {
 "question": "What is the vergence-accommodation conflict in VR headsets?",
 "seconds": 4.52,
 "steps": [
  {
   "tool": "check_concept",
   "args": {
    "name": "vergence-accommodation conflict"
   },
   "result_summary": "unknown name 'vergence-accommodation conflict'; closest fact 0.48 (low)"
  }
 ],
 "covered": false
}

The course does not specifically cover the "vergence-accommodation conflict." The closest related topics discussed are:

* In binocular vision, when both eyes focus on infinity, they see the same image over a common region of about 120 degrees, while areas outside this region are seen by only one eye (lecture 28, 13:06).
* The human eye does not have a focal plane as such because its non-planar, spherically curved retina replaces the focal plane (lecture 28, 3:11).
* A single lens of at least 40 diopters would be required to converge parallel rays onto the center of the retina because the outer diameter of the human eyeball is approximately 24 millimeters (lecture 28, 6:57).
    A plain LLM would explain the conflict from its own knowledge, correctly perhaps, but not from this course.
  • Prerequisites from the lecture graph. “What do I need before lecture 30?” is answered from stage 4c, not from text that happens to match (B14): 
    {
 "question": "What do I need to know before lecture 30?",
 "seconds": 3.65,
 "steps": [
  {
   "tool": "lecture_dependencies",
   "args": {
    "lecture": "30"
   },
   "result_summary": "lecture 30: revise 29; relies on 1 earlier concepts, 2 general, 4 in passing, 5 assumed"
  }
 ],
 "covered": true
}

Before lecture 30, you should be familiar with the following concepts:

* **Frequency**: This is a property of a sound wave measured in hertz (Hz), representing the number of changes from one maximum to another maximum per second (lecture 29, 10:59; lecture 29, 11:19).
* **Extended Reality (XR)**: Introduced in lecture 1.
* **Virtual Reality (VR)**: Defined in lecture 4.

Lecture 30 also relies on lecture 29, "Auditory Sense & Immersive Experience."
  • Definitions with “where it is defined”. Each defined concept has one definition, written from its defining facts, with the lecture, segment and time where it is taught (the concept card).
  • Facts are cleaned before they are searched. A transcript sentence: “And it depends on the size and shape of the head, ears, and ear canal.” The fact built from it and two others: “Head-related transfer functions (HRTF) depend on the size and shape of the head, ears, and ear canal, and every person has a unique HRTF.” The pronoun is resolved, the acronym spelled out, the point whole. fact_lookup is retrieval too, but over 2,006 such facts, not over 206,605 words of speech.
  • Activities from the same verified store. A quiz question cites the facts that answer it, and a judge checked it against them (stage 5). Neither RAG nor a plain LLM produces a course's quizzes, crosswords and concept maps with that link.

The one component that was measured against baselines: segmentation

Before v1, a study compared LLM segmentation with classical methods on lecture 1 against a human annotator (event-segmentation/compare/REPORT.md). WindowDiff, lower is better; 0.45 is what no cuts at all score.

MethodSegments (human: 7)WindowDiff
Gemma 4, one pass, standalone-segments prompt, temperature 080.09
TextTiling, best window40.28
TSVLT (published model, trained with its own code)2301.00
TopicTiling480.99

Sampled runs are less reliable: at the sampling temperature, the same prompt scored WindowDiff 0.08 to 0.49 (REPORT.md), and v3 samples at temperature 1.0. One lecture and one annotator: a small test. v3's segmentation was then measured for run-to-run stability (boundary F1 0.75 between seeds), not against a human again.

Measured comparison (baseline tests)

Placeholder · to be filled by a later pass

A separate run is testing three baselines on this course, writing into baseline-tests/: a plain LLM with no course data, RAG over the lecture transcripts, and a graph-only approach. When it finishes, this subsection gets its numbers: answers correct, claims backed by the course, citations that point at the right lecture and time, correct “not covered” answers, latency and tokens per question. Until then, nothing on this page claims a measured advantage over these baselines.

Why this design

Every argument for the design, each marked measured when a number in the repository backs it, or argued when it rests on the design and examples only.

ArgumentWhat it means, with the evidenceStatus
Heavy work once, offlineThe 63 lectures are processed once (about 10.8 GPU hours, summed from the stage logs). Answering reads a precomputed folder, knowledge/ (31 MB, of which 21 MB are fact embeddings). Nothing is re-read or re-summarised at question time.measured
Light inference per questionA 12B model answers: Gemma 4 12B QAT, a 6.72 GB GGUF file. Median 4.9 s on a 12 GB RTX 5070 (34 answers, bench_think_newlayer.jsonl), 4.5 s on vLLM (17 answers). A median question takes 2 model calls.measured
Little context per questionTools return only matched facts and concept cards. Over the 49 questions in the local QA log (pipeline/output/nptel_vr/qa/answers.jsonl (local)), a question used a median 4,783 input tokens over all its model calls (max 11,526) and wrote 283. In the benchmark the final call's prompt is a median 2,805 tokens. For scale: one lecture's subtitles are a median 3,135 words; the 63 segmentation prompts, each one lecture's transcript, total 416,620 tokens.measured
Checked before a learner sees itEvery model answer passes code checks with a retry; every activity unit is judged (3,911 items, 1,086 failed), one rewrite is tried, and items failing the criteria that matter are hidden. The activity judge itself is not yet measured against its seed set. Honest limits: the fact judge is off, and recap texts the judge failed are still shown.measured (counts); judge accuracy not measured
TraceableEvery claim cites a fact ID, and a fact ID becomes “(lecture N, m:ss)”. Stage 4d checks 206,903 references in the layer, all resolve. The answer loop drops any ID no tool returned. In the benchmarks every answer was read: no wrong citation was seen (answers read, not scored).measured
Honest “not covered”A calibrated cut-off on retrieval scores (0.55) separates 80 labelled queries with AUC 0.989; it flags 27 of 39 out-of-course queries and none of the 38 in-course ones (analysis/qa/calibration.json). The FIFA question is answered “not covered” in every run.measured
Code where possible, a model where neededTimes, segment maps, the registry merge, the lecture graph, concept maps, complete-the-map, match-the-pairs, recall, crossword layout and review cards are code. A model writes only facts, concepts, “same?” decisions, definitions, goals, text activities and answers.argued
Cheap rerunsEvery model answer is cached by a hash of the request. A rerun only calls the model for what changed: the 2026-11-17 rewrite job found its 410 writer answers in the cache in 4 s (ec2_sync/queue/logs/10.log (local)).measured
Model- and engine-agnosticOne client for vLLM and llama.cpp, configs in JSON. Qwen 3.8 27B built the layer; Gemma 4 12B answers; the demo ran both on llama.cpp (check_demo.sh ALL PASS) and on vLLM (benchmark).measured
Bounded promptsUnits are a segment, a cluster, a concept or a lecture, so no prompt grows with the course (v2's links prompt reached 63.9k tokens). 2c averaged about 3,700 input tokens per call.measured
Structure a learner can useGoals (8 course goals, 125 objectives), prerequisites per lecture, concepts introduced / met again / assumed, activities and review cards. RAG returns passages; it has none of these.argued
Inference apart from the appOnly text crosses the network: the request (question and tool results, a few thousand tokens) and the streamed answer. Videos, the layer and the page stay on the learner's machine (below).argued (being built)
Modest hardware to serveServing runs on the 12 GB RTX 5070 used throughout this work. Building needs more: the 27B FP8 model needs about 28 GB of weights alone and was run on 46 GB and 96 GB cards.measured
Tested before trustedSmall first (lectures 1, 4, 16), gold sets, seeded errors, a reviewer at each gate. This is how the fact judge was found to do more harm than good before it touched the full course.measured

Where it runs: the model on a server, the app with the learner

This split works because the pipeline does the heavy work in advance. What the server must do per question is small and stateless: run one model over a prompt of a few thousand tokens, a median of two times. Everything that is large (videos: 5.5 GB for the course) or private to the learner (progress, highlights, answers) stays local. The client already supports a model on another machine: a config with "launch": false and a port (or url) attaches to a running server, which is how every build stage reached the EC2 through an SSH port forward (pipeline/ec2.md).

Part II · The pipeline

Stage by stage

Each stage below gives what it does and why, its inputs and outputs, the command, the model and config, how long it took on the VR course, its prompt in full (click to open), a real input and output from lecture 30, and the changes made to it with the reason. All paths are under pipeline/; outputs under pipeline/output/nptel_vr/.

Common to every model stage
  • One client (scripts/llm/) for vLLM and llama.cpp, both through the OpenAI chat API. A config file (configs/*.json) sets the engine, model, sampling, thinking budget and server flags.
  • Answers are constrained to a JSON schema built by code, then parsed and checked. A failed check is sent back to the model with the problems listed (“Your answer has these problems: … Answer again in the same format, fixing these problems and changing nothing else.”), up to the config's retries. When every attempt fails, the attempt with the fewest problems is kept and marked unresolved (decision D12).
  • Every answer is cached in the stage's llm/cache.jsonl under a hash of the request (engine, model path and request body: prompt, schema, sampling, token limits). The same request is never sent twice; a prompt change reruns only the requests it changes. Every call is logged in llm/calls.jsonl (tokens, time, errors).
  • All requests of a stage go out at once (generate_many), and the server batches them: up to 128 at a time on the 96 GB GPU. Qwen 3.8 27B FP8 on vLLM produced about 1,650–1,850 output tokens per second with the batch full, and about 45 tokens per second for a single request (ec2_sync/queue/logs/ (local)).
  • Prompts are plain text files in prompts/ with {placeholders}. Most begin with the course block (prompts/course_meta.txt: the course outline and syllabus from course.json), so the engine reuses its cached prefix across calls.
prompts/course_meta.txt5 lines · 233 characters · the full file
THE COURSE
Use this to understand what the course covers as a whole: its goals and the order of its topics.
Outline: {outline}
Syllabus (the planned topics in order; a lecture may cover part of a topic or several topics):
{syllabus}
0a

Preprocess: subtitles to timed sentences

code

Every later stage works with sentences: the segmenter reads them, segments are runs of them, facts cite them, and their times turn a citation into a moment of the video. Stage 0a joins all subtitle cues of a lecture into one stream, splits it at sentence ends and gives each sentence an ID and the times of the cues it came from.

In
input/nptel_vr/input/subs/**/lec_<n>.srt (human-written subtitles: their punctuation is trusted)
Out
stage_0a_preprocess/lec_<n>.json sentences {"30/0": "Hello and welcome …"}; times/lec_<n>.json {"30/0": {"start", "end"}}; times/cues/ the cue timeline; times/meta.json the speaking rate (VR: 143 words per minute)
Command
python scripts/s0a_preprocess.py nptel_vr
Model
none
Time
seconds: “63 lectures, 11893 sentences (364 with a boundary inside a cue), median 143.0 words per minute” (output/logs/qwen_vllm_0a.log)
Code
scripts/s0a_preprocess.py, scripts/lectures.py (lecture keys: lec_24b.srt → 24b, tut_1)

The rule. A sentence ends at ., ? or ! (with any closing quote or bracket), followed by a space and a capital letter, a digit or an opening quote. Dr., Prof., e.g., i.e., vs., Fig., Eq. are not ends; MR. (mixed reality) is. A lowercase word after a full stop does not start a sentence: in the VR course that full stop is the mistake (286 times, “…technologies. which, …”). A sentence keeps an exact time only where it starts or ends at a cue's edge; otherwise that side is null, and no time is made up.

We are going to continue our discussion on our auditory sense and the immersive experience.

So, previously we talked about how sound is relevant for immersive experiences, what is sound, and

4
00:00:52,107 --> 00:00:55,772
how our auditory sense is structured.

 "30/2": "So, previously we talked about how sound is relevant for immersive experiences, what is sound, and how our auditory sense is structured."

Changes and why

  • Rule-based splitter, kept after a comparison with pySBD on six courses: it finds 94–99.7% of pySBD's boundaries, and where they disagree ours was right in the samples read (pySBD splits “…this is the result is n by ‖ n.”). analysis/preprocessing/report.md.
  • MIT caption conventions (speaker labels, sound tags, the OCW licence notice) handled so the same code runs on five other courses; the VR output stayed byte-identical.
  • Times of a segment boundary inside a cue, three versions: forward from the nearest exact time (VR median error 1.4 s, max 14.4 s), both sides from the nearest exact sentence times (0.9 / 7.4 s), then both sides from the edges of the cue the boundary is in (0.4 / 5.8 s; in Programming in C the max fell from 264 s to 12 s). Only segment boundaries are estimated (in stage 1b); sentences keep null. Example, lecture 57 sentence 206, 4 words into a 33-word cue from 2129.808 s to 2146.991 s: forward 2131.49 s, backward 2134.82 s, estimate 2133.16 s.
1a–1c

Segmentation: one sub-goal per segment

Qwen 3.8 27Bcode checks

A learner should be able to watch a segment on its own and come away able to do one thing. Stage 1a asks the model, once per lecture, where each sub-goal starts and what it is. Stage 1b checks the answer in code and, when a check fails, sends it back with the problem. Code never merges, splits or moves a cut itself. Stage 1c writes one text file per segment for the next stages.

In
0a sentences; course.json (outline and syllabus); titles.tsv
Out
stage_1a_segmentation/raw/lec_<n>.json (the answer, its reasoning, tokens); stage_1b_verify_segments/segment_maps/lec_<n>.json (segment → first and last sentence, start, end, sub-goal, estimated when a time is estimated); retry/lec_<n>.json (every attempt); report.json; stage_1c_segment_texts/lec_<n>/S<k>.txt
Command
python scripts/s1a_segmentation.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json [--only 1-5] [--sequential]
python scripts/s1b_verify_segments.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json [--only 1-5] (without --llm: checks only)
python scripts/s1c_segment_texts.py nptel_vr [--only 1-5]
Model
Qwen 3.8 27B FP8 on vLLM, configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json: temperature 1.0, top_p 0.95, top_k 20, thinking capped at 16,384 tokens, 32 parallel requests
Time
1a: 63 calls in 1,113 s (19 min), 6,613 input and 12,572 output tokens per call on average (16 calls ran to the thinking cap). 1b: 37 retry calls, about 8 minutes. RTX PRO 6000, 2026-11-12.
Result
63/63 lectures pass; 386 segments, 3–12 per lecture, median 4.1 min, longest 14.5 min; 33 lectures retried for size (29 once, 4 twice); 1 segment still flagged and kept by the model (41/S9, 1 min 39 s)

The prompts

The 1a prompt is the task first, then the course block, the lecture's title, the definitions (sub-goal, segment, what is not a sub-goal, where to cut, standing alone), a working method, the answer format and the transcript numbered from [1]. The answer is {"reasoning", "segments": [{"start", "subgoal"}]}: the reasoning comes first so the model commits to why, and 1b sends it back in a retry. The two retry prompts are appended to the conversation: the 1a prompt, the model's last answer, then the retry. The model is told which segments “look too small” or “look too big”, never the bounds or the durations, and it may keep a segment it judges right.

prompts/segmentation.txt43 lines · 5,175 characters · the full file
TASK
Divide the transcript of one lecture into segments. Each segment is the part of the lecture in which the learner achieves one sub-goal, and each segment must be one that a learner can watch independently of the others.

{course}

THIS LECTURE
This lecture is titled "{lecture_title}".

SEGMENTS AND SUB-GOALS
- The course has goals: what a learner should understand and be able to do by the end of it. The outline and syllabus above describe them. Every lecture moves the learner a step closer to them.
- A sub-goal is one such step that this lecture achieves: something the learner can do after that part of the lecture that they could not do before, such as explain a concept, describe how a system works, compare two approaches or solve a type of problem. Taken in order, the sub-goals of a lecture are the learner's task list for that lecture.
- A sub-goal is a whole step toward the course goals, not a piece of one. The examples, illustrations, details and digressions the lecturer uses to achieve a sub-goal are part of that sub-goal.
- A segment is the run of consecutive sentences in which the lecturer achieves one sub-goal.

WHAT IS NOT A SUB-GOAL
- Some parts of a lecture are about the lecture itself, not about the course goals: the greeting, the course name, the lecturer introducing themselves, a recap of the previous lecture, an outline of this lecture, the closing summary, a preview of the next lecture and the goodbye. These are not sub-goals.
- The opening remarks belong to the first segment, never to a segment of their own. The closing remarks belong to the last segment, or may be the last segment on their own; its "subgoal" then says what they do ("Summarize the main points of the lecture.").

WHERE TO CUT
- Cut where a sub-goal has been achieved and closed: the lecturer has finished it, often with a concluding remark ("So, that is how tracking works."), and moves on to the next one.
- A bridge belongs to the segment that follows it. A bridge is a sentence that leads into the next part: it announces, motivates or asks about what comes next ("Now, let us see how we can measure presence.", "So, what is the problem with this approach?", "Next, we will talk about displays."). The new segment starts at the bridge, not after it. A concluding remark stays with the part it concludes.
- Never cut inside an explanation, a derivation, a worked example, or between a question and its answer.

EACH SEGMENT MUST STAND ALONE
- A learner must be able to follow any segment by itself, without having seen the segment before it.
- What breaks this is a real dependency: the segment needs something that only the previous segment explains and that the segment itself never names or restates. Examples: "these three values" when the values are not given again, "this algorithm" when the algorithm is never named, "the matrix we just computed" when the segment works with that matrix without saying what it is.
- Connectives are not dependencies. A segment may open with "So,", "Now,", "Given this background,", "Now that we know what presence is," or a short recap of what came before, as long as what it builds on is named. A segment may also build on what earlier parts taught when it names it ("Head-mounted displays, which we discussed earlier, ..."). Never merge segments or move a cut because of a connective.
- If a segment has a real dependency, look for a cut a few sentences away that avoids it, such as at the bridge. Merge it with the previous segment only when its sub-goal cannot be understood apart from the previous one.

HOW TO WORK
1. Read the whole transcript. Decide what this lecture contributes to the course goals, and list its sub-goals in the order they are achieved.
2. For each sub-goal, find the sentence where it begins (with its bridge) and the sentence where it is closed.
3. Check every segment: it achieves one whole sub-goal; it ends where that sub-goal is closed; it starts with its own bridge; it has no real dependency on the segment before it; the opening remarks are in the first segment.

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with two fields, in this order:
"reasoning": in a few sentences, why you chose this segmentation: the sub-goals you found and why you cut where you did, including any cut you moved or segments you merged because of a dependency.
"segments": a list with one object per segment, in order. "start" is the number of the segment's first sentence; the first segment starts at 1. "subgoal" says in one short sentence what the learner can do after the segment, starting with a verb ("Explain why ...", "Describe how ...").
Answer with the JSON only. Example of the format:
{"reasoning": "The lecture first defines extended reality and then explains presence. I cut at sentence 38, where the lecturer asks how a headset creates presence, because that question is the bridge into the second sub-goal.", "segments": [{"start": 1, "subgoal": "Explain what extended reality means and how VR, AR and MR relate to it."}, {"start": 38, "subgoal": "Describe how a head-mounted display creates a sense of presence."}]}

Transcript (one sentence per line, each with its number in brackets):
{transcript}
prompts/segmentation_structural_retry.txt8 lines · 440 characters · the full file
Your answer breaks the rules of the answer format:
{problems}
Fix these problems. Keep the rest of your segmentation as it is unless a fix requires changing it.

Answer with a JSON object with two fields, in this order:
"reasoning": in a few sentences, what you changed to fix each problem.
"segments": the complete list of segments for the whole lecture, in order, each {"start": ..., "subgoal": ...} as before.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/segmentation_logical_retry.txt12 lines · 958 characters · the full file
Some of your segments look too small or too big:
{problems}
Check these segments again, carefully, against the instructions above.
- For a segment that looks too small: see whether it and the segments next to it together make a more meaningful sub-goal. If they do, combine them into one segment.
- For a segment that looks too big: see whether it can be divided into more manageable sub-goals, each a whole step that is closed before the next one begins. If it can, cut it at those points.

Every segment must still achieve one whole sub-goal, end where that sub-goal is closed, start at its bridge and stand alone. If a segment is right as it is, keep it.

Answer with a JSON object with two fields, in this order:
"reasoning": for each segment listed above, what you changed, or why you kept it as it was.
"segments": the complete list of segments for the whole lecture, in order, each {"start": ..., "subgoal": ...} as before.
Answer with the JSON only.

1b's checks

CheckKindOn a failure
The answer is a non-empty list; integer starts; the first segment starts at sentence 1; starts in range and increasing; segments exhaustive, exclusive, contiguous, non-emptystructural (hard)up to 2 structural retries per answer; an answer that fails is never kept
A segment under 2 minutes (not the last) or over 15 minuteslogical (soft)up to 2 logical retries per lecture; the kept answer is the valid one with the fewest flagged segments

A real run: lecture 30

1c writes each segment as one sentence per line, the format later stages read:

[30/130] Now, for localization, we can rely on localization cues.
[30/131] Now, there can be different cues: monaural involvement of a single ear to perform localization, and binaural, where both ears are involved.
[30/132] So, either we can deploy one ear for localization or we can deploy both ears.

Changes and why

RunModelWhat changedSegmentsOver 15 minLongest
1Gemma 4 E4Bv1/v2's prompt, unchanged557218.3 min
2Gemma 4 E4Bnew prompt: segments by sub-goal; bridges go to the next segment; stand alone622216.8 min
3Gemma 4 12Bsame prompt, larger model3551227.9 min
4Gemma 4 12Btask first; course outline; sub-goals defined by the course's goals; stand alone limited to real dependencies431630.8 min
4 + 1bGemma 4 12Bverification with retries (“look too small / too big”)391014.6 min
currentQwen 3.8 27Brun 4's prompt with a reasoning field; 1b386014.5 min
  • A small model ignores the finer rules; a capable one applies them literally. The 4B labelled greetings as sub-goals and cut list items apart; the 12B followed “stand alone” into 28-minute segments (“Therefore, Segment 2 and 3 must be merged.”). The fix was rules it cannot over-apply: “Connectives are not dependencies. … Never merge segments or move a cut because of a connective.”
  • Length is checked in code, not prompted. A 6–15 minute rule in the prompt was considered and dropped: run 1's median segment was 2.4 minutes (557 segments), so code would have done the segmenting. Two retry wordings were tested on 8 lectures; the one with a direction (“see whether small ones together make a more meaningful sub-goal”) kept the lecturer's structure better than the bare “check them”.
  • Run to run, about three quarters of the cuts reproduce: boundary F1 0.75 between seeds of the same pipeline, WindowDiff 0.15. The disagreement is in lectures that enumerate items (lecture 8: six qualities of immersion). Qwen 27B against Gemma 12B: F1 0.76, about the same as Gemma against itself. Later stages should not treat a boundary as exact.
  • A dependency judge was tried and left out (prompts/segmentation_judge.txt, below): no false alarm on 6 controls, but 2 of 10 borderline segments flipped between two runs. Decision of 2026-11-11: not part of the pipeline.
prompts/segmentation_judge.txt35 lines · 2,403 characters · the full file
TASK
You check one segment of a lecture: can a learner start watching the lecture at this segment and follow it?

{course}

THIS LECTURE
This lecture is titled "{lecture_title}".

THE LEARNER
The learner has watched all the earlier lectures of this course, and knows what the outline and syllabus above describe. They have not watched the earlier part of this lecture: they start at the segment below.

WHAT COUNTS AS A DEPENDENCY
- The segment depends on the earlier part of the lecture when it needs something that only the earlier part explains and that the segment itself never names or restates, so that a learner starting here cannot follow its main point. Examples: "these three values" when the values are not given again; "this algorithm" when the algorithm is never named; "the R3 question" when the questions R1 to R3 are defined only in the earlier part.
- These are not dependencies:
  - connectives and transitions: "So,", "Now,", "Given this background,", or "This brings us to ..." followed by what it brings us to;
  - a short recap, or a reference to something earlier that the segment names ("Head-mounted displays, which we discussed earlier, ...");
  - anything taught in earlier lectures, or known to a student with this course's prerequisites;
  - a word like "this" or "it" whose meaning the segment itself makes clear, in that sentence or the ones right after it.
- Judge the segment's main point. A passing mention the learner can do without is not a dependency.
- A dependency must be explained in the earlier part shown below, and you must give the sentence where. If the segment refers to something the earlier part does not explain (an example or a question from an earlier lecture, say), the learner already knows it from that lecture: it is not a dependency.

THE EARLIER PART OF THE LECTURE (for your reference only: the learner has not seen it)
{earlier}

THE SEGMENT
{segment}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with these fields, in this order:
"reasoning": a few sentences on what a learner starting here would and would not understand.
"depends": true or false.
"needs": what the segment needs from the earlier part, in a few words; "" if nothing.
"sentence": the number of the first sentence in the segment that shows it; 0 if nothing.
"explained_at": the number of the sentence in the earlier part where that is explained; 0 if nothing.
Answer with the JSON only.

Sources: analysis/segmentation/report.md; notebook pipeline/pipeline.ipynb, run notes “Segmentation on Qwen 3.8 27B (2026-11-12, EC2)”.

2a

Facts: what each segment teaches

Qwen 3.8 27B

A fact is one complete point of subject knowledge that the lecturer teaches in the segment: something a student should know after it and could be asked about in an exam. Facts are the store everything else is built from, so each one must be worth looking up on its own and must cite every sentence that states it. One request per segment.

In
the segment's sentences numbered [1]…[n] (1c), its sub-goal (1b), the course block and lecture title
Out
stage_2a_fact_extraction/fact_maps/lec_<n>.json {"30/S4/F1": {"text", "evidence": ["30/133", …]}}; fact_text/ one line per fact; checks/ per segment: coverage, flags
Command
python scripts/s2a_fact_extraction.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json [--only 1-5] [--sequential]
Model
Qwen 3.8 27B FP8, configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json (thinking up to 16,384 tokens)
Time
386 segments in 5,140 s (1 h 26 min), every answer valid on the first try; EC2 queue job of 2026-11-12, RTX PRO 6000
Result
2,006 facts; 62% of sentences cited by some fact; flags: long 117, bare acronym 84, pointing 52, acronym 1

The prompt states the eight properties of a fact (significant, whole, established, grounded, self-contained, terms in full, on the subject, faithful), asks for subject–relation–predicate sentences, and gives two worked examples from other subjects with the reasons sentences were left out. Code maps the local numbers back to sentence IDs.

prompts/fact_extraction.txt136 lines · 8,000 characters · the full file
You extract the facts taught in one segment of a university lecture transcript.

A fact is one complete point of subject knowledge that the lecturer teaches in
this segment: something a student should know after the segment and could be
asked about in an exam on the course subject. The facts are used to answer
students' questions and to write quiz questions, so each fact must be worth
looking up on its own. Typical facts say what something is, what kinds, parts
or members it has, how it works, what causes it or follows from it, what it is
used for, what limits it, how it differs from or relates to something else, or
give a rule, formula, quantity or result of the subject.

Requirements:
1. Significant. Keep what the segment is there to teach. Leave out passing
   remarks and trivia: who made a word popular, what the lecturer once saw or
   did, how impressive or important something is. If a student who missed
   the point would lose nothing about the subject, it is not a fact.
2. Whole. One fact per point, not per detail. Everything the lecturer says
   about one point goes into one fact:
   - a definition with what the thing is and what it is not;
   - a list with all its members, and their figures if they have any;
   - a quantity with what it measures, its unit, year and source;
   - a source (a paper, a person, a standard) with what it is and why it
     matters to the subject.
   Never write a fact that only says something in passing about a thing
   another fact is about.
   A fact is still one point, not a summary of the segment. Different points
   about the same thing (what it is, how it works, what it costs, where it
   comes from) are different facts, because each could be asked about
   separately. Give a list by naming its members; do not describe each
   member unless the description is the point. A fact that needs more than
   about 50 words is usually several points, unless it carries a list of
   members or figures.
3. Established. The lecturer asserts it as true. Not a hypothesis, a
   question, or something the lecturer only asks about. A view the lecturer
   reports ("some people think", "many assume") is a fact only if the
   lecturer is teaching that view, and then the fact says whose view it is;
   a view raised only to question it is not a fact.
4. Grounded. Cite every segment sentence needed to state the fact, and no
   others. If the fact names something that an earlier sentence introduces,
   cite that sentence too.
5. Self-contained. A reader who has not seen the lecture must understand it.
   Replace pronouns and pointing words (this, it, here, that method, the
   slide) with the specific term. Do not write "the lecturer says".
6. Terms in full. Write every term in full words. When the lecturer uses an
   acronym or abbreviation for the term in this segment, add it in brackets
   after the term's first mention in the fact: "extended reality (XR)".
   Never write the acronym alone, and never add one the lecturer does not use.
7. On the subject, not on the course. Skip greetings, logistics, what the
   course or the lecture will cover, its objectives and how it is organised,
   anecdotes, jokes, rhetorical questions, and promises of later coverage
   ("we will see this next week"). Skip a recap of what was taught earlier
   ("as we saw", "to sum up"). When a recap adds something new (a source, a
   figure, another way of putting a definition), the fact holds what is new
   and only names the thing recapped; it does not restate it. A recap
   sentence may be cited as more evidence for a fact of this segment.
8. Faithful. Add nothing the lecturer did not say, even if it is true. Keep
   hedges ("usually", "in most cases") and who or what a statement is limited
   to ("for some people", "in this design"). When the lecturer says "this
   field" or "these technologies", name what they mean in the lecture, not
   the nearest noun. If the lecturer corrects themselves, keep only the
   corrected version.
9. No repeats. If a point is made twice, output one fact citing all its
   sentences.
10. Few good facts are better than many thin ones. If the segment
   establishes nothing, return an empty list.

Write each fact as one declarative sentence in the order subject, relation,
predicate: what the fact is about, then the verb that relates it ("is",
"covers", "consists of", "causes"), then the rest. A fact that carries a list
or several figures may be a long sentence.

Write the evidence before the text. Output JSON only:
{"facts": [{"evidence": [int, ...], "text": "..."}]}

### Example 1
COURSE TITLE: Virtual Reality
THE COURSE
Use this to understand what the course covers as a whole: its goals and the order of its topics.
Outline: The course explains the foundations of virtual and augmented reality systems: their concepts, hardware and design.
Syllabus (the planned topics in order; a lecture may cover part of a topic or several topics):
- Introduction: Immersive technologies; History
- Hardware: Displays; Tracking
LECTURE: Introduction to immersive technologies
SEGMENT GOAL: Distinguish XR and AR from VR
SEGMENT:
[1] Okay, so now there is this other term you will keep hearing, XR, extended reality.
[2] It is basically an umbrella term.
[3] It covers VR, AR, MR, and honestly whatever comes next in this space.
[4] So it is not one device or one product, remember that.
[5] I remember when I first tried a headset in 2014, I felt sick in five minutes, anyway.
[6] So AR, augmented reality, here you still see the real world and the virtual content is overlaid on top of it.
[7] We will do the tracking part of this in week five.
OUTPUT:
{"facts": [
{"evidence": [1, 2, 3, 4],
"text": "Extended reality (XR) is an umbrella term, not one device or product, that covers virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), mixed reality (MR) and future technologies of the same kind."},
{"evidence": [6],
"text": "Augmented reality (AR) lets the user still see the real world, with virtual content overlaid on top of it."}
]}
Sentence 4 belongs to the definition, so it is not a fact of its own. Sentence 5 is an anecdote and sentence 7 a promise of later coverage.

### Example 2
COURSE TITLE: Data Structures
LECTURE: Hashing
SEGMENT GOAL: Explain what a hash table is and what its operations cost
SEGMENT:
[1] As I said last class, an array gives you constant-time access by index.
[2] Now a hash table, it stores key-value pairs.
[3] It finds the slot for a key by running the key through a hash function.
[4] It is not ordered, mind you, you cannot walk it in key order.
[5] On average, lookup is constant time.
[6] Insertion, constant.
[7] Deletion, constant.
[8] In the worst case, when every key lands in the same slot, all three become linear in the number of keys.
[9] By the way, the word hash comes from chopping and mixing, somebody told me that at a conference once.
[10] Two keys landing in the same slot, that is what we call a collision.
[11] So that is the outline for today, after this we do trees.
OUTPUT:
{"facts": [
{"evidence": [2, 3, 4],
"text": "A hash table stores key-value pairs, finds the slot for a key by applying a hash function to the key, and is not ordered, so its keys cannot be traversed in key order."},
{"evidence": [2, 5, 6, 7, 8],
"text": "Lookup, insertion and deletion in a hash table each take constant time on average and time linear in the number of keys in the worst case, when every key lands in the same slot."},
{"evidence": [2, 10],
"text": "A collision in a hash table is two keys landing in the same slot."}
]}
The three operations and their costs are one list, so they are one fact, not six. What a hash table is, what its operations cost and what a collision is are three points, so they are three facts, not one. Sentence 1 is a recap, sentence 9 is trivia about a word, and sentence 11 is about the lecture.

[30/S4/F5] Head-related transfer functions (HRTF) model how a listener receives sound from a location in space, and this model is relevant for dealing with sound in any system, including extended reality (XR) systems.
[30/S4/F6] Head-related transfer functions (HRTF) depend on the size and shape of the head, ears, and ear canal, and every person has a unique HRTF.
