Changes and why

The first definition, of 2026-11-11, asked for atomic facts, “a single teachable proposition”, and wrote every term in full with no acronyms at all:

The old prompt (first 30 lines) git show 1305a56:pipeline/prompts/fact_extraction.txt 
You extract atomic facts from one segment of a university lecture transcript.
A fact is a single teachable proposition about the course subject that the
lecturer establishes in this segment.

Requirements:
1. Established. The lecturer asserts it as true. Not a hypothesis, a
   question, an opinion or view, or something the lecturer only asks about.
2. Grounded. Cite the smallest set of segment sentence numbers that state
   the fact.
3. Atomic. One proposition per fact: it can be tested on its own and cannot
   be split into smaller facts. Keep the conditions, qualifiers, quantities
   and scope that make it true. Do not split one definition or one list of
   members across several facts.
4. Self-contained. A reader who has not seen the lecture must understand it.
   Replace pronouns and pointing words (this, it, here, that method, the
   slide) with the specific term. Do not write "the lecturer says".
5. Canonical. Write every term in full words, never as an acronym or
   abbreviation, even where the lecturer uses one: "extended reality", not
   "XR" or "extended reality (XR)".
6. On-subject only. The fact is about the segment goal and the course
   subject. Skip greetings, logistics, anecdotes, jokes, rhetorical
   questions, and promises of later coverage ("we will see this next week").
7. Faithful. Add nothing the lecturer did not say, even if it is true. Keep
   hedges ("usually", "in most cases"). If the lecturer corrects themselves,
   keep only the corrected version.
8. No repeats. If a point is made twice, output one fact citing all its
   sentences.
9. If the segment establishes nothing, return an empty list.

Where it fits, write each fact as one declarative sentence in the order
Old definition · lecture 1 (Gemma 4 12B)
[1/S0/F0] Extended reality is a term that is often heard.
[1/S5/F4] The course covers a basic understanding of all relevant concepts, a theoretical background to build a foundation in the field, and practical knowledge on development and deployment.
[1/S5/F5] The course uses case studies involving game engines and software development kits to explain how to develop and deploy technologies.

Talk about the course counted as facts, a definition split into three, 65 facts for lecture 1 on Qwen.

New definition · lecture 1 (Qwen 27B)
[1/S1/F0] Extended Reality (XR) is an umbrella term, not a specific technology, that covers all present or future immersive technologies, such as augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and mixed reality (MR).
[1/S2/F0] The reality-virtuality continuum is a scale whose extremes are a completely real environment with nothing virtual and a completely virtual environment with nothing real.

20 facts for lecture 1. “Whole: one fact per point, not per detail”; “On the subject, not on the course”.

  • Significant and Whole added, Atomic dropped (2026-11-12): atomic facts were thin and the judge could not tell a split definition from two points. Lecture 1 went from 65 facts to 20.
  • Acronyms in brackets instead of never: the learner and the question box need “extended reality (XR)”, and the alias later becomes the concept's acronym.
  • Thinking budget. On lectures 1, 4, 16 a 6,144-token budget gave 64 facts in 196 s, 16,384 gave 62 in 493 s; the larger budget was kept for 2a (its cache would not answer a changed request).
  • Not yet measured: what 2a misses. A read of 15 segments found 5 missed points, 1 major (analysis/fact_judge/missed.json). Concepts that 2a leaves out can never become concepts (plan, weak point 2).
2b

Fact verification, and why it is switched off

built · off

Stage 2b was designed to judge each segment's facts against the eight properties with a second model (Sage-A1.5, a 35B mixture-of-experts, on port 8095), and have the extractor rewrite or remove the ones that fail. It is built and tested. In the course as built it runs with --no-judge: the 2a facts pass through unchanged, keeping their IDs, and each judgement reads “not judged”.

In
2a facts and 2a's cached conversation (the rewrite continues it exactly)
Out
stage_2b_verify_facts/fact_maps/, fact_text/, judgements/ (verdicts, rewrites, the outcome of each fact), checks/
Command
python scripts/s2b_verify_facts.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json --no-judge (as built)
python scripts/s2b_verify_facts.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json --judge configs/sage_a15_vllm.json --only 1,4,16 (with the judge)
Model
judge Sage-A1.5 FP8, configs/sage_a15_vllm.json (with its verification chat-template switch and system prompt); rewriter: 2a's config
Time
pass-through: seconds. With the judge, lectures 1, 4, 16: 12 judge calls in 5 min 49 s, then 5 rewrite calls in 6 min
prompts/fact_judge_system.txt1 lines · 214 characters · the full file
You are a careful reviewer. You check facts extracted from university lecture transcripts against the transcript itself, following the instructions you are given exactly, and you answer with the JSON they ask for.
prompts/fact_judge.txt54 lines · 8,305 characters · the full file
TASK
You check facts extracted from one segment of a university lecture transcript. A fact is one complete point of subject knowledge that the lecturer teaches in the segment: something a student should know after the segment and could be asked about in an exam on the course subject. The facts are used to answer students' questions and to write quiz questions, so each fact must be worth looking up on its own. Check each fact against the eight properties below and decide whether it has all of them.

{course}

THIS LECTURE
This lecture is titled "{lecture_title}".
The goal of the segment: {subgoal}

THE EIGHT PROPERTIES
1. Significant. It is something the segment is there to teach. Not a passing remark or trivia (who made a word popular, what the lecturer once saw or did, how impressive or important something is): a student who missed it would lose nothing about the subject. Fail a fact on this property only if you can say which of these kinds of remark it is. The following are subject knowledge and pass, also when the lecturer says them briefly or they are not the goal of the segment: what a term stands for; another name for a term ("a hash table is also called a hash map"); examples of a thing; how widely a method or device of the subject is used; the source of a concept.
2. Whole. It is one complete point: not one detail of a point, and not several points. Check it in three ways.
Split. It fails if another fact in the list is about the same point: a definition split into what the thing is and what it is not; one list split member by member; one figure split from its year or source; several facts about one paper or person; two short facts that only together answer one question ("a stack is a basic data structure", "a stack is important in every compiler"). All the facts of a split fail: in each reason name the others and say that together they are one point.
Several points. It fails if it holds different points that could be asked about separately (what a thing is, how it works, where it comes from), or if it reads as a summary of the segment, that is, it explains several things that other facts each explain. Say which part is which point. A fact that names the members of a list (the operations of a stack, the layers of a model, the kinds of a thing) is one point and passes, also when other facts each explain one member: naming the members and explaining one member are different points. A list fact fails only if it also explains its members or carries another point besides the list.
Recap. It fails if all it holds is something the lecturer recalls from before this segment and it adds nothing to it. The signs are in the evidence sentences or in the sentence just before them: "we have seen", "as we discussed", "as I said earlier", "remember", "to recollect". A sign alone is not enough: the point itself must be one the lecturer says was already taught. A fact that holds only what a recap adds (a source, a figure, another way of putting a definition) passes, and so does a fact of this segment that also cites a sentence summing the segment up.
3. Established. The lecturer asserts it as true. Not a hypothesis, a question, or something the lecturer only asks about. A view the lecturer reports ("some people think") is stated as that view, and is not one raised only to question it.
4. Grounded. Its evidence sentences state it: every part of the fact is found in them. If the fact is right but part of it is stated in segment sentences that are not cited, it fails: give the numbers of the sentences to add. Do not ask for that part to be removed.
5. Self-contained. A reader who has not seen the lecture understands it. No pronouns or pointing words (this, it, here, that method, the slide) that the fact itself does not make clear. No "the lecturer says".
6. Terms in full. Every term is written in full words. An acronym or abbreviation is written in brackets after the full term at the term's first mention in the fact ("binary search tree (BST)"), and only if the lecturer uses it in the segment. A fact that uses an acronym or abbreviation without giving the full term anywhere fails, even when the lecturer always says it that way ("A BST keeps its keys in order" fails; so does "2D" for "two-dimensional"). After the first mention the acronym may stand alone in the same fact ("a binary search tree (BST) ... a BST"). A missing acronym is not a failure. A name that is simply written in capitals or with digits is not an acronym of a term and passes: a product or model ("Intel 8086"), a company ("IBM"), a country ("UK"), a unit.
7. On the subject, not on the course. Not a greeting, logistics, what the course or the lecture will cover, its objectives or how it is organised, an anecdote, a joke, a rhetorical question, or a promise of later coverage ("we will see this next week"). This holds even when the goal of the segment is itself to outline the course.
8. Faithful. A student who learns the fact must end up believing what the lecturer said, no more and no less. The fact may shorten and reword, and may leave out words that only fill the speech ("so", "of course", "basically", "say"). It fails in each of these cases:
- It adds something the lecturer did not say, even if it is true.
- It is stronger or weaker than the lecturer. The words that set this are "may", "can", "might", "likely", "most", "many", "some", "usually", "often", "sometimes", "broadly", "in a broad sense", "mainly", "more about", "for some people", "today", "in this design". "Lookup is usually constant time" turned into "lookup is constant time" fails; "this can cause a collision" turned into "this causes a collision" fails; "is often called" turned into "is" fails.
- It is about more, fewer or other things than the lecturer's statement. "Chaining is used by most hash tables today" turned into "most hash tables that use linked lists use chaining" fails: the first says how common chaining is, the second does not.
- It names the wrong thing. Where the lecturer says "this field", "the area", "these technologies" or "this", work out from the segment, the lecture title and the course what is meant, and check that the fact names that and not the nearest noun or name.
- It keeps something the lecturer corrected afterwards.
Do not judge this property from memory of the segment: read the evidence sentences again and compare the wording.

A fact passes only if it has all eight properties.

HOW TO CHECK
Go through the facts once, in the order given. For each fact:
a. Read its evidence sentences again, and the sentence before the first of them.
b. Properties 4 and 8: put each part of the fact next to the words of the evidence that state it, and note the words that set how strong and how wide the lecturer's statement is.
c. Property 2: compare the fact with every other fact in the list.
d. Then the other properties.
Decide and go on to the next fact. Go back to a fact only when a later fact turns out to be part of the same split. If you are still in doubt after these steps: on properties 1 and 2 pass the fact (a fact failed on property 1 is removed, and one failed on property 2 is merged or split); on the other properties fail it (the fact is then only corrected). Write the answer once, at the end.

THE SEGMENT
One sentence per line, each with its number in brackets.

{segment}

THE FACTS
Each fact is given with its ID, the numbers of the segment sentences it cites as evidence, and its text.

{facts}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per fact, in the order given, each with these fields in this order:
"fact": the fact's ID.
"reason": one or two sentences. If the fact fails: "Fails property N (name)" for each property it fails, then why, quoting the words of the fact and of the sentence that differ, with the numbers of the sentences and the IDs of the other facts involved, and what a correct fact would hold. The writer of the fact is given only this text, so it must be enough to correct the fact. If the fact passes: the words in its evidence sentences that set how strong and how wide the statement is (for example: "may" in sentence 12; or "none") and how the fact carries them. Do not answer "passes all eight properties".
"pass": true or false.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/fact_rewrite.txt15 lines · 1,575 characters · the full file
A check found that some of your facts do not meet the requirements. A fact's ID ends with its place in your answer, counted from F0 (the first fact is F0). Each failed fact is listed with its evidence, its text and the problem found:

{failed}

Deal with each of these facts, against the requirements above:
- If only its evidence is wrong or incomplete, keep its text and give the right evidence.
- If several of the listed facts are details of one point, rewrite the first of them as one fact that holds the whole point and cites all its sentences, and remove the others.
- If the fact can otherwise be made to meet every requirement, rewrite it: give its evidence (the numbers of the segment sentences that state it) and its text. Change only what the problem names and keep the rest of the fact. A fact that holds different points may be rewritten as several facts, but only if each of them is a whole point that no other fact in your answer above holds; a part that only repeats or adds a detail to another of your facts is left out, not made into a fact of its own.
- If it cannot (it is not significant, not about the subject, or not established), remove it.

Answer with a JSON object with one field, "facts": one entry per fact listed above, in the same order, each with these fields in this order:
"fact": the fact's ID.
"action": "rewrite" or "remove".
"rewrite": when the action is "rewrite", a list of one or more facts, each {"evidence": [int, ...], "text": "..."} as in your answer above; when the action is "remove", an empty list.
Answer with the JSON only.

Why it is off: block 1's measurement

Block 1 measured the judge on the 62 facts of lectures 1, 4 and 16 against a hand-made gold set (55 pass, 7 fail), 34 facts made wrong on purpose, and three runs with different seeds (analysis/fact_judge/report.md):

Measurebaseseed 1seed 2Target
Seeded (blatant) errors caught32/3430/3432/34≥ 90%
False alarms on gold-pass facts, judge and code checks7/556/558/55≤ 10%
Real (gold) failures caught1/71/70/7(none set)
Facts whose verdict flips over three runs9/62 (15%)≤ 5%
The reviewer's finding (Fable, gate review 1)

“2b's net effect on the 62 facts is negative … Of 8 facts acted on, 7 are gold-pass. … 0 of 7 gold failures properly fixed, 6 never touched.” A sentence that tracking is also called sensing was removed on a verdict that flips; the lecturer's “may not be required” stayed “is not required”. The judge checks that words are present, not how strong or wide a statement is: the right reason for a Faithful failure appeared in 2 of 30 verdicts.

On 2026-11-13 the MVP skipped the judge (--no-judge), at the user's decision after that review. Block 1 continues separately: the judge and rewrite prompts were revised with the reviewer's exact text (changes 1–7), the gold gained 13 subtle seeded errors and labels for the 8 rewrites (changes 8, 9). The rerun is pending. The cost of leaving it off is visible: facts like 1/S0/F0 (“The full forms of the abbreviations are …”) stay in the store.

2c

Concepts and relationships per segment

Qwen 3.8 27B

One conversation per segment. Turn 1 lists the concepts the segment's facts name: terms of the subject a textbook glossary would list, each with its name, aliases, the facts that name it and whether the facts define it. Code checks the list. Turn 2 gets the checked names back and lists the relationships the facts state, as triples whose subject, label and object read as one sentence.

In
the segment's facts numbered [1]…[n], its sub-goal, the course block
Out
stage_2c_concepts/concept_maps/lec_<n>.json {"30/S4/C8": {"name", "aliases", "facts", "defined"}}; relations/lec_<n>.json {"30/S4/R7": {"subject", "label", "object", "facts"}}; text/; checks/ (and checks/summary.json)
Command
python scripts/s2c_concepts.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k.json [--only 1,4,16] [--seed 1 --out DIR]
Model
Qwen 3.8 27B FP8, configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k.json (thinking 6,144 tokens: “6k is optimal for speed and quality”, the user's choice of 2026-11-13)
Time
3 h 53 min on an L40S 46 GB with 32 parallel requests (2026-11-13); 779 calls, 2,890,550 input and 4,914,805 output tokens
Result
347 segments with facts; 5,805 segment concepts (1,306 defined), 7,502 relationships; turn 1 retried in 63 segments, turn 2 in 22; nothing unresolved
prompts/concept_extraction.txt103 lines · 6,305 characters · the full file
You list the concepts in the facts of one segment of a university lecture.

The facts below were extracted from one segment of a lecture transcript and
checked against it. A concept is a term of the course subject: a thing the
subject has a name for, such as a technology, device, component, method,
process, quantity, property, principle, or a named system, standard or model.
It is what a student would look up in the glossary or the index of a textbook
on the subject, and what would be a node in a concept map of the course. The
concepts are used to find the facts about a term when a student asks about
it, to link the lectures that teach or use the same term, and to build
concept maps and exercises.

Requirements:
1. From the facts only. Take every concept the facts name. Do not add a
   concept the facts do not name, even if it belongs to the topic.
2. Terms of the subject, not ordinary words. Take a term of the subject even
   when the facts only mention it while teaching something else. Leave out
   ordinary words and descriptions that the facts only use to say something
   ("the real world", "the user", "a problem"). Leave out a source that is
   only named as where a statement comes from (a dictionary, a report, a
   survey). A named product, system, person or organisation is a concept
   only when a fact says something about it that belongs to the subject.
3. One concept per thing. If the facts use several names or wordings for the
   same thing ("headset", "head-mounted display", "HMD"), that is one
   concept. A thing and a kind of it, a thing and a part of it, and a thing
   and a property of it are different concepts ("display" and "head-mounted
   display"; "tracking" and "tracking accuracy").
4. Name. Give the concept the name a textbook glossary would use: the full
   term in words, singular, lower case except for proper names, no article
   ("head-mounted display", not "HMDs" or "the headset"). The name is never
   an acronym or abbreviation and never contains one: "virtual reality
   headset", not "VR headset"; "three-dimensional space", not "3D space".
   When a fact gives the full term with its acronym in brackets ("field of
   view (FoV)", "three-dimensional (3D) space"), the name is the full term.
   When the facts write only the acronym, still name the concept by the
   full term the acronym stands for, and list the acronym as an alias. The
   name of a product or company is kept as it is written ("Microsoft
   HoloLens").
5. Aliases. List every other name the facts use for the concept, written as
   in the facts: its acronym or abbreviation ("XR") and other wordings
   ("headset"). When part of the name is abbreviated in the facts, the
   abbreviated wording is an alias ("3D space" for "three-dimensional
   space"). Acronyms belong here and only here. Do not add an alias the
   facts do not use. Most concepts have one or none.
6. Facts. Give the numbers of all the facts that name the concept: the facts
   that say something about it, and the facts that use it to say something
   about another concept. Every concept has at least one fact, and every
   fact should belong to at least one concept.
7. Defined. true when the facts of this segment teach what the concept is:
   one of them defines it, or says what it consists of or how it works, so
   that a student who read these facts could answer "what is it?". false
   when the facts only mention it: they use it to teach something else, or
   say something about it without saying what it is.

List the concepts in the order the facts first name them. Output JSON only:
{"concepts": [{"name": "...", "aliases": ["..."], "facts": [int, ...], "defined": true}]}

### Example 1
COURSE TITLE: Virtual Reality
LECTURE: Introduction to immersive technologies
SEGMENT GOAL: Distinguish XR and AR from VR
FACTS:
[1] Extended reality (XR) is an umbrella term, not one device or product, that covers virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), mixed reality (MR) and future technologies of the same kind.
[2] Augmented reality (AR) lets the user still see the real world, with virtual content overlaid on top of it.
OUTPUT:
{"concepts": [
{"name": "extended reality", "aliases": ["XR"], "facts": [1], "defined": true},
{"name": "virtual reality", "aliases": ["VR"], "facts": [1], "defined": false},
{"name": "augmented reality", "aliases": ["AR"], "facts": [1, 2], "defined": true},
{"name": "mixed reality", "aliases": ["MR"], "facts": [1], "defined": false}
]}
Virtual reality and mixed reality are only named as members of extended reality, so they are not defined here. "Umbrella term", "the real world" and "virtual content" are ordinary words that the facts use to describe the concepts.

### Example 2
COURSE TITLE: Data Structures
LECTURE: Hashing
SEGMENT GOAL: Explain what a hash table is and what its operations cost
FACTS:
[1] A hash table stores key-value pairs, finds the slot for a key by applying a hash function to the key, and is not ordered, so its keys cannot be traversed in key order.
[2] Lookup and insertion in a hash table each take constant time on average and linear time in the worst case, when every key lands in the same slot.
[3] A collision in a hash table is two keys landing in the same slot.
OUTPUT:
{"concepts": [
{"name": "hash table", "aliases": [], "facts": [1, 2, 3], "defined": true},
{"name": "key-value pair", "aliases": [], "facts": [1], "defined": false},
{"name": "slot", "aliases": [], "facts": [1, 2, 3], "defined": false},
{"name": "hash function", "aliases": [], "facts": [1], "defined": false},
{"name": "lookup", "aliases": [], "facts": [2], "defined": false},
{"name": "insertion", "aliases": [], "facts": [2], "defined": false},
{"name": "constant time", "aliases": [], "facts": [2], "defined": false},
{"name": "linear time", "aliases": [], "facts": [2], "defined": false},
{"name": "collision", "aliases": [], "facts": [3], "defined": true}
]}
The hash function is used to say how a hash table works; the two operations and the two costs are named without being explained; a slot is named in all three facts but never explained. None of them is defined here. "Key order" is a description, not a term.

### Your segment
COURSE TITLE: {course_title}
{course}
LECTURE: {lecture_title}
SEGMENT GOAL: {subgoal}
FACTS:
{facts}
OUTPUT:
prompts/concept_relations.txt43 lines · 4,405 characters · the full file
Now give the relationships between the concepts of your segment that its facts state.

These are the concepts after checking. Use these names exactly:
{concepts}

A relationship is a triple: a subject concept, a label, and an object concept. The label is written so that subject, label and object read as one sentence that the facts state: "extended reality | is an umbrella term that covers | virtual reality". The relationships become the links of the course's concept map and are used to answer how two concepts relate.

Requirements:
1. Stated by the facts. Write a relationship only when one or more facts of the segment state it, and cite those facts. Add nothing from your own knowledge, even if it is true.
2. Reads as a sentence. Subject, label and object, read in that order, make a true sentence. The direction matters: the subject is what the sentence is about.
3. The label says what the relationship is. Write it as a verb phrase in the present tense, as specific as the fact: "is a kind of", "consists of", "is measured by", "causes", "is used for", "requires", "differs from ... in that ...". Never "is related to", "is associated with" or "involves". If the fact limits the relationship ("on average", "in the worst case", "usually", "in this design"), the label carries the limit. The label does not repeat the name of the subject or the object.
4. Exhaustive. Go through the facts one by one. For every two concepts that a fact names, ask whether the fact states how one relates to the other; if it does, write the triple. A fact with a list gives one triple per member. A relationship that takes two facts to state cites both.
5. Directed, and once. Every relationship has a direction, from the subject to the object; the concept map draws it as an arrow that way. Take the direction from the fact: the subject is the concept the fact's statement is about. Do not write the same statement a second time turned round ("A consists of B" and "B is part of A"; "A may use B" and "B may be used in A"). Write triples in both directions between two concepts only when the facts state two different things, one about each. Two concepts may have more than one triple in the same direction when the facts state different relationships between them.
6. Two different concepts from the list above. A fact about one concept alone gives no relationship.

If the facts state no relationship, return an empty list. Write the facts before the triple. Output JSON only:
{"relations": [{"facts": [int, ...], "subject": "...", "label": "...", "object": "..."}]}

For Example 1 above the answer is:
{"relations": [
{"facts": [1], "subject": "extended reality", "label": "is an umbrella term that covers", "object": "virtual reality"},
{"facts": [1], "subject": "extended reality", "label": "is an umbrella term that covers", "object": "augmented reality"},
{"facts": [1], "subject": "extended reality", "label": "is an umbrella term that covers", "object": "mixed reality"}
]}
Fact 2 is about augmented reality alone. No fact says how virtual, augmented and mixed reality relate to each other, so there is no triple between them.

For Example 2 above the answer is:
{"relations": [
{"facts": [1], "subject": "hash table", "label": "stores", "object": "key-value pair"},
{"facts": [1], "subject": "hash table", "label": "finds the slot for a key by applying a", "object": "hash function"},
{"facts": [1], "subject": "hash function", "label": "is applied to a key to find its", "object": "slot"},
{"facts": [2], "subject": "lookup", "label": "is an operation on a", "object": "hash table"},
{"facts": [2], "subject": "insertion", "label": "is an operation on a", "object": "hash table"},
{"facts": [2], "subject": "lookup", "label": "in a hash table takes on average", "object": "constant time"},
{"facts": [2], "subject": "lookup", "label": "in a hash table takes in the worst case", "object": "linear time"},
{"facts": [2], "subject": "insertion", "label": "in a hash table takes on average", "object": "constant time"},
{"facts": [2], "subject": "insertion", "label": "in a hash table takes in the worst case", "object": "linear time"},
{"facts": [3], "subject": "collision", "label": "happens in a", "object": "hash table"},
{"facts": [3], "subject": "collision", "label": "is two keys landing in the same", "object": "slot"}

The costs hold only under a condition, so each label carries it.

Code checks

CheckFlagsOn failureCourse
out_of_rangea fact number outside 1..nretry (both turns)—
name_is_acronym, empty_name, alias_not_writtena name that is an acronym; an empty name; an alias no fact writesretry (turn 1)—
vague_label, self_triple, duplicate_triple“is related to”; a triple to itself; a repeated tripleretry (turn 2)—
reversea triple whose reverse is also therereport437
name_not_in_factsa name none of its own facts writes (often a coined name)report322
no_triplea concept in no triplereport184
no_shared_facta triple none of whose facts names both endsreport113
acronym_in_name“VR headset”: code cannot tell it from “PlayStation VR”report38

Counts from stage_2c_concepts/checks/summary.json. Retries changed little: across all retried answers, 9 concepts were lost and 9 added, 18 triples lost and 144 added.

Changes and why

  • Names are full terms; acronyms are only aliases (D8, 2026-11-13): “virtual reality headset”, not “VR headset”. Without it, an acronym name joins unrelated concepts in stage 3 (v1's “Error rates”).
  • Every answer is checked and retried with the problems shown (D12), and the best attempt is kept when none passes. The appears test was fixed for apostrophes and “X (Y) Z” forms: on the trial's answers false alarms fell from 8 to 0 (alias_not_written) and from 9 to 4 (name_not_in_facts).
  • Labels are free text (the user's choice of 2026-11-12, D6), so the same relation can appear under several wordings: stage 3c finds 827 concept pairs with more than one label.
  • Relationships only within a segment (D4): bounded prompts, at the cost of links between segments.
3a–3c

The concept registry and the synonym merge

codeembedderQwen 3.8 27B

Stage 2c names concepts segment by segment, so “headset” in lecture 1 and “head-mounted display” in lecture 2 are two entries. Stage 3 joins them into the course's concepts, in four steps, and rebuilds the relationships over the joined concepts.

  1. Exact merge (3a, code). Same normalised name: case, hyphens, slashes, punctuation and a plural ending ignored (“lens” = “lenses”, “head set” = “headsets”). A name that is an acronym is never merged by name.
  2. Candidates (3a, embedder). Each entry is embedded as <name>; also called: <other names>. <up to 5 defining facts> with Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B. A pair is a candidate at cosine ≥ 0.70 or when it shares a name or alias. Clusters are the connected candidates, at most 12 entries; larger components are split at their weakest links, and the cut pairs are listed (they can never merge).
  3. Same or different (3b, model). One call per cluster: the entries with their names, lectures and up to 8 facts (defining facts first, marked *). The answer groups them, gives a reason per group and the group's name.
  4. Merge (3c, code). One concept per group, C0001… in course order; losing names become aliases. Relationships are rebuilt from the 2c triples through segment_concepts.json: a triple that now joins a concept to itself is dropped, identical triples are joined. Every decision is a line in decisions.jsonl.
Out
stage_3_registry/concepts.json, segment_concepts.json, relations.json, clusters.json, decisions.jsonl, text/, embed/cache.jsonl, llm/
Command
python scripts/s3a_candidates.py nptel_vr [--embed-model M --threshold 0.70 --cap 12 --embed-facts … --device cuda]
python scripts/s3b_same.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json
python scripts/s3c_registry.py nptel_vr [--no-model] (--no-model: exact merges only, as in the MVP)
Time
3a under a minute on the local GPU (on the CPU, 90 s per batch); 3b 425 calls in 24 min 6 s at 64 parallel requests (2026-11-17); 3c 1 s
Result
5,805 segment concepts → 3,384 entries (2,421 exact merges) → 425 clusters (18,733 candidate pairs, 11,030 cut by the cap) → 3,122 concepts (262 model merges); 7,502 triples → 7,427 relationships (46 joined, 29 self-loops dropped); 827 concept pairs with several labels
prompts/concept_same.txt22 lines · 2,789 characters · the full file
TASK
You decide which entries of a course's concept list are the same concept. The entries below were collected from different segments and lectures of one course, and they have similar names or meanings. Each entry has a name, the other names used for it, the lectures it comes from, and facts from those lectures that name it. Sort the entries into groups: entries that are the same concept go into one group, and an entry that is the same as no other entry is a group of its own.

{course}

SAME OR DIFFERENT
- Two entries are the same concept when they are one thing of the subject under different names or wordings: a full name and its acronym, a spelling or singular and plural variant, a synonym ("headset" and "head-mounted display"), or the same name collected in two lectures. Their facts describe the same thing, and a student who looks up either name should find one glossary entry with the facts of both.
- Decide from the facts, not from the names alone. The same name can mean different things in different lectures (the "resolution" of a display and the "resolution" of a tracker), and different names can mean one thing.
- Two entries are different concepts when one is a kind of the other ("display" and "head-mounted display"), a part of the other ("lens" and "head-mounted display"), a property, measure or use of the other ("tracking" and "tracking accuracy"), the opposite of the other, or a related thing that is often named with it ("augmented reality" and "mixed reality").
- When the facts are too few to tell, keep the entries apart. A wrong merge mixes the facts of two concepts; a missed merge only leaves two entries.

THE ENTRIES
Each entry is given with its number, its name, its other names, its lectures, and its facts. Facts marked * come from a segment that defines the concept. An entry with many facts shows only some of them, and a last line says how many are not shown; judge it by the facts shown.

{entries}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "groups": a list of groups. Every entry number appears in exactly one group. Each group has these fields in this order:
"entries": the numbers of the entries in the group.
"reason": one sentence. For a group of several entries, what in their facts shows that they are the same concept. For a group of one, what makes it different from the entry closest to it.
"name": the name of the group's concept: the name of one of its entries (the name on the entry's first line, not one of its other names), the one that is the full term in words, singular, in the wording the course uses most. Never an acronym or abbreviation: an acronym stays among the other names. If every entry of the group is named by an acronym, take the full term from their other names instead.
Answer with the JSON only.

Changes and why

  • Deferred, then run. The MVP of 2026-11-13 skipped 3b for time (425 clusters, about 1.5 h on the L40S) and built the registry from exact merges: 3,384 concepts, with “headset”, “head-mounted display”, “virtual reality headset” and “VR headset” as separate concepts on 11 lecture maps. The rebuild of 2026-11-17 ran 3b in 24 minutes on the 96 GB GPU: 3,384 → 3,122 concepts.
  • Acronyms (D8). A group is never named by an acronym; an entry named only by an acronym takes its full name from its aliases. A frequent acronym can form a large shared-name cluster that the cap splits (listed as cut pairs).
  • What is left. Some synonyms stayed apart: “head-mounted display” (C0005) and “virtual reality headset” (C0075) were in the same cluster (K0002), and the model kept them as two groups; “interaction” / “user interaction”; “iPhone operating system” / “ios”; “field of view” / “horizontal field of view” share the alias FOV (pipeline/continue/DEMO_PLAN.md). The pair gold that would measure merges (block 3) is not written yet.
4a

Definitions: one per concept

Qwen 3.8 27B

One call per concept writes the definition a student sees when they look it up, from the facts that name it. The facts are shown in lecture order with the lecture, defining facts marked *, capped at 40 (defining facts kept first). The answer: the facts the definition is built from, the definition, defined (false when the lectures only use the concept), and misplaced facts that use the name for another thing (reported, never split: the check on exact-name merges).

prompts/concept_definition.txt36 lines · 4,886 characters · the full file
TASK
You write the glossary definition of one concept of a university course, from the facts that the lectures teach about it. The definition is what a student sees when they look the concept up, and it is the one definition of the concept for the whole course. The student reads it alone, without the facts and without having seen the lectures, and must learn from it what the concept is.

{course}

THE CONCEPT
Name: {name}
Other names: {aliases}
The other names are the other ways the facts write the concept: its acronym, its plural, another wording. A fact that uses one of them is about this concept.

THE FACTS
The facts from the course that name the concept, in lecture order, each with its number in square brackets and the lecture it comes from.
- A fact records what a lecture said, and it is often mainly about something else and only mentions the concept. Only the part of a fact that is about the concept belongs in the definition.
- A fact marked * comes from a segment of a lecture that defines the concept. Every fact of that segment carries the mark, so a marked fact need not say what the concept is; read the marked facts first.
- When the course has more facts about the concept than fit here, the marked facts are kept before the others and a last line says how many facts are not shown. Write the definition from the facts shown.

{facts}

THE DEFINITION
- It says what the concept is, in the form "<name> is <the kind of thing it is> that <what sets it apart from other things of that kind>". Add its parts, its kinds or its purpose only when the facts give them and a student needs them to understand what it is.
- Begin with the name as given above: the same words in the same singular or plural form, unbroken, with a capital first letter, and with "A", "An" or "The" before it when the sentence needs one. When one of the other names is an acronym that stands for the whole name, write that acronym once, in round brackets, straight after the name ("Gross domestic product (GDP) is ..."). Leave the other names out of the definition, and do not expand an acronym that the facts do not expand.
- Short: one sentence when one is enough, never more than three, and never more than 60 words. In plain words, with no term of the subject that the facts do not use.
- It stands alone. No pronoun or pointing word that the definition itself does not make clear ("this method", "the above device"). Nothing about where it comes from: no "in this lecture", "the lecturer", "the course mentions", "according to the facts", and no fact numbers.
- From the facts only. Merge what the facts say; when two facts say the same thing in different words, say it once. Add nothing from your own knowledge, even if it is true. Keep the hedges and limits of the facts ("usually", "in most cases").
- A definition, not a summary and not a copied fact. Figures, dates, examples, history, names of people and products, and comparisons with other concepts stay in the facts, unless one of them is what sets the concept apart.
- If the facts give more than one definition (a dictionary's and the course's own), use the one the later lectures build on, and give it without saying whose it is.
- Most concepts of a course are never defined in it: the lectures only use the name, list it or give it as an example, and then no fact says what the concept is. Do not define such a concept from your own knowledge. Write one sentence, begun with the name in the same way, that says what the facts do show about it: the kind of thing it is, what it is a part of, one of or an example of, or what it is used for. Set "defined" to false. From the fact "The tools of monetary policy include open market operations, the discount rate and reserve requirements", the definition of "discount rate" is "The discount rate is one of the tools of monetary policy", and not what you know a discount rate to be.
- A fact may use the same name for a different thing (the "power" of a statistical test in one lecture, the "power" of an electric motor in another). Define the thing that most of the facts are about, and list the facts that are about the other thing under "misplaced". A fact that is about the concept but is not used in the definition, or that only mentions the concept, is not misplaced: it goes in neither list.

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with these fields in this order:
"facts": the numbers of the facts the definition is built from: only the facts whose content is in the definition, usually one to five of them, and never an empty list.
"definition": the definition.
"defined": true if at least one of the facts says what the concept is, false if the facts only use it, list it or give it as an example.
"misplaced": the numbers of the facts that use the name for a different thing. Usually an empty list. No number is in both "facts" and "misplaced".
Answer with the JSON only.

Defined and connecting concepts, and the glossary rule

Most terms a course names it never explains: “discount rate” in a list of policy tools, “gigabyte” in a spec. The prompt says not to define such a concept from the model's own knowledge, but to write one sentence on what the facts show about it, and set defined to false. These 1,806 connecting concepts stay on the concept maps and keep their cards; the glossary lists only the 1,316 defined ones (viewer rule since commit 194d12a).

Changes and why

MVP (--from-facts): the first defining fact

presence: “Presence is the psychological side that captures whether a user perceives the immersive qualities that are generated.”

head-mounted display: “The formative years of virtual reality are the second phase of its historical evolution, roughly 1950 to 1990 …”

fully immersive system: “Virtual reality (VR) system categories are based on the experience or psychological aspect, and comprise non-immersive, semi-immersive, and fully immersive systems.”

Rebuild: written by the model from the facts

presence: “Presence is a subjective psychological state in which an experience generated by human-made technology is perceived as real, because the user fails to acknowledge the technology's role and perceives computer-generated stimuli as coming from the real world.”

head-mounted display: “A head-mounted display (HMD) is a wearable, mobile visual display that integrates electronic, optical, mechanical, and audio components and uses one or two small screens directly in front of the user's eyes …”

MVP text from git show mvp-2026-11-13:pipeline/output/nptel_vr/stage_4a_definitions/definitions.json.

  • Why it was skipped first. On 2026-11-13 the MVP ran 4a --from-facts (no model) to meet a deadline. The test report found only about 26% of the 1,107 review cards read like a definition; match-the-pairs, recall, crossword clues and “what is X” answers all suffered.
  • The prompt was revised by Fable before the real run (716e6b5): used-but-not-defined concepts get a role sentence and defined: false; no copied facts; the name as given, with its acronym in brackets; at most three sentences and 60 words.
  • Article capitals (3b8deef): 271 definitions began “A virtual …” for a lower-case name; code lowers the first letter of the name after a leading article.
  • The question box uses the new cards' definitions as its first sentence; on the old layer, “a fully immersive system” was answered only as “one of the categories of VR systems” (analysis/qa/STATE.md).
4b

Lecture and course goals

Qwen 3.8 27B

Two levels above the segment sub-goals. One call per lecture groups consecutive segments into objectives and writes the lecture goal, each with what it “achieves” for the learner; it sees each segment's sub-goal and the concepts it defines and uses. Then one call for the course writes four to eight course goals from the lecture goals, each with the lectures that serve it.

Out
stage_4b_goals/lecture_goals/lec_<n>.json, course_goals.json, goals.txt, checks.json
Command
python scripts/s4b_goals.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json [--only 1,4,16] [--seed 1 --out DIR]
Time
63 lecture calls in 3 min 46 s, then the course call alone in 2 min 43 s (one request decodes at about 45 tokens per second); 391 s in all
Result
8 course goals, 63 lecture goals, 125 objectives; in_no_objective 60 (allowed for a closing summary: 61 lectures end with one)
prompts/lecture_goals.txt32 lines · 2,500 characters · the full file
TASK
You write the goals of one lecture of a university course, from the sub-goals of its segments. The lecture was divided into segments, and each segment achieves one sub-goal: something the learner can do after it. Build the two levels above the sub-goals:
1. Objectives. Group the segments into the larger steps the lecture takes. An objective is what the learner can do after a run of consecutive segments that work toward the same thing.
2. The lecture goal. What the learner can do after the whole lecture.
The goals are used to tell a learner what a lecture is for, to answer what a lecture or a part of it teaches, and to build the goals of the whole course from the goals of its lectures.

{course}

THIS LECTURE
This lecture is titled "{lecture_title}".

THE SEGMENTS
Each segment is given with its number, its sub-goal, the concepts it defines and the concepts it only uses.

{segments}

OBJECTIVES
- An objective covers one or more consecutive segments. Every segment belongs to exactly one objective, except a segment that teaches nothing of the subject: one that only recaps earlier lectures, outlines the course or the lecture, or summarises or closes the lecture. Such a segment belongs to none.
- Group segments when their sub-goals are steps toward one thing (defining something and then applying the definition; the parts of one system, one after the other). Keep segments apart when each could be a lecture topic of its own. A lecture usually has one to four objectives; a short lecture may have one.
- An objective is not a list of its sub-goals joined with "and". It names the one thing they add up to.

EVERY GOAL
- "goal": one sentence that says what the learner can do, starting with a verb ("Explain why ...", "Classify ...", "Describe how ..."). It names the subject matter itself, not "the lecture" or "the topics".
- "achieves": one sentence that says what reaching the goal gives the learner in this course: what it lets them understand or do next, or which aim of the course it serves. Take this from the course description above and the sub-goals; do not promise what neither of them supports.
- Claim only what the sub-goals cover. Do not add an ability that no segment teaches.

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with these fields in this order:
"objectives": a list in lecture order, each with "segments" (the numbers of its segments), "goal" and "achieves".
"goal": the lecture goal.
"achieves": what the lecture goal gives the learner.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/course_goals.txt22 lines · 2,020 characters · the full file
TASK
You write the goals of a university course from the goals of its lectures. A course goal is one of the few large things a learner can do after the whole course. Each course goal is reached through several lectures. The course goals are the top of a hierarchy (course goal, lecture goal, objective, segment sub-goal) that is used to tell a learner what the course is for, which lectures each aim needs, and where a lecture fits.

{course}

THE LECTURES
Each lecture is given with its key in square brackets, its title, its goal, what the goal achieves, and its objectives. The key is the lecture's identifier in the course: usually its number ("4"), sometimes with a letter or a word ("24b", "tut_1" for a tutorial).

{lectures}

COURSE GOALS
- Write as many course goals as the course has distinct aims, usually four to eight. Two goals that most lectures would serve together are one goal; a goal that needs half the course is probably two.
- "goal": one sentence that says what the learner can do, starting with a verb. It names the subject matter itself, not "the course" or "the topics".
- "achieves": one sentence that says what reaching the goal gives the learner: what it lets them understand, build or decide.
- "lectures": the keys of the lectures whose goals work toward this course goal, written exactly as in the square brackets, in course order. Every lecture serves at least one course goal. A lecture may serve more than one when its objectives belong to different aims. The lectures of a goal need not be consecutive.
- Build the goals from the lecture goals and objectives given. Use the course description above to see how the course is meant to be organised, but do not write a goal that no lecture serves.

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with two fields, in this order:
"reasoning": in a few sentences, the aims you found in the lectures and why you divided them this way.
"goals": a list of course goals, each with "lectures", "goal" and "achieves", in this order.
Answer with the JSON only.

4c

The lecture graph

code

Lecture K is needed for lecture L when L uses a concept that K defines and K comes first. The link carries those concepts. A concept used everywhere should not make every lecture depend on lecture 1, so it weighs less:

Hub weighting

With N lectures and n lectures using a concept, free = max(3, 0.25·N). The concept weighs 1 when n ≤ free, else 1 − 0.9·(n − free)/(N − free), down to 0.1. A link is a prerequisite when its concepts weigh at least 1; lighter links are kept as weak. Direct prerequisites are those not implied through another lecture (transitive reduction); the rest are indirect. Defaults proposed, D10.

Out
stage_4c_lecture_graph/graph.json (links, prerequisites, concept kinds, concept paths), needed/lec_<n>.json (what is needed before a lecture), graph.txt, checks.json
Command
python scripts/s4c_lecture_graph.py nptel_vr [--hub-share 0.25 --hub-min-lectures 3 --hub-floor 0.1 --min-weight 1]
Time
under a second
Result
83 direct, 391 indirect and 245 weak links; 10 hub concepts; lectures 1, 4 and 23 need none; 2,074 concepts used but never defined (assumed); the graph's own consistency checks find no problem

A lecture's concepts fall into four kinds: introduced (first defined here), met_again (defined earlier), defined_later, assumed (never defined). Lecture 30 has 23, 7, 0 and 15. Recall activities and “what do I need before lecture N” answers read this. A weakness found by the QA work: a link counts any single mention, so lists of prerequisites held odd items; the QA tool now ranks earlier concepts by how often the lecture uses them and lists single mentions as “in passing” (QA).

4d

The knowledge layer: assemble and check

codeembedder

Stage 4d writes output/nptel_vr/knowledge/, the folder the question answering, the activities and the viewer read, and checks it: every file against schemas/knowledge.schema.json (a standard-library checker, scripts/schema_check.py), every reference against the IDs the layer defines, no ID defined twice, every fact with a vector, every concept in the name index and the graph. It exits 1 when anything is found.

5

Activities: ten kinds, code checks, a judge, one rewrite

Qwen 3.8 27Bcode

Stage 5 reads only the knowledge layer and writes what a learner does around each lecture. Every activity lists the facts and concepts it is built from, so every item can be checked against them and every answer linked to a moment of the video. A model writes only the kinds that need language; the others are code.

In
knowledge/ (concepts, relations, facts, lectures, lecture graph), course.json
Out
activities/lec_<n>.json, course.json (review cards), checks.json, activities.txt (readable, with every verdict), llm/; the contract is schemas/activities.md
Command
python scripts/s5_activities.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json --judge [--judge-rewrite] [--judge-llm CFG] [--only 1-5] [--kinds quiz,recap] [--out DIR]
python scripts/s5_activities.py nptel_vr --check (the contract check alone)
Time
writers: 945 calls in 1 h 9 min; judge: 915 calls in 1 h 1 min (job total 7,829 s); one rewrite round for quizzes and open questions: 246 rewrites + 246 re-judgements in 34 min; the recap re-judge after its criteria changed: 347 calls in 22 min. All at 128 parallel requests on the RTX PRO 6000, 2026-11-17.
Result
1,168 activities: quiz 347, recap 347, concept map 63, complete the map 63, match the pairs 63, open question 63, crossword 62, recall 62, odd one out 57, put in order 41; 1,316 review cards. 197 units retried by the code checks, none unresolved, no item dropped; the contract check “all good”.

The ten kinds and spaced review

KindWhereMade byHowCount
recallbefore the lecturecodethe concepts the lecture graph says it needs (direct prerequisites first), at most 6; “What is X?” with its definition and the lecture that defines it62
quizafter each segmentmodel3–5 questions from the segment's facts; wrong options from related concepts and other facts of the lecture; code shuffles the options347
recapafter each segmentmodel2–5 key points with their facts, and a 2–4 sentence summary of how they connect347
put in ordersegment or lecturemodelonly where the facts state an order; zero to two per lecture41
concept mapafter the lecturecodethe lecture's relationships as edges, each with its facts63
complete the mapafter the lecturecodethe map with up to 4 blanks (a label, or a concept on no other edge), 3–4 options each; one blank per concept pair63
match the pairsafter the lecturecodeup to 8 concepts and the first sentence of their definitions, names masked63
crosswordafter the lecturecode + modelcode lays out up to 12 concept names (greedy, best crossing); the model writes the clues from the definitions62
odd one outafter the lecturemodelthree members of a group the facts state and one near miss; zero to three puzzles57
open questionafter the lecturemodel2–4 questions needing two or more facts; reference answer and its key facts63
spaced reviewcourse-widecodea card per defined concept: name (and acronym) on the front, definition on the back; scheduled per learner in the browser1,316 cards

The writer prompts

Each prompt says what the learner will see (and what they will not: the facts), gives the rules with examples from another subject, and ends with a self-test the model runs before answering. The quiz prompt, for instance, asks it to “cover the options”, “compare lengths” and “argue for each wrong option as a learner would”.

prompts/activity_quiz.txt46 lines · 5,682 characters · the full file
TASK
You write a short multiple-choice quiz on one segment of a lecture of a university course. The learner takes it right after the segment, to check what they understood. Every question is answered by the segment's facts below; the learner does not see the facts. A good question can be answered only by someone who understood the segment: not by picking the longest or the most careful-sounding option, and not by striking out options that are plainly wrong.

{course}

THE SEGMENT
Lecture: {lecture_title}
What the segment teaches: {subgoal}

THE FACTS
What the segment teaches, one fact per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

MATERIAL FOR WRONG OPTIONS
Related concepts of the course (with a short definition) and other facts of the same lecture. Use them only to make wrong options that a learner who misunderstood the segment might pick; do not ask about them.

{related}

THE QUESTIONS
- Write {count} questions, each about a different point of the facts. When the facts are few, stay at the lower number: a question that asks again what another question asks is worse than one question fewer.
- Ask about what matters for what the segment teaches: what a thing is, why, how it works, how two things differ, what follows from it. No trivia (a name, a date, a figure, the place of an item in a list) and no question about the lecture itself.
- "facts": the numbers of the facts that answer the question. The correct option follows from those facts alone; add nothing from your own knowledge.
- "question": one full question that ends with a question mark and stands alone: no "this", "the above", "in this segment", "according to the lecture", "is described as". It does not hand the answer over: no key word that then appears in the correct option and in none of the wrong ones.
- "correct": the right answer, as short as it can be and still be right: a few words or one short clause. Keep a hedge of the fact ("can", "usually", "in most systems"). Leave out examples and side details; a detail the answer needs goes into the question.
- "wrong": three wrong options. Write the correct option first, then build each wrong option to match it.
  - Wrong for this question. A learner who knows the facts can say why each one is wrong. A statement that is true of something else is a good wrong option only when it is false as an answer to this question: read each wrong option against all the facts and the material above, and replace it if any of them makes it a possible answer.
  - Tempting. What a learner who half understood might believe: a neighbouring concept of the same kind, the property of a sibling concept, a likely mix-up. Not something from another topic that anyone could strike out.
  - Same form as the correct option: the same kind of thing, the same grammatical shape, the same number of parts (when the correct option says "X, so Y", so does each wrong one), the same level of detail and about the same length. At least one wrong option is as long as the correct one or longer.
  - No tell-tale wording. No option made false by adding "only", "always", "never", "all", "none" or "must". Never "all of the above", "none of the above" or "both". At most one wrong option is the correct one with a detail reversed, and the four options are never the four combinations of two halves ("A and B", "A but not B", "B but not A", "neither"): the learner then picks the option that shares most with the others.
- The four options end the same way, without a full stop.
- "explanation": one or two sentences saying why the correct option is right, as a statement about the subject (not "the segment says" or "the facts state"). It may add why the most tempting wrong option is wrong.
- Across the quiz: no question gives away the answer of another, and the same few statements are not passed around as the correct option of one question and the wrong options of the others, or the learner answers by elimination.

Before you answer, test each question three ways. Cover the options: can a learner who knows the facts answer it without seeing them? Compare lengths: if the correct option is the longest, shorten it or lengthen a wrong one. Argue for each wrong option as a learner would: if one can be defended, replace it.

EXAMPLES (other subjects)
Facts: [1] Mitochondria produce most of a cell's ATP through cellular respiration.
{"questions": [{"facts": [1], "question": "Which organelle produces most of a cell's ATP?", "correct": "The mitochondrion", "wrong": ["The ribosome", "The Golgi apparatus", "The cell nucleus"], "explanation": "Mitochondria make most of a cell's ATP through cellular respiration."}]}

Facts: [1] A hash table finds a key in constant time on average because a hash function maps the key directly to its slot. [2] A balanced search tree finds a key in logarithmic time by comparing keys along a path from the root.
{"questions": [{"facts": [1, 2], "question": "Why is finding a key in a hash table usually faster than in a balanced search tree?", "correct": "A hash function maps the key straight to its slot", "wrong": ["The table keeps its keys sorted, so half of them are skipped at each step", "Each comparison on the way down from the root rules out half of the keys", "The slots are read one after another until the key turns up"], "explanation": "A hash function computes the slot from the key itself, so no keys are compared on the way; a search tree has to compare keys along a path from the root."}]}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "questions": a list of {count} questions, each with the fields "facts", "question", "correct", "wrong", "explanation" in this order.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_recap.txt33 lines · 3,259 characters · the full file
TASK
You write the recap of one segment of a lecture of a university course: what the learner should take away from it, shown right after the segment and read again when revising. The recap has two parts with different jobs. The key points are what to remember, one idea each. The summary says in a few sentences what the segment explains as a whole and how those ideas hang together.

{course}

THE SEGMENT
Lecture: {lecture_title}
What the segment teaches: {subgoal}
The larger step of the lecture it belongs to: {objective}

THE FACTS
What the segment teaches, one fact per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

THE RECAP
- "points": {count} key points, in the order the segment teaches them. Each point is one sentence of at most about twenty words that a learner can remember, with "facts": the numbers of the facts it comes from.
  - One idea per point, and no idea in two points. When there are more facts than points, join the facts that belong to one idea into one point and cite them all, rather than leaving out a fact the segment needs. Leave out examples, figures and asides unless the segment is about them.
  - Each point can be read on its own, also weeks later: it names its subject in full ("the heap sort algorithm", not "the algorithm" or "it").
  - Keep the meaning exact. Shorten, but do not swap a term for another one, and keep whatever limits a statement: a hedge ("usually", "can", "in most systems"), a condition ("if the list is already sorted"), both sides of a comparison.
- "text": a summary of two to four sentences. It is not the points again one after the other: it says what the segment is about and how its ideas connect (what leads to what, what is set against what, what it is all for), so that it reads as one explanation. Connect the ideas only the way the facts and the lines under THE SEGMENT connect them; do not invent a cause or a purpose.
- From the facts only: add nothing from your own knowledge.
- Plain words for a learner. No "the lecturer", "in this segment", "the facts" or "as we saw". A convention that the course itself sets may be given as one ("in this course, the two terms are used for the same thing").

EXAMPLE (another subject)
Facts: [1] Supply is the amount of a good that sellers offer at each price. [2] Demand is the amount of a good that buyers want at each price. [3] When the price rises, sellers usually offer more of a good. [4] When the price rises, buyers usually want less of a good.
{"points": [{"facts": [1], "point": "Supply is the amount of a good that sellers offer at each price."}, {"facts": [2], "point": "Demand is the amount of a good that buyers want at each price."}, {"facts": [3, 4], "point": "When the price of a good rises, sellers usually offer more of it and buyers usually want less."}], "text": "Supply and demand describe the two sides of the market for a good: what sellers offer and what buyers want at each price. A change in price usually moves the two in opposite directions, so a higher price brings more of the good to the market while fewer buyers want it."}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with these fields in this order:
"points": the key points, each with the fields "facts" and "point".
"text": the summary.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_open_question.txt40 lines · 3,643 characters · the full file
TASK
You write open questions on one lecture of a university course: questions the learner answers in a few sentences of their own, after the lecture. A good open question asks the learner to explain, compare or reason: why one thing follows from another, how two concepts relate or differ, what a concept means for a case the lecture treats. Each has a reference answer built from the facts below; the learner's answer is compared with it and the learner then reads it as feedback.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}
Its goal: {goal}

THE FACTS
What the lecture teaches, then facts from earlier lectures that it builds on, one per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

RELATIONSHIPS
How concepts of the lecture relate, each with the numbers of the facts that state it.

{relations}

CONCEPTS
The concepts you may name in "concepts":
{concepts}

THE QUESTIONS
- Write {count} questions, each about a different point. Stay at the lower number unless the lecture has material for more: a forced question is worse than one question fewer.
- What to ask. A question whose answer needs two or more facts put together: a reason, a comparison, a consequence, how a part serves the whole. The relationships above are links the lecture states: use them. A question that only asks to repeat one fact, a list or a figure is not an open question.
- The link must be in the facts. Ask "why" or "how" only when the facts give the reason or the way it works. If they only say that something is so, do not ask why it is so: the reference answer could then only repeat the facts without answering.
- Earlier lectures. Use a fact from an earlier lecture when this lecture really builds on it: it applies, extends or contrasts with that concept. Do not force the link: a question that ties this lecture to an early definition only because both name the same term ("How does X reflect the definition of Y?") teaches nothing.
- "key_facts": the numbers of the facts the reference answer is built from: at least two, and only facts the answer uses.
- "question": one question in one or two sentences, that stands alone: no "this", "the above" or "in this lecture", and things are named in full. It follows one thread: not two questions joined by "and" ("How ..., and why ...?"). It does not contain its own answer, and it does not presuppose something odd ("Why can X be understood as ...?").
- "reference_answer": two to four sentences, at most about eighty words. The first sentence answers the question directly; the others give the support from the key facts. Every part of the question is answered. From the key facts only: add nothing from your own knowledge, keep the hedges of the facts ("usually", "can"), and no "the lecture says".
- "concepts": the concepts the question is mainly about (usually two to four), by their names exactly as listed above.

EXAMPLE (another subject)
Facts: [1] An index lets a database find rows without reading the whole table. [2] Every index must be updated when a row is inserted.
{"questions": [{"key_facts": [1, 2], "question": "Why can adding an index make some database operations faster and others slower?", "reference_answer": "An index speeds up lookups and slows down inserts. Lookups get faster because the database can find rows through the index without reading the whole table. Inserts get slower because every index must be updated when a row is inserted.", "concepts": ["index"]}]}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "questions": a list of {count} questions, each with the fields "key_facts", "question", "reference_answer", "concepts" in this order.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_odd_one_out.txt40 lines · 3,612 characters · the full file
TASK
You write "odd one out" puzzles on one lecture of a university course. Each puzzle shows four items: three belong to one group that the lecture teaches (kinds of one thing, parts of one system, steps of one process, properties of one thing), and one does not. The learner sees only the four items, with no hint of what the group is, picks the odd one and then sees the reason. So the three members must bring their group to mind by themselves, and the odd item must be a near miss that only knowledge of the lecture rules out.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}

CONCEPTS
The concepts of the lecture, with a short definition.
{concepts}

RELATIONSHIPS
{relations}

THE FACTS
One fact per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

THE PUZZLES
- Write up to three puzzles, each from a different group, and only from groups the facts state. If the facts state no such group, answer with an empty list: a forced puzzle is worse than none.
- "group": three members of one group, in a few words each. A fact names the group as a group of the subject: the kinds of X, the parts of X, the steps of X, the properties of X ("X has three ...", "the kinds of X are ...", "X consists of ..."). Not things that only happen to be mentioned together: the features of one product, the examples in one illustration, the places or people in one story. Being absent from such a list does not put an item outside a group.
- "odd": an item of the same sort and level as the three (a kind among kinds, a part among parts, a device among devices), for which the facts say what it is instead: a kind of something else, a part of another system, a step of another process. A learner who half remembers the lecture could take it for a member. The odd item is wrong by the facts, not by your own knowledge. It is never:
  - the name of the group itself, or the whole that the three are parts of ("engine" beside three parts of an engine);
  - a thing of another sort (a unit of measurement beside three measuring instruments);
  - an item the facts simply do not mention with the group;
  - an item from so far away that anyone could spot it without the lecture.
- One answer only. Check that no other item can be singled out by another reasonable grouping (three are hardware and one is software, but not the item you chose), and that no fact lets a learner argue the odd item into the group. If another choice can be defended, drop the puzzle. Do not use the same four items in two puzzles with a different odd item.
- Written alike: the four items are the same kind of phrase, about the same length, each beginning with a capital letter. The odd item must not stand out by its form: when the three members share a word that the odd item lacks ("... acid" three times and one "enzyme"), the wording gives the answer away, so choose an odd item that carries the word too, or another group.
- "reason": one sentence that names the group and says what the odd item is instead, as a statement about the subject: not "the facts state", "is listed as", "is described as" or "in the lecture".
- "facts": the numbers of the facts that state the group and place the odd item.

EXAMPLE (another subject)
Facts: [1] Mammals include whales, bats and humans. [2] Sharks are fish, not mammals.
{"items": [{"facts": [1, 2], "group": ["Whale", "Bat", "Human"], "odd": "Shark", "reason": "Whales, bats and humans are mammals; a shark is a fish."}]}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "items": a list of zero to three puzzles, each with the fields "facts", "group", "odd", "reason" in this order.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_put_in_order.txt37 lines · 3,818 characters · the full file
TASK
You write "put in order" exercises on one lecture of a university course. The learner sees a title and, under it, the steps of a process or the stages of a sequence shuffled, and puts them back in order. An exercise is worth doing only when the order itself is something the lecture teaches.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}

THE FACTS
The lecture's facts by segment, one per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

THE EXERCISES
- Most lectures have none or one. Write an exercise only where the facts state an order: the steps of a procedure, the stages of a pipeline from input to output, the path something travels, events in time, phases that follow one another. The facts must say what comes after what ("first", "then", "after", "before", "in the order", numbered stages, dates). The order is never your own knowledge, and never only the order in which a fact happens to list things.
- Test every pair of neighbouring steps: could the two be swapped without contradicting a fact? Then the facts do not fix their order: leave one of the two out, or drop the exercise.
- These are not sequences, so write no exercise from them: the kinds, parts, factors or properties of a thing; a problem with its cause, its solution and what the solution needs or brings; the things done in a project when the facts do not say which came after which; choices that can be made in any order.
- No exercise that a learner could solve without the lecture, from the wording alone ("the task is started", "the task is executed", "the task is completed"; "input is given", "it is processed", "output is produced"). Prefer four or more steps; write three only when their order is a point the lecture makes.
- A scale, a range or a stack of layers has two ends: use it only when the title says at which end to start ("from the lowest layer up", "from the coldest to the hottest").
- If the facts give no such order, answer with an empty list: no exercise is better than a forced one.
- "title": what is put in order, as a short instruction that stands alone and names the process in full ("Order the stages of the water cycle"). It does not reveal the order and gives no count ("the first four stages").
- "steps": three to eight steps in the correct order.
  - Each step says in a few words what happens in it or what is passed through. Where the facts say what a stage does, describe the stage rather than only naming it: ordering descriptions takes more understanding than ordering names.
  - All steps have the same grammatical form and about the same length, and each is one distinct moment: one event is not split into two steps, and two events are not put into one.
  - Nothing in the wording sorts the steps by itself: no "first", "then", "next", "finally", "initial", "final", no step numbers, dates or years, and no step that speaks of "the previous step".
- "facts": the numbers of the facts that give the steps and their order.

EXAMPLES (another subject)
Facts: [1] In mitosis, the chromosomes first condense, then line up at the middle of the cell, then are pulled apart to the two poles, and finally two new nuclei form.
{"items": [{"facts": [1], "title": "Order the stages of mitosis", "steps": ["Chromosomes condense", "Chromosomes line up at the middle of the cell", "Chromosomes are pulled to the two poles", "Two new nuclei form"]}]}

Facts that state no order (the first lists parts, the second gives a reason): [1] A cell membrane consists of lipids, proteins and carbohydrates. [2] Because the membrane lets only some molecules through, a cell can keep its inside different from its surroundings.
{"items": []}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "items": a list of zero to two exercises, each with the fields "facts", "title", "steps" in this order.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_crossword.txt31 lines · 3,047 characters · the full file
TASK
You write the clues of a crossword on one lecture of a university course. Each answer is a concept of the lecture. The learner sees the grid, the clues and the number of letters of each answer, and nothing else; solving the crossword is a way to recall the concepts. So each clue must lead a learner who knows the lecture to its answer, and to no other answer.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}

THE ANSWERS
Each answer with its number in brackets, its other names and its definition (or the lecture's main statement about it).

{answers}

THE CLUES
- One clue per answer, by its number.
- What a clue says. What the concept is or does: the property that makes it this concept and not another one. Take it from the text given for the answer and add nothing from your own knowledge. When that text is mostly about another thing, use only what it says about the answer itself, and check that the clue describes the answer and not that other thing.
- Not where the answer stands in a list, and not a figure: "the second of the four steps", "one of the three states of matter" and "about 4% of the market" do not say what the thing is. When the text only names the answer in a list, say what the list is a list of and what sets this member apart, putting the plain meaning of its name into other words ("the layer of the Earth that lies deepest" for "inner core").
- Its own answer only. Read each clue against every other answer in the list: no two clues could be swapped, so sibling concepts each get the detail that tells them apart. A clue also fails when it fits a more familiar thing better than its answer.
- It stands alone. The learner reads the clue without the text: name the setting it needs ("in a compiler", "in the nitrogen cycle"), and no "their", "these", "the above", "the software" or "the sequence" that points at something the clue does not name.
- Form: a phrase that could stand in the place of the answer (for a noun, a noun phrase: "Study of how physical stimuli are perceived", not "When stimuli are studied"), of at most fifteen words, without a full stop at the end.
- Never the answer's words: not the answer, not any of its other names, not any word of them in any form (not "table" or "tables" for "hash table"), and not a longer word built on one of them ("graphical" for "graph"). Say it in other words.
- No "this lecture" or "the lecturer".

EXAMPLE (another subject)
[1] photosynthesis (other names: none). Definition: Photosynthesis is the process by which plants turn light, water and carbon dioxide into sugar and oxygen.
[2] chloroplast (other names: none). Definition: Photosynthesis takes place in the chloroplasts of plant cells.
{"clues": [{"number": 1, "clue": "How plants make sugar from light, water and carbon dioxide"}, {"number": 2, "clue": "Part of a plant cell where light is turned into sugar"}]}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "clues": a list with one entry per answer, in the order of the numbers, each with the fields "number" and "clue".
Answer with the JSON only.
