Code checks: hard and soft

Every model answer is checked in code. A failure goes back to the model with the problem written out, as the next turn of the same conversation. The checks come in two classes (Fable's review, analysis/activities/review_prompts.md section 7):

  • Hard: an item that still has the problem after the last attempt is dropped. A cited fact number outside the list; a count out of range; quiz options not one right and three wrong, two the same, “all of the above”, a question not ending in “?”; an odd-one-out group of the wrong size or reused; put-in-order steps not 3–8; a clue containing its answer, over 15 words or the same as another clue.
  • Soft: retried, but an item that still fails is kept and flagged by name, and the judge decides. source_talk (“the segment”, “the facts state”, “according to the lecture”) for every kind; correct_longest (the right option over 1.2 times and 12 characters longer than every wrong one) and correct_longest_share, absolute_wrong for quizzes; text_repeats_points, point_long, text_length for recaps; order_cue; shared_word, length_tell; two_questions, reference_long; clue_list_place.
  • Fixes in code: options and clues lose a trailing full stop; odd-one-out items get a capital; final masking hides only the answer's own name and its plain forms.

A real retry, lecture 30's first quiz (checks.json):

Question 3: the correct option (86 characters) is clearly longer than every wrong option (the longest has 53). Cut it down to what the answer needs, or rewrite the wrong options to its length and shape: at least one wrong option must be as long as the correct one.

The judge, the rewrite and the hiding rules

With --judge, one call per unit (a segment's quiz or recap, a lecture's puzzles, exercises, questions or clues) checks every item against named criteria, after the code checks. The judge sees what the writer saw, with the same fact numbers, and the items as the learner will see them (quiz options in their shuffled order). It must do a piece of work before deciding (the check field: for a quiz, the letters of every option the facts make correct, found before reading the key) and name the criteria an item fails. In doubt it fails an item on the criteria where a learner would be taught something wrong, and passes it on criteria of form.

prompts/activity_judge_quiz.txt47 lines · 5,325 characters · the full file
TASK
You check the questions of a multiple-choice quiz before learners see it. The quiz is on one segment of a lecture of a university course and was written from the segment's facts below. The learner takes it right after the segment and sees, for each question, the question and the four options, and after answering the explanation; the learner never sees the facts. Decide for each question whether it meets all six criteria below. A question that fails is rewritten once and then dropped, and a wrong or arguable key shown to a learner does more harm than a missing question.

{course}

THE SEGMENT
Lecture: {lecture_title}
What the segment teaches: {subgoal}

THE FACTS
What the segment teaches, one fact per line, each with its number in brackets. They are the only ground for the correct option.

{facts}

OTHER MATERIAL OF THE LECTURE
Related concepts of the course (with a short definition) and other facts of the same lecture. The writer took wrong options from here. Use it only to check whether a wrong option is in truth also right.

{related}

THE SIX CRITERIA
1. answerable. The option marked correct is right, and it follows from the facts the question cites, without any other knowledge. It says no more than those facts and is no stronger than they are: a hedge of the fact ("can", "usually", "may") is kept. It fails when the key is wrong, when the cited facts do not state it, or when it can only be known from outside the facts.
2. one_answer. None of the three other options is also a defensible answer to the question as it is worded. Take each wrong option in turn and argue for it as a learner would, with all the facts and the other material in hand: it fails when one of them is true by a fact and the question is wide enough to admit it (asked for "a data structure that keeps its keys in order", an option naming another such structure is also right), or when the question has no single answer.
3. no_giveaway. A learner who did not understand the segment cannot find the key from the form of the question. It fails when you can name the tell: the correct option is clearly the longest or the only detailed one; only it has two parts, a hedge or a careful qualification; the wrong options are made false with "only", "always", "never", "all", "none"; the options are the combinations of two halves, so the key is the one that shares most with the others; a key word of the question appears in the correct option and in no other; two or more wrong options are off the topic or absurd; another question of this quiz states the answer.
4. worth_asking. The question is about a point that matters for what the segment teaches: what a thing is, why, how it works, how things differ. It fails for trivia (a name, a date, a figure, the place of an item in a list), for a question about the lecture itself, and for a question that asks again what an earlier question of this quiz asks (name that question; only the later one fails).
5. clear. A learner understands the question on first reading: one full, grammatical question that stands alone (no "this", "the above"), four options of the same kind and shape, and an explanation that is true, agrees with the key and gives the reason from the facts.
6. no_source_talk. Neither the question, the options nor the explanation speaks of the lecturer, the lecture, the segment, the video or the facts ("according to the lecture", "the segment states", "is described as"). A convention the course itself sets, stated as such, is not source talk.

HOW TO CHECK
Take the questions one at a time, in the order given.
a. Read the question and its four options, but not yet the line "Marked correct". From the facts alone, decide which options are correct answers. Write their letters down: this is "check".
b. Now read the marked letter. If it is not among yours, criterion 1 fails; if you found more than one, criterion 2 fails. Read the cited facts again before you settle either.
c. Compare the form of the four options (criterion 3), then go through criteria 4 to 6.
Fail a criterion only for a problem you can point to and quote. When you are still in doubt after reading the facts again: on criteria 1 and 2 fail the question; on criteria 3 to 6 pass it.

THE QUESTIONS
Each question with its number, the numbers of the facts it cites, the options lettered in the order the learner sees them, the letter marked correct, and the explanation.

{questions}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per question, in the order given, each with these fields in this order:
"item": the number of the question.
"check": the letters of all the options that the facts make a correct answer, found before reading the marked letter ("C"; "A, C"; "none").
"reason": one or two sentences. If the question fails: what is wrong, quoting the words concerned and giving the fact numbers, and what a repaired question would do. The writer is given only this text, so it must be enough to repair the question. If it passes: the words of the cited facts that state the correct option.
"failed": the names of the criteria it fails, from "answerable", "one_answer", "no_giveaway", "worth_asking", "clear", "no_source_talk"; an empty list if it fails none.
"pass": true if "failed" is empty, otherwise false.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_judge_recap.txt44 lines · 4,979 characters · the full file
TASK
You check the recap of one segment of a lecture of a university course before learners see it. The recap was written from the segment's facts below and is shown right after the segment and again when the learner revises; the learner never sees the facts. It has two parts: the key points (what to remember, one idea each) and the summary text (what the segment explains as a whole and how its ideas hang together). Decide for each point and for the text whether it meets the criteria below. A part that fails is rewritten once.

{course}

THE SEGMENT
Lecture: {lecture_title}
What the segment teaches: {subgoal}
The larger step of the lecture it belongs to: {objective}

THE FACTS
What the segment teaches, one fact per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

THE CRITERIA
For each point and for the text:
1. supported. Everything it says is stated by the facts of the segment. It fails when it adds anything from outside, even something true. If the support is in a fact of the segment that the point does not cite, the point passes; give the number of the fact to add in "check".
2. faithful. A learner who remembers it believes what the facts say, no more and no less. Judge the meaning, not the wording: a paraphrase, a shorter phrase, or a different everyday word for the same thing passes, and so does leaving out a detail the point does not need. It fails only when a learner would come away believing something the facts do not say: a term is swapped for a different concept ("mass" turned into "weight"), a hedge or condition that changes what is true is dropped ("can", "usually", "typically", "if the list is already sorted"), examples ("such as") are turned into a complete list, one side of a comparison is lost, or the statement is made clearly stronger, weaker or wider than the fact. For the text it also fails when it joins two ideas with a cause or a purpose that neither the facts nor the lines under THE SEGMENT give.
3. stands_alone. It can be read without the segment and without the other points: its subject is named in full (not "the method", "the algorithm", "it" or "these" when nothing in the same sentence says which).
4. clear. Plain, grammatical and short enough to remember: a point is one sentence of about twenty words at most; the text is two to four sentences.
5. no_source_talk. It does not speak of the lecturer, the lecture, the segment, the video or the facts ("in this segment", "as we saw", "the lecturer explains"). A convention the course itself sets, stated as such ("in this course, the two terms are used for the same thing"), is not source talk.
For each point also:
6. one_idea. The point holds one idea that the segment is there to teach. It fails when it repeats the idea of an earlier point (name that point; only the later one fails), when it packs unrelated ideas into one sentence, or when it is only an example, a figure or an aside and the segment is not about that.
For the text also:
7. summary. The text reads as one explanation of what the segment teaches: it says what the segment is about and how its ideas connect. It fails when it is the points again one after the other with little changed, or when it leaves out the main thing named under "What the segment teaches".
8. coverage. The points and the text together leave out no fact that a learner needs for what the segment teaches. It fails only when you can name such a fact by its number; examples, figures and asides may be left out.

HOW TO CHECK
First the points, in order: put each point next to the facts it cites and compare the wording part by part, noting every word that sets how strong or how wide the statement is. Then the text: compare each sentence with the facts, then compare the text with the points. Fail a criterion only for a problem you can point to and quote. When you are still in doubt: on criteria 1 and 2 fail the part (it is then only reworded); on the others pass it.

THE RECAP
The points with their numbers and the numbers of the facts each cites, then the text.

{recap}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per point, in the order given, followed by one entry for the text, each with these fields in this order:
"item": the number of the point; 0 for the text.
"check": the words of the facts whose meaning the point or the text changes or goes beyond, each with its fact number; "none" if there are none.
"reason": one or two sentences. If it fails: what is wrong, quoting the words concerned and giving the fact numbers, and what a repaired version would say. The writer is given only this text, so it must be enough to repair the part. If it passes: which facts carry it.
"failed": the names of the criteria it fails, from "supported", "faithful", "stands_alone", "clear", "no_source_talk", "one_idea" (points only), "summary", "coverage" (text only); an empty list if it fails none.
"pass": true if "failed" is empty, otherwise false.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_judge_open_question.txt44 lines · 4,498 characters · the full file
TASK
You check open questions before learners see them. They were written from the facts of one lecture of a university course. The learner answers each question in a few sentences of their own after the lecture; the answer is compared with the reference answer, and the learner then reads the reference answer as feedback. The learner never sees the facts. A good open question asks the learner to explain, compare or reason, and its reference answer is the answer a learner who understood the lecture would give. Decide for each question whether it meets all seven criteria below. A question that fails is rewritten once and then dropped.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}
Its goal: {goal}

THE FACTS
What the lecture teaches, then facts from earlier lectures that it builds on, one per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

RELATIONSHIPS
How concepts of the lecture relate, each with the numbers of the facts that state it.

{relations}

THE SEVEN CRITERIA
1. answerable. Every statement of the reference answer is stated by the key facts the question cites: nothing from outside, nothing from a fact that is not cited (give its number), no hedge dropped ("can", "usually"), nothing made stronger. A learner who knows only those facts could write this answer.
2. answers_question. The reference answer answers what is asked, every part of it, and says so directly. It fails when it sets related facts side by side without stating the link the question asks for: asked "how does A improve on B", it describes A and describes B but never says how. It fails too when the facts hold no such link, so that no answer from the facts is possible.
3. open. Answering takes putting two or more facts together: a reason, a comparison, a consequence, how a part serves the whole. It fails for a question that only asks to repeat one fact, a list or a figure. It fails for a forced link: a question that ties two things together only because they share a term, or ties this lecture to an earlier one that it does not in fact build on.
4. one_thread. The question asks one thing, and the reference answer is the answer to it: a learner who understood the lecture would give an answer that can be compared with the reference. It fails for two questions joined into one ("How ..., and why ...?") and for a question so wide that many different answers would be as good.
5. no_giveaway. The question does not contain its own answer or most of it.
6. clear. The question is one or two grammatical sentences that stand alone (no "this", "the above"; things named in full) and presuppose nothing odd. The reference answer is two to four plain sentences of about eighty words at most, with the direct answer first.
7. no_source_talk. Neither the question nor the reference answer speaks of the lecturer, the lecture, the video or the facts ("in this lecture", "according to the lecture"). A convention the course itself sets, stated as such, is not source talk.

HOW TO CHECK
For each question: read the question only and the facts it cites, and write the direct answer in one sentence as those facts give it; if they give none, say so. Then read the reference answer: compare it with your sentence, and put each of its statements next to the key fact that states it. Then go through the criteria. Fail a criterion only for a problem you can point to and quote. When you are still in doubt: on criteria 1 and 2 fail the question; on the others pass it.

THE QUESTIONS
Each question with its number, the numbers of its key facts, the question and the reference answer.

{questions}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per question, in the order given, each with these fields in this order:
"item": the number of the question.
"check": the direct answer to the question in one sentence, as the key facts give it; "the facts give no answer" if they do not.
"reason": one or two sentences. If the question fails: what is wrong, quoting the words concerned and giving the fact numbers, and what a repaired question or reference answer would do. The writer is given only this text, so it must be enough to repair the question. If it passes: the key facts that carry the link the question asks for.
"failed": the names of the criteria it fails, from "answerable", "answers_question", "open", "one_thread", "no_giveaway", "clear", "no_source_talk"; an empty list if it fails none.
"pass": true if "failed" is empty, otherwise false.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_judge_odd_one_out.txt45 lines · 4,583 characters · the full file
TASK
You check "odd one out" puzzles before learners see them. Each puzzle was written from the facts of one lecture of a university course. The learner sees four items and nothing else: no hint of what the group is. Three of the items belong to one group that the lecture teaches (kinds of one thing, parts of one system, steps of one process, properties of one thing) and one does not; the learner picks the odd one and then reads the reason. The learner never sees the facts. Decide for each puzzle whether it meets all seven criteria below. A puzzle that fails is rewritten once and then dropped; a lecture without a puzzle is normal.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}

CONCEPTS
The concepts of the lecture, with a short definition.
{concepts}

RELATIONSHIPS
{relations}

THE FACTS
One fact per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

THE SEVEN CRITERIA
1. in_facts. The facts the puzzle cites state that the three items belong to the group, and state what the odd item is instead. It fails when the odd item is outside the group only because the facts do not mention it there, when the grouping rests on outside knowledge, or when the reason says something the facts do not.
2. real_group. The group is a group of the subject that the lecture teaches: the kinds, parts, steps or properties of one thing, named as such by a fact. And a learner who followed the lecture can recognise it from the three items alone. It fails for things that only happen to be mentioned together: the features of one product, the examples in one illustration, the places or people in one story.
3. near_miss. The odd item is of the same sort and level as the three (a kind among kinds, a part among parts) and belongs to a neighbouring group. It fails when the odd item is the name of the group or the whole that the three are parts of, when it is a thing of another sort (a unit of measurement beside three measuring instruments), or when it is so far from the topic that anyone could spot it without the lecture.
4. one_answer. Only the marked item can be defended as the odd one. Try each of the other three as the odd one: is there a reasonable grouping, by the facts and the concepts above or by what a learner plainly knows, that singles it out instead? Then try to argue the marked item into the group from any fact. It fails when either works, and when another puzzle of this lecture shows the same items with a different odd one.
5. no_giveaway. The odd item does not stand out by its form: the three members do not share a word that it lacks (or the reverse), and it is not much longer or shorter, differently capitalised, or a different kind of phrase.
6. clear. The four items are short and each is understood by itself; the reason is one correct sentence that names the group and says what the odd item is instead.
7. no_source_talk. The reason is a statement about the subject: it does not speak of the lecturer, the lecture or the facts ("the facts state", "is listed as", "is described as").

HOW TO CHECK
For each puzzle: read the four items only, as the learner does, and pick the odd one and the group yourself; note any second choice you could defend. Then read the marked item, the reason and the cited facts, and go through the criteria. Fail a criterion only for a problem you can point to and quote. When you are still in doubt: on criteria 1 and 4 fail the puzzle; on the others pass it.

THE PUZZLES
Each puzzle with its number, the numbers of the facts it cites, the four items in the order the learner sees them, the item marked as odd, and the reason.

{puzzles}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per puzzle, in the order given, each with these fields in this order:
"item": the number of the puzzle.
"check": the group in a few words with the number of the fact that names it, then any other item that could be defended as the odd one and why; "no other" if there is none.
"reason": one or two sentences. If the puzzle fails: what is wrong, quoting the items and the facts concerned, and what would repair it (another odd item, other wording), or that the facts hold no such group, in which case the puzzle should be left out. The writer is given only this text. If it passes: the facts that state the group and the fact that places the odd item.
"failed": the names of the criteria it fails, from "in_facts", "real_group", "near_miss", "one_answer", "no_giveaway", "clear", "no_source_talk"; an empty list if it fails none.
"pass": true if "failed" is empty, otherwise false.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_judge_put_in_order.txt38 lines · 4,404 characters · the full file
TASK
You check "put in order" exercises before learners see them. Each exercise was written from the facts of one lecture of a university course. The learner sees its title and, under it, the steps shuffled, and puts them back in order; the learner never sees the facts. An exercise is worth showing only when the order is something the lecture teaches and when exactly one order is right. Decide for each exercise whether it meets all seven criteria below. An exercise that fails is rewritten once and then dropped; a lecture without an exercise is normal.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}

THE FACTS
The lecture's facts by segment, one per line, each with its number in brackets.

{facts}

THE SEVEN CRITERIA
1. in_facts. Every step is stated by the facts the exercise cites, and so is the place of every step in the order. It fails when a step is only implied or comes from outside the facts, or when the order rests on general knowledge of the subject.
2. real_sequence. What is ordered is a sequence: the steps of a procedure, the stages of a pipeline, the path something travels, events in time, phases that follow one another, and the cited facts say what comes after what ("first", "then", "after", "before", "in the order", numbered stages, dates). It fails for the kinds, parts, factors or properties of a thing; for a problem with its cause, its solution and what the solution needs or brings; for things done in a project when no fact says which came after which; and when the only ground for the order is the order in which a fact lists things that could be done in any order.
3. one_order. Exactly one order is right. Take each pair of neighbouring steps and ask whether the two could be swapped without contradicting a fact: if so, it fails. It also fails when two steps describe the same moment or one event is split in two, and when a scale, a range or a stack of layers is ordered and the title does not say at which end to start.
4. no_giveaway. Nothing in the wording sorts the steps without understanding: no "first", "then", "next", "finally", "initial", "final", no step numbers, dates or years, no "the previous step", and no title that reveals the order.
5. worth_doing. The order has to be learned from the lecture. It fails when someone who never followed the lecture could order the steps from the wording or from common sense ("the task is started", "the task is executed", "the task is completed"; "input is given", "it is processed", "output is produced").
6. clear. The title is a short instruction that names in full what is ordered and stands alone; the steps are short, grammatical, alike in form, and each can be understood by itself.
7. no_source_talk. Neither the title nor a step speaks of the lecturer, the lecture, the segment, the video or the facts.

HOW TO CHECK
For each exercise: read the cited facts and mark the words in them that state an order. Then, without looking at the order given, order the steps yourself from those facts; note every pair you could not place from the facts. Then compare with the order given and go through the criteria. Fail a criterion only for a problem you can point to and quote. When you are still in doubt: on criteria 1 to 3 fail the exercise; on criteria 4 to 7 pass it.

THE EXERCISES
Each exercise with its number, the numbers of the facts it cites, its title and its steps in the order given as correct.

{exercises}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per exercise, in the order given, each with these fields in this order:
"item": the number of the exercise.
"check": the pair of neighbouring steps whose order the facts fix least, and the words of the fact that fix it, with the fact number; "not fixed" if no fact fixes it.
"reason": one or two sentences. If the exercise fails: what is wrong, quoting the steps and the facts concerned, and what would repair it (which steps to drop or reword, what to add to the title), or that the facts hold no sequence, in which case the exercise should be left out. The writer is given only this text. If it passes: the words of the facts that state the order.
"failed": the names of the criteria it fails, from "in_facts", "real_sequence", "one_order", "no_giveaway", "worth_doing", "clear", "no_source_talk"; an empty list if it fails none.
"pass": true if "failed" is empty, otherwise false.
Answer with the JSON only.
prompts/activity_judge_crossword.txt37 lines · 3,996 characters · the full file
TASK
You check the clues of a crossword before learners see it. The crossword is on one lecture of a university course; each answer is a concept of the lecture, and each clue was written from the text given for its answer below. The learner sees the grid, each clue and the number of letters of its answer, and nothing else. Solving it is a way to recall the concepts, so each clue must lead a learner who knows the lecture to its answer, and to no other. Decide for each clue whether it meets all six criteria below. A clue that fails is rewritten once.

{course}

THE LECTURE
{lecture_title}

THE ANSWERS
Each answer with its number in brackets, its other names and its definition (or the lecture's main statement about it).

{answers}

THE SIX CRITERIA
1. from_text. What the clue says is stated by the text given for its answer, and it is true of the answer itself. It fails when the clue adds something from outside the text, and when it describes another thing that the text is about: the text "Photosynthesis takes place in the chloroplasts of plant cells" is about photosynthesis, and a clue for "chloroplast" that says "how plants make sugar from light" describes photosynthesis, not the answer.
2. leads_to_answer. The clue says what the concept is or does, so that a learner who knows the lecture can reach the answer from the clue and the letter count. It fails when the clue only gives the answer's place in a list or a figure ("the second of the four steps", "one of the three parts of ...", "about 4% of ..."), or is so general that it gives nothing to go on ("a part of the system").
3. unique. The clue fits its own answer only. Read it against every other answer in the list: it fails when it fits one of them as well (name that answer; two answers with clues that could be swapped both fail), and when it fits a more familiar concept of the subject better than its answer.
4. no_giveaway. The clue contains neither the answer, nor one of its other names, nor a word of them in any form, nor a longer word built on one of them.
5. readable. The clue stands alone and reads as a phrase that could stand in the place of the answer: grammatical, at most fifteen words, with no blank ("___") that leaves a hole in the sense, and no "their", "these", "the above", "the software" or "the sequence" that points at something the clue does not name.
6. no_source_talk. The clue does not speak of the lecturer, the lecture or the course.

HOW TO CHECK
First read all the answers, so that you know which concepts stand next to each other in this grid. Then take the clues one at a time: cover the answer, read the clue with its letter count, and say which answer of the list you would give and whether another would fit as well. Then read the text of its answer and go through the criteria. Fail a criterion only for a problem you can point to and quote. When you are still in doubt: on criteria 1 and 3 fail the clue; on the others pass it.

THE CLUES
Each clue with the number of its answer, the answer, the letter count the learner sees, and the clue.

{clues}

ANSWER
Answer with a JSON object with one field, "verdicts": a list with one entry per clue, in the order given, each with these fields in this order:
"item": the number of the answer.
"check": the other answers of the list, or other concepts of the subject, that the clue fits as well as or better than its own answer; "none" if there are none.
"reason": one or two sentences. If the clue fails: what is wrong, quoting the words concerned, and what in the answer's text a better clue could use; if the text gives nothing that tells the answer apart, say so. The writer is given only this text. If it passes: the words of the answer's text that the clue puts in other words.
"failed": the names of the criteria it fails, from "from_text", "leads_to_answer", "unique", "no_giveaway", "readable", "no_source_talk"; an empty list if it fails none.
"pass": true if "failed" is empty, otherwise false.
Answer with the JSON only.

With --judge-rewrite, a unit with a failed item gets one rewrite: the writer's conversation continues with prompts/activity_rewrite.txt, one line per failed item with the judge's criteria and reason; the code checks run again, then the judge once more. The rewrite is kept only when the judge fails strictly fewer of its items. Nothing is dropped on a verdict: what still fails is flagged judge_failed.

{problems}

Write your answer again as the full JSON object, in the same form as before. Rewrite the items named so that the reviewer's objection no longer holds, and keep every other item exactly as it was. Where the reviewer says that the facts do not allow the item at all (they state no order, no group, no answer), leave the item out if the answer may hold fewer items; otherwise replace it with an item on another point of the facts. Everything the task asks still holds for what you rewrite.

Changes and why

The MVP's activity prompts (2026-11-13) were written in a hurry by Opus and never reviewed. Fable read the 63 lectures' outputs against their facts, counted each weakness with a rule that code could later apply, and rewrote the prompts and wrote the six judges (312cb6c, analysis/activities/review_prompts.md).

MVP quiz: the right option is the long one (18/A18, Q2)
{
 "question": "Why can inertial trackers be readily used in devices such as smartphones and head-mounted displays?",
 "options": [
  "They are typically built-in MEMS sensors with no range limitation",
  "They rely on external GPS satellites",
  "They require visible light cameras",
  "They depend on acoustic markers"
 ],
 "answer": 0,
 "explanation": "Inertial trackers are typically based on MEMS, are part of the device the user is using, and have no range limitation.",
 "facts": [
  "18/S8/F1"
 ]
}

The prompt had said “about the same length as the correct one”, and the model did not act on it:

THE QUESTIONS
- Write {count} questions. Each asks about one point of the facts that matters for what the segment teaches: what a thing is, why, how it works, how two things differ. No trivia (a name, a date or a number said in passing) and no question about the lecture itself.
- "facts": the numbers of the facts that answer the question. The correct option follows from those facts alone; add nothing from your own knowledge.
- The question stands alone: no "this", "the above", "in this segment" or "according to the lecture".
- "correct": the right answer, in a few words or one short sentence.
- "wrong": three wrong options. Each is plausible, of the same kind and about the same length as the correct one, and wrong by the facts: take them from the related concepts and the other facts, or change one detail of a fact so that it becomes false. Never "all of the above", "none of the above", or a second right answer.
- "explanation": one or two sentences saying why the correct option is right, from the facts.
- Each question asks about a different point.
Rebuild: an order of work, a self-test, a soft check, a judge

“Write the correct option first, then build each wrong option to match it … At least one wrong option is as long as the correct one or longer.” Plus the correct_longest check with retry and the judge's no_giveaway criterion. See quiz 30/A9 above: four options of one shape.

The correct option is the single longest in 52% of MVP questions (751 of 1,443) and 14% now (160 of 1,163). Chance is 25%.

The recap judge was too strict on wording. Its first run failed 969 recap items. Reading them, Fable found it failed paraphrases and dropped details that do not change what is true. Criteria 1 and 2 were rewritten to “judge the meaning, not the wording” (d730cd4) and the recaps judged again: 593 items failed. Over all kinds the judge's failures went from 1,465 to 1,086 (376 of the drop from the recap re-judgement, 3 from the odd-one-out rerun) (the counts are in the judge cache, activities/llm/cache.jsonl, keys judge1:recap:*, old and new).

The old criteria 1 and 2 git show d730cd4^:pipeline/prompts/activity_judge_recap.txt 
THE CRITERIA
For each point and for the text:
1. supported. Everything it says is stated by the facts: for a point, by the facts it cites; for the text, by the facts of the segment. It fails when it adds anything from outside, even something true, or when the support is in a fact the point does not cite (give the number of the fact to add).
2. faithful. A learner who remembers it believes what the facts say, no more and no less. It fails when a term is swapped for another one ("mass" turned into "weight"), when a hedge or a condition is dropped ("can", "usually", "if the list is already sorted"), when one side of a comparison is lost, or when the statement is made stronger, weaker or wider than the fact. For the text it also fails when it joins two ideas with a cause or a purpose that neither the facts nor the lines under THE SEGMENT give.

Odd one out, lectures 1 and 26 failed their code checks on every attempt at 6k thinking (“the three group items all contain ‘reality’…”, “the odd item is much longer”) and were rerun with a 10,240-token budget (configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_10k_96gb.json): lecture 26 now has a puzzle; for lecture 1 the model answered with none.

The judge is not yet measured. The review asked to run it three times on a seed set of known failures and passes before letting it drop anything (listed in Evaluation). That has not been done; so the judge only flags, and the viewer hides by the rule above.

QA

Question answering: a tool agent over the layer

Gemma 4 12Bquery embedder

A learner's question goes to a small model with five tools over knowledge/. It may only answer from what the tools return, and must put the IDs it used in square brackets after each claim. Code then turns each ID into “(lecture N, m:ss)”, drops any ID no tool returned, and reads the model's last line, COVERED: yes / partly / no.

In
the question, optionally the lecture being watched; knowledge/ (or $QA_OUTPUT/<course>/knowledge/)
Out
{"answer", "citations": [{"fact", "lecture", "segment", "start", "end", "text"}], "steps", "covered"}; logs in output/<course>/qa/answers.jsonl and llm_calls.jsonl (gitignored)
Command
python -m qa "What is an HMD?" [--lecture 4] [--course nptel_vr] [--config FILE] [--think | --no-think] [--json]; python -m qa --stop
Model
Gemma 4 12B QAT (unsloth/gemma-4-12B-it-qat-GGUF, UD-Q4_K_XL) on llama.cpp, configs/gemma4_12b_llamacpp.json (port 8092, 6 slots, thinking up to 8,192 tokens, temperature 1.0); on a cloud instance configs/gemma4_12b_demo.json or the vLLM configs/gemma4_12b_vllm_demo.json (google/gemma-4-12B-it). Queries embedded with Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B on the CPU.
Time
local RTX 5070: median 4.9 s, p90 7.6 s, max 8.9 s (34 answers); vLLM on the RTX PRO 6000: median 4.5 s, max 6.6 s, none over 8 s (17 answers); first words after a median 3.2 s when streamed
prompts/qa_system.txt23 lines · 2,728 characters · the full file
You answer a learner's questions about the lecture course "{course_title}". You know nothing about this course except what your tools return: the facts taught in its lectures, its concept cards, its lecture and course goals, and which lectures come before which.

WHICH TOOL
- A term ("what is X", "how does X work", "what kinds or parts of X are there", an acronym, a misspelt name): check_concept with the term. For "how do X and Y relate" or "how do X and Y differ": check_concept for X and for Y.
- check_concept says the term is unknown, or the card does not answer the question: fact_lookup.
- A question that is not about one term (how or why something happens, an example, steps, something across several lectures): fact_lookup, with the question as a short statement and two or three other wordings.
- "Where is X taught": check_concept (the card says where X is defined and which lectures use it).
- "What do I need before lecture N": lecture_dependencies. "What do I need before X": check_concept (the card lists the lectures that lead up to X).
- "What is lecture N about" or "for": lecture_overview. "What is this course about": course_goals.
- "This lecture" is the lecture the learner is watching, when the question says which.
- An unclear question: try fact_lookup with your best reading. If the results do not settle what is meant, ask the learner one short question back instead of guessing.
Call more than one tool when the question needs it. Never call a tool twice with the same arguments.

HOW TO ANSWER
1. Use only what the tools returned. Add nothing from your own knowledge: no facts, examples, numbers or explanations that the tool results do not contain, even when you know them to be true.
2. After each claim, put the IDs it comes from in square brackets, exactly as the tools show them: a fact like [4/S1/F2] or a segment like [4/S1]. Every sentence about the subject needs at least one. Use only IDs that appear in the tool results. The learner sees each one as the lecture and the time to watch.
3. If the tool results do not answer the question, say plainly that the course does not cover it. You may then add, with its IDs, what the course says that is closest. Do not answer it from your own knowledge.
4. If they answer only part of the question, answer that part and say which part the course does not cover.
5. Write for a learner: short and plain, two to five sentences, or a short list for kinds, parts or steps. Use the course's own terms. Do not mention tools, cards, scores or searches.
6. End with one line on its own: "COVERED: yes" if the course answers the question, "COVERED: partly" if it answers part of it, "COVERED: no" if it does not.

A real answer, with its timing

“What is the difference between immersion and presence?” on the rebuilt layer, local GPU (analysis/qa/bench_think_newlayer.jsonl, B07, run 0). Round 0 thought for 716 characters and called check_concept("immersion"); round 1 (no thinking) called it for presence; round 2 wrote the answer:

   },
   "result_summary": "card of presence; 24 facts"
  }
 ]

In virtual reality (VR), immersion and presence represent two distinct types of concerns:

* **Immersion** is the **technological issue** (lecture 8, 21:49). It is the objective, quantifiable degree to which a system projects stimuli onto a user's sensory receptors in an extensive, matching, and surrounding way (lecture 8, 10:12). 
* **Presence** is the **psychological issue** (lecture 8, 21:49). It is a subjective state of consciousness or psychological sense of being in the virtual environment where the user fails to acknowledge the technology's role and perceives the computer-generated stimuli as coming from the real world (lecture 8, 23:14; lecture 8, 26:40).

While immersion can aid or produce presence, it does not ensure it; the greater the immersion a system provides, the greater the potential for a user to feel present (lecture 8, 26:40).

Changes and why

34 answers each (17 questions × 2 runs)medianmeanp90maxover 8 s
before (thinking every round, cut-off 0.4)5.46.412.116.29
thinking in round 0 only + early exit4.85.28.09.34
shipped, rebuilt layer4.95.27.68.93
experiment: thinking off everywhere (not shipped)3.13.25.45.90
vLLM on the RTX PRO 6000, shipped (17 answers)4.56.60

Seconds; analysis/qa/STATE.md and the bench files. The vLLM row is computed from bench_vllm_shipped.jsonl.

  • Where the time went: thinking before the final answer (1,400–2,400 characters, 7–14 s), not the tools (0–0.9 s). Later rounds are now sent with enable_thinking: false; llama.cpp honours it per request and keeps its KV reuse (ebcc593).
  • Early exit. When every tool result so far found nothing close (ToolResult.found false), the next round must answer (“not covered” answers took about 20 s before; now about 4.4 s).
  • Thinking off everywhere was faster and wrong. Without thinking in round 0 the model answered a question the course does teach (B17, statistical tests, lecture 46) with “the course does not cover this”, without a search. Forcing a tool call did not work on llama.cpp: tool_choice: "required" made the model write its answer and run to max_tokens (3+ minutes), and a named function was ignored. On vLLM the forced variant had median 3.7 s but answered B17 without a search too (04a797a). Not shipped; the switches are qa.answer.ROUND0_THINK and ROUND0_TOOL_CHOICE.
  • Calibrated “no close match”. The cut-off was a guess (0.4). On 80 labelled queries (analysis/qa/calibration_queries.json) in-course scores run 0.63–0.86 and out-of-course 0.21–0.72 (AUC 0.989); 0.55 flags 27 of 39 out-of-course queries and none in-course (0.4 flagged 18).
  • Prerequisites ranked by use. Lists held odd items (“HMDs: displays from the 1950s” before lecture 16) because the graph links lectures on any single mention and the tool listed concepts in ID order. Now ranked by uses in the lecture.
  • Citations tidied: a time the model wrote before a citation is removed (the label carries the time); repeated labels in one line are written once; a list whose items share one label gets it once (90ffe5e). A tool's name written as a citation (“[lecture_dependencies]”) is removed (c0a0c8d).
  • Streaming (qa.answer_stream, efa7e21): time to the first draft median 3.2 s against 3.2–6.6 s to the whole answer.
Viewer

The viewer

standard-library Python + one page
As of 2026-11-17 · being redesigned

The viewer is what a learner opens. This describes it as it is on main; its look is being redesigned by another agent. viewer/serve.py (standard library only) serves the page (index.html, app.js, style.css, vendored dagre for map layout), the layers through lean JSON routes, the videos with HTTP Range, and the question box. Every layer is optional: a lecture without knowledge/ shows its 1b segments and its 0a transcript.

Command
python viewer/serve.py nptel_vr [--port 8765] [--host 127.0.0.1] [--root DIR] [--media DIR] [--output DIR] [--activities NAME] [--qa-path DIR] [--warm] [--prefer-youtube] [--video-chunk-mb 4] [--video-pace 2], then open http://localhost:8765
Pages
#/ course home (goals, lectures by week); #/lecture/<key>[/<tab>] with tabs overview, concepts, map, before, activities; #/lecture/<key>/t/<sec> seeks the video; #/concept/C0042 a card; #/glossary; #/map (course map and concept explorer); #/review
Flags in the page
?dev=1 developer mode (IDs, counts, judge marks, hidden items; learners never see statistics); ?yt=1 / ?yt=0 prefer YouTube; the theme toggle
  • Glossary: only the 1,316 defined concepts; connecting concepts stay on maps and keep their cards, and come in the glossary route by name only, for the explorer's search. Search by name, alias or acronym (“HRTF”).
  • Hiding: judge-failed items by the per-kind rule above; activities marked unresolved show only in developer mode.
  • Concept maps: a key view of about 20 concepts by default and an “all N, by part” view; the course map shows lectures by week with prerequisite arcs (0965ba9).
  • Videos: local files with Range, or YouTube by the youtube_id in titles.tsv when a file is missing.

Speed work and why

The live demo of 2026-11-13 felt choppy: the page loaded the four big knowledge files (9.6 MB) on every visit, and the demo was to run through an SSH tunnel. The rework (analysis/viewer/perf.md): lean routes built once and gzip-compressed with ETags (a reload answers 304); static files versioned and cached for good; lectures loaded lazily with neighbours prefetched when idle; tabs and map views built once and kept; the glossary rendered in slices; video answered in 4 MB chunks and paced at twice its bitrate so a tunnel never queues minutes of video ahead of the page; answers streamed.

Through an emulated SSH tunnel (a TCP proxy: 200 ms RTT, 10 Mbit/s, 4× CPU slowdown)BeforeAfter
Home, first visit9,297 ms (9,636 kB)1,082 ms (128 kB)
Home again8,894 ms426 ms
Lecture 5 opened directly9,127 ms641 ms
Glossary309 ms + 882 ms of long tasks343 ms, no long task
Page start to video metadata9,980 ms1,893 ms
Segment click (seek)1,875 ms576 ms
Streamed answer: first words (fake 3 s answer)—1,226 ms
Slower: concept card (one round trip, no longer preloaded)21 ms222 ms
Slower: course map (one round trip, then kept)24 ms235 ms

Measured with headless Chrome over CDP (analysis/viewer/tools/perf.mjs, video.mjs) through tools/tunnel.py, a stand-in for ssh -L (a real tunnel and tc netem were not possible on the test machine). On demo day, SSH from the user's network to us-east ran at about 40 kB/s each way, so the demo plan says to pick a region near the venue and time the tunnel first.

Part III · Evaluation

Facts and the fact judge

Block 1 built five sets on lectures 1, 4 and 16, labelled by Fable against the transcripts and the eight properties. The results are in stage 2b: the judge catches blatant errors (32 of 34) and misses real ones (1 of 7).

Property (gold failures)Caught in base / seed 1 / seed 2
Faithful (3: a dropped hedge, “this field” named wrong, a statement made true by definition)0 / 1 / 0
Whole (4: a summary, a recap, one point split in two)1 / 0 / 0
Significant, Terms in full (1 fact, 1/S0/F1)caught in base only

From analysis/fact_judge/report.md. The code checks against the same gold: pointing flagged 3 facts, all false alarms; acronym flagged none and missed “3D”, “4D”.

1/S0/F0The full forms of the abbreviations are extended reality (XR), virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), and mixed reality (MR), respectively.pass · borderlineOnly expands the four acronyms, and is worded badly ('the abbreviations', 'respectively'), but introducing the terms is the goal of the segment and the brackets make the sentence clear.
1/S0/F1To different people, hearing extended reality (XR), virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), or mixed reality (MR) may lead them to associate one or more of these terms with magical movie scenes such as those in Avatar, interactive video games, 3D or 4D movie-theater documentaries about extinct animals, a user wearing a headset that blocks vision and transports the user to a virtual world, which is the most common perception of VR, games such as Pokémon Go in which the real world is visible on a device screen and virtual objects that do not exist in the real world can also be seen on that screen, or visualizations of internal body structures such as a skeleton or veins.failSignificant, Whole, Established, Terms in fullA 120-word summary of the segment's motivating examples of what people associate with the terms; the lecturer raises each to ask whether it is XR, and teaches none of them. '3D or 4D' is not written in full. The one teachable point inside is the headset (sentences 59-61): worn on the head, blocks vision, the most common perception of VR.
1/S1/F0Extended Reality (XR) is an umbrella term, not a specific technology, that covers all present or future immersive technologies, such as augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and mixed reality (MR).passThe definition with what it is not, in one fact.
1/S1/F1The X in Extended Reality (XR) can be read as a variable, not only as part of 'extended', and its values such as A, V, and M denote augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and mixed reality (MR), while its many possible values form present or future immersive technologies.passThe lecturer teaches this reading of X on purpose (sentences 98-109).
1/S1/F2An immersive experience is a perceived experience through sensory organs that appears real: when a person experiences an environment through sensory organs and fails to distinguish that environment from the person's real environment, perceiving it as the real environment, the technology producing that experience is an immersive technology.passImmersive experience and immersive technology are one point here; nothing added.
1/S2/F0The reality-virtuality continuum is a scale whose extremes are a completely real environment with nothing virtual and a completely virtual environment with nothing real.passSentences 125 and 126 state both extremes ('nothing virtual there', 'nothing real here'); sentence 127 only repeats them, so it need not be cited.
1/S2/F1Augmented reality on the reality-virtuality continuum is the intermediate state in which the real environment is augmented with virtual content.pass'Intermediate state' paraphrases 'in between ... at one end we have augmented reality'; acceptable.
1/S2/F2Augmented virtuality on the reality-virtuality continuum is the intermediate state, opposite augmented reality, in which the virtual environment is augmented with real-world content.pass
1/S2/F3Mixed reality, in a broad sense, is the entire space between the real environment extreme and the virtual environment extreme of the reality-virtuality continuum, containing different degrees of augmentation of the real environment with virtual content and of the virtual environment with real content.passKeeps the limit 'in a broad sense'.
1/S3/F0A 2022 forecast estimated the share of content related to extended reality technologies in different application domains as video games 34 percent, healthcare 15 percent, engineering 14 percent, live events 12 percent, video entertainment 9 percent, real estate 7 percent, retail 5 percent, and military 4 percent, and these figures may now be larger.passOne list with all members and figures, year and the remark that the figures may be larger now. Market figures are what this segment is there to teach.

1/S3/F3In India, the extended reality industry was estimated at about 1.83 billion US dollars in 2020 and is expected to be about 14 billion US dollars in 2027.pass
1/S3/F4The metaverse is a term that has been in use for more than 30 years and has heavily stimulated growth in the extended reality field.passRightly leaves out who made the word popular.
1/S3/F5The metaverse is not merely an application of virtual reality, mixed reality, or augmented reality technology, while one broad view is that it reimagines the way humans interact with the digital world.pass · borderlineF6 is the detail of this fact's 'reimagines the way humans interact'; the two could be one point.
1/S3/F6In the metaverse, people act as they do in the real world in an all-around three-dimensional environment and use natural interaction with digital content, unlike current mostly flat two-dimensional screen interactions with occasional three-dimensional interaction.pass
1/S3/F7The metaverse is not yet fully realized because current technology is not advanced enough and has technological bottlenecks, but companies are investing heavily to make it a reality, with growth potential in both hardware and software.pass
1/S3/F8The growth of extended reality creates a need for more knowledgeable and skilled manpower who can develop and deploy systems based on the technology.pass
1/S3/F9The extended reality field is one of the major emerging areas identified by many national governments, along with artificial intelligence (AI), large language models, generative artificial intelligence, drones, and nanomaterials.pass
1/S5/F0The seminal reference for the reality-virtuality continuum is the 1994 paper 'A Taxonomy of Mixed Reality Visual Displays' by Paul Milgram and Fumio Kishino, one of the earliest papers in the field of mixed reality visual displays, which significantly influenced the development of that field.failFaithfulThe lecturer says the paper is one of the earliest 'in this field' and shaped 'the area', meaning virtual and augmented reality. The fact names the nearest noun instead: 'the field of mixed reality visual displays', taken from the paper's title.
4/S0/F0The term virtual means, according to Webster's New Universal Dictionary, being in essence or effect but not in fact, that is, something that is there but cannot be attributed to any factual things.pass
4/S0/F1The term reality is defined in dictionary terms as the state or quality of being real, as something that exists independently of ideas concerning it, or as something that constitutes a real or actual thing as distinguished from something merely apparent.pass
4/S0/F2Virtual reality (VR) is, according to the Miriam Webster dictionary, an artificial environment experienced through sensory stimuli, in which sights and sounds play a primary role and are provided by a computer, possibly along with other sensory stimuli, and in which a person's actions partially determine what happens in the environment.passFolds the lecturer's comments (other stimuli possible, sights and sounds primary) into the dictionary definition; they are cited.
4/S1/F0A virtual reality (VR) system is defined by three characteristics: an artificial or synthetic environment, sensory experience through computer-generated stimuli, and user actions that partially determine what happens in the environment; a system with these aspects can be categorized as a virtual reality system and distinguished from other systems, which are not supposed to have these aspects.fail · borderlineWholeTwo points. The first is the list of the three characteristics, named without describing them, which is one point and would pass alone (F1, F3 and F4 each describe one member; it is not a summary of the segment). The second, after the semicolon, is what the list is used for: a system with these aspects can be categorized as a virtual reality system and told from other systems, 'which are not supposed to have these aspects'. The lecturer brings that in as 'Another thing to note' (sentences 36-39) and teaches it in the next segment (4/S2/F0 holds it); it can be asked about separately. A correct fact holds the list only. Borderline: the clause can also be read as only saying what 'defining characteristics' means. Decided again on 2026-11-15; was pass, borderline.
4/S1/F1The artificial or synthetic environment of a virtual reality (VR) system is a computer-generated graphical representation of a real world or an imaginary or fantastic world, populated with artificial objects, and is not a real environment.pass
4/S1/F2An artificial environment in a virtual reality (VR) system can recreate a real world using real-world imagery or video, as in a virtual tour, but it still counts as a virtual reality system because it is a computer-generated scenario and changes are not happening in real time.pass
4/S1/F3The sensory experience characteristic of a virtual reality (VR) system means that the system provides computer-generated stimuli to the user's sensory organs, such as eyes and ears, so the user can immersively experience the artificial or synthetic environment, and this immersive experience is a hallmark of a virtual reality system.pass'this immersive experience' points at the experience named in the same sentence; the code check's flag is a false alarm.
4/S1/F4The user interaction characteristic of a virtual reality (VR) system means that the system must be interactive: the user can act on or interact with elements in the environment, and user actions or inputs partially determine what happens in the environment, producing dynamic changes in the virtual environment, just as people interact with objects in the real world.pass
4/S2/F0A system is a virtual reality (VR) system if it satisfies all three characteristics of a VR system—virtual environment, immersive experience through sensory organs, and interaction—and it is not a VR system if it does not satisfy at least one of them.pass
4/S2/F1A computer-aided design (CAD) system is not a virtual reality (VR) system because, although it supports a virtual environment and interaction, it does not support an immersive experience, since the user interacts with screen content through a mouse while still seeing the real world around the computer screen.pass
4/S2/F2A video conferencing system is not a virtual reality (VR) system because, although it is interactive, it is not a virtual environment or an immersive experience, since it presents a real-world environment in real time through a screen and the user remains in the real world, with the screen only aiding interaction.pass
4/S2/F3A three-dimensional (3D) movie experience is not a virtual reality (VR) system because, although it provides a virtual environment and a partly immersive experience, it is not interactive, as the viewer only watches and cannot change the displayed content.passLeaves out why it is partly immersive (it blocks the visual and partly the auditory sense, sentence 146); see missed.json.
16/S0/F0Tracking is an integral hardware component of an extended reality (XR) system.fail · borderlineWholeWith F1 this is one point, that tracking is an essential part of an XR system, split into two thin facts.
16/S0/F1Keeping track of motion in the physical world is a crucial part of any extended reality (XR) system, more importantly in virtual reality (VR) systems.fail · borderlineWholeSee F0: the same point.
16/S0/F2In virtual reality (VR), tracking is primarily about tracking the user's physical motion or input so that it can be translated into virtual motion in the virtual world.pass
16/S0/F3In augmented reality (AR), tracking is primarily about registering virtual content in three-dimensional (3D) space with respect to an anchor in the real world, and it requires tracking the user's pose, position, and orientation as well as tracking moving real-world objects in the field of view.fail · borderlineFaithfulThe lecturer says that in AR tracking 'is more about tracking objects and how they are moving in the field of view' (sentence 12), and gives registration as what AR requires (sentence 13: 'AR requires virtual content to be registered in 3D with respect to some anchor'), that is, the reason. The fact moves 'primarily about' to the registering: 'tracking is primarily about registering virtual content'. A student asked what tracking in AR is mainly about, against VR, would answer registration, not tracking moving objects. A correct fact: in AR, tracking is more about tracking objects and how they move in the field of view, because AR requires virtual content to be registered in 3D with respect to an anchor in the real world; this needs the user's pose, position and orientation and moving real-world objects to be tracked. Borderline: every part of the lecturer's statement is in the fact, only the weight is on the wrong part. Decided again on 2026-11-15; was pass, borderline.
16/S0/F4Tracking is also commonly called sensing; tracking is the more popular term, but the devices that enable tracking are more often called sensors than trackers.pass
16/S1/F0Tracking can be categorized by what is being tracked into three categories: tracking the user's sense organs, tracking the user's other body parts, and tracking the surrounding environment not related to the user.pass
16/S1/F1Tracking of the user's sense organs, such as eyes and ears, is needed because they have body-controlled degrees of freedom and a display attached to them must be perceived as attached to the surrounding world, so their position and orientation are tracked to update the virtual world or user experience.pass
16/S1/F2In virtual reality (VR), sense-organ tracking mostly focuses on head tracking, which is sufficient for the visual and aural components related to the eyes and ears.pass
16/S1/F3In virtual reality (VR), eye tracking may be required if rendering and display technology require compensating for eye movements, and eye tracking may be part of head tracking.passOne point about eye tracking (when it is needed, and that it may be part of head tracking). Splitting it gives two thin facts.
16/S1/F4Tracking of the user's other body parts is required when the user wants a representation of the body in the virtual world, such as avatars or virtual hands, and it may involve facial expressions or hand gestures for interaction, so the motion can be reproduced in the virtual world's matched zone.pass
16/S1/F5For tracking of the user's other body parts, perfect matching is not required; approximate movement or an approximate representation of the body-part movement in the virtual world can provide a good experience.failFaithfulThe lecturer says perfect matching 'may not be required'; the fact says it 'is not required'.
16/S1/F6A limiting case of tracking the user's other body parts is to send a simple explicit input signal, such as pressing a button, to change to a predetermined body configuration, such as making a virtual hand grasp an object, without elaborate or further tracking.pass
16/S1/F7Tracking the surrounding environment not related to the user may involve tracking physical objects in the real world around the user; it is more important in augmented reality (AR), but also important in virtual reality (VR).pass
16/S1/F8In augmented reality (AR), a moved physical object must be tracked in the real-world view so that digital content can be appropriately registered in three-dimensional (3D) space.pass
16/S1/F9In virtual reality (VR), tracking the surrounding environment can alert the user to physical objects not represented in the virtual world, such as a wall, to reduce harm; for this, tracking the user is not useful, and the environment itself must be tracked.pass'for this' points at the alerting named in the same sentence; the code check's flag is a false alarm.
16/S1/F10In virtual reality (VR), tracked physical objects may be matched in the virtual world so that the user receives touch feedback while interacting with them, giving a better, more natural experience.pass
16/S1/F11In telepresence applications, a large part of the physical world can be brought into the virtual world through live capture, augmenting the virtual world with a live capture of the real world; this is generally classified as augmented virtuality and is a form of tracking the surrounding environment.pass'this is generally classified' points at the live capture named in the same sentence; the code check's flag is a false alarm.
16/S2/F0The choice of how to track, and which tracking method or technique to apply, depends on tracker position, tracking process, and tracker types.pass
16/S2/F1Based on the position of the tracker or sensor that provides input for tracking, tracking is categorized into outside-in tracking and inside-out tracking.passThe list of the two kinds, by name; F2 and F5 define them.
16/S2/F2In outside-in tracking, the user's experience device does not contain the tracking sensors or trackers; instead, external trackers or sensors in the real world, such as cameras or markers, provide data that helps the system attached to the user identify and track the user's motion, pose, and orientation.pass
16/S2/F3Most virtual reality (VR) headsets today that use external sensors fall under outside-in tracking.failFaithfulThe lecturer says outside-in tracking is used by most VR headsets today. The fact says most VR headsets that use external sensors fall under outside-in tracking, which is true by definition and loses the point.
16/S2/F4Outside-in tracking is restricted to the specific area where its external sensors are placed and works only when the user is in that environment within the sensor range, whereas inside-out tracking has no such restriction because the user wears the sensors or the sensors are attached to the user.pass
16/S2/F5Inside-out tracking is a tracking type in which the cameras or other sensors required for tracking are located within the device used for the experience, such as the head-mounted display (HMD) in virtual reality (VR), which is more common, so no other external devices are needed.pass
16/S2/F6Examples of inside-out tracking are Microsoft HoloLens, Intel Project Alloy, and Qualcomm VR820.pass
16/S3/F0There are two broad ways to obtain input for tracking: inside-out and outside-in.failWholeSentences 102-103 recap the previous segment ('we have seen ... there are two ways'); the fact restates the recap and adds nothing (the same point is 16/S2/F1).
16/S3/F1The tracking process broadly consists of four steps: calibration, registration, integration, and drift correction.pass
16/S3/F2In the tracking process, calibration is the step that transforms the output of imperfect sensors so that it behaves as closely as possible to the natural or real value, reducing sensor error.pass
16/S3/F3In the tracking process, registration estimates or determines the initial position and orientation of the sensor, so that the starting point is known before movement can be tracked.pass
16/S3/F4In the tracking process, integration aggregates sensory measurements from multiple sensors or input data sources at discrete time points to combine the values and track sense organs, body parts, or the environment.pass
16/S3/F5In the tracking process, drift correction compensates for the difference between the estimated and actual position and orientation of the user, the user's sense organs, body parts, or objects in the environment, reducing estimation error.pass
