Block 6 question set, first draft (85 questions: what_is 9, how_works 8, relate_differ 9, list_kinds_parts 9, where_taught 7, prerequisites 7, lecture_purpose 7, cross_lecture 8, acronym_misspelt 8, not_covered 7, unclear 6) branch fable-gold-sets, analysis/qa/questions.json (not merged; not yet checked)
IDKindQuestionCoveredReference answerLectures
Q001what_isWhat is extended reality (XR)?yesXR stands for extended reality. It is an umbrella term, not one specific technology: it covers all immersive technologies, present or future, such as AR, VR and MR. The X can be read as part of 'extended' or as a variable that takes the values A (augmented), V (virtual) or M (mixed).1
Q002what_iswhat does immersion mean in VR?yesImmersion is the objective degree to which a VR system and application projects stimuli onto the user's sensory receptors in a way that is extensive, matching, surrounding, vivid, interactive and plot informing (the definition of Slater and Wilbur). It is the technological, system side of the experience: what the technology provides. Earlier, in lecture 1, 'immersive' is introduced as an experience through the sense organs that appears real, so that the user cannot tell the environment from the real one.1, 8
Q003what_isWhat is presence?yesPresence is a psychological state or subjective perception in which, although part or all of what a person experiences is generated or filtered by technology, the person's perception fails to acknowledge the role of the technology (definition of the International Society for Presence Research). In VR it means the user perceives the computer-generated stimuli as if they came from the real world: the feeling of really being in the virtual world. Creating a high degree of presence is the overall goal of a VR system.8
Q004what_iswhat is a matched zoneyesThe matched zone is the place where the real and the virtual world perfectly align: a region of the real world in which the user can physically move (the movement is confined to this safe region) and which is also a part of the virtual world, so physical motion there is matched by the same motion in the virtual world. Each user has their own matched zone; moving beyond it needs locomotion techniques.19, 20
Q005what_isWhat is the uncanny valley effect?yesThe uncanny valley effect is the finding that digital representations of humans that look almost real evoke unease or eeriness instead of familiarity, because of subtle imperfections. As human likeness rises, familiarity rises, but at about 70 to 85 percent likeness it drops before rising again: that dip is the uncanny valley. The study was originally about robots. For VR it means that pushing for realism does not always give a better experience.9
Q006what_isWhat is usability?yesThe course uses the ISO definition (ISO 9241-210): usability is the extent to which a product can be used by specified users to achieve specified goals with effectiveness, efficiency and satisfaction in a specified context of use. Effectiveness: can the users do what they want to do; efficiency: how fast; satisfaction: how satisfied they are while using it.24
Q007what_isWhat is a game engine?yesA game engine is a middleware on which the developer's application runs. It bundles several engines, typically a rendering engine, a physics engine, an audio engine and a user interface, with core functions such as rendering, physics, audio, scripting and networking support; an authoring tool gives access to them. Examples named: Unity 3D, Unreal Engine, CryEngine, Godot, Lumberyard, Amazon Sumerian. In the course a game engine serves as a ready-made virtual world generator for VR.20
Q008what_iswhat is contextual inquiry?yesContextual inquiry is a method to gather end-user (usability) requirements. It is a semi-structured interview process made of observation and interviews: the designers watch users perform their tasks in their natural work setting and ask questions to clarify what they see. The observer acts as an apprentice and treats the user as the master (master-apprentice model).35, 36
Q009what_isWhat is a prefab in Unity?yesA prefab lets you create, configure and store a game object so that it acts as a template from which instances are made in the scene. Example from the lecture: in a shooter game a type of bullet is a prefab, and many instances of it are created around a level.60
Q010how_worksHow does tracking work? What are the steps?yesTracking works in four broad steps. Calibration: the sensor output is transformed to be as close to the real value as possible, since no sensor is perfect. Registration: the initial position and orientation of the sensor are determined. Integration: the sensor measurements, taken at discrete points of time and possibly from several sensors, are combined to follow what is tracked. Drift correction: the difference between the estimated and the actual position and orientation is compensated.16
Q011how_worksHow is drift corrected when tracking with an IMU?yesDrift correction compensates the error between the estimated and the actual orientation, and it needs at least one extra sensor besides the gyroscope: in an IMU the accelerometer and the magnetometer, or a separate camera. A classic approach blends the two separately computed orientations at the same instant with a gain parameter alpha between 0 and 1: corrected orientation = alpha x (orientation from integrating the gyroscope) + (1 - alpha) x (orientation from the other sensor).17
Q012how_worksHow can I tell whether some system is a VR system or not?yesCheck it against the three defining characteristics of VR: (1) an artificial, computer-generated environment, (2) an immersive experience through computer-generated stimuli to the sense organs, (3) interactivity: the environment changes with the user's actions. A system is VR only if it has all three. Examples in the lecture: a CAD system (virtual and interactive but not immersive), a video conference (interactive only) and a 3D movie (virtual, partly immersive, not interactive) are not VR.4
Q013how_workshow does marker based AR workyesA marker-based AR application uses markers placed in the environment to trigger the augmentation. When the device is pointed at a marker and detects it, the digital content is placed at the marker on the screen view of the real world. Markers can be QR codes, colour codes or bars, or fiducial markers. Such applications are easy to build but hard to maintain, because markers are prone to damage.3
Q014how_worksHow does the virtual world generator work?yesThe virtual world generator (VWG) is an abstraction of the VR software, treated as a black box. It receives input events from low-level systems (head tracker, keyboard, mouse, game controller), which arrive in a queue. Its key role is to maintain enough of an internal reality: it keeps the virtual world in step with the input so that the renderers (visual, aural, haptic) can compute matching output for the displays. Its basic operation is to keep the user's motions in the real and the virtual world in correspondence. For this it must support the virtual world (graphics, or a captured world), matched motion, locomotion, physics and networked experiences.19
Q015how_worksHow does the Wizard of Oz method work?yesIn a Wizard of Oz experiment the user believes they are using a working system, while a hidden human (the wizard) produces the system's responses. In the lecture's example the subject speaks into a microphone and thinks the computer converts speech to text; in fact the wizard, hidden behind a wall, hears the speech, types it, and the text appears on the subject's screen. So an idea can be tested with users before the technology exists.42
Q016how_worksHow is a heuristic evaluation carried out?yesHeuristic evaluation is an expert (quick) evaluation technique: a prototype is evaluated against a checklist whose items are called heuristics. No scenario or task description is needed; the system is judged as a whole. A team of three to five evaluators, usually design team members or people familiar with the problem, each evaluate independently and report a list of usability issues. A general checklist is Nielsen's 10 heuristics.43
Q017how_worksHow do you find the best tour path when the user should not stay in VR longer than a time limit?yesThe virtual world is modelled as a weighted graph whose nodes are the points of interest. A time threshold is set (above it cybersickness may occur). As not every node can then be visited, each node gets an importance (a positive number) and a waiting time. The task is to find a path that maximises the total importance while travel time plus waiting time stays within the threshold. This is a vehicle routing problem with profit (VRPP): the graph is made complete with hypothetical edges, edge weights are set to travel times from the natural walking model, an existing VRPP solver is run, and hypothetical edges in the result are replaced by shortest paths.52
Q018relate_differWhat is the difference between AR and VR?yesVR completely replaces the user's perception of the real world with a virtual environment; AR alters (augments, enhances) the ongoing perception of the real world with digital content. So VR needs nothing real to exist, while AR needs the real scene: in the lecture's example, a building that is not yet built can be walked through in VR, but for AR the building must exist, and virtual furniture can then be shown in its rooms.5
Q019relate_differHow are immersion and presence related?yesImmersion is objective and quantifiable: what the system provides (the technological side). Presence is a state of consciousness, the psychological sense of being in the virtual environment; it is inside the user's mind and hard to measure objectively. Immersion aids presence and limits it (more immersion, more potential presence), but does not guarantee it: a user who shuts their eyes has immersion without presence. Presence is a function of both the user and the immersion.8
Q020relate_differinside-out vs outside-in tracking: what's the difference?yesThe difference is where the sensors are. In outside-in tracking the sensors (or markers) are external to the device, placed in the environment; tracking only works inside the area covered by them. In inside-out tracking the cameras or other sensors are in the device itself (for VR, the HMD), no external device is needed, and the user can go anywhere. Examples of inside-out: Microsoft HoloLens, Intel Project Alloy, Qualcomm VR820.16
Q021relate_differIs mixed reality the same thing as augmented reality?yesIn this course, yes: MR is treated as another term for AR. The lecture notes that practitioners do not agree on a definition of MR and lists several: everything between the two extremes of the reality-virtuality continuum (so AR is part of MR); a combination of AR and VR in one app or device; a 'strong', more capable AR with an advanced understanding of the physical environment; or simply a synonym of AR. The course takes the last one for simplicity.5
Q022relate_differWhat's the difference between augmented reality and augmented virtuality?yesThey are opposites on the reality-virtuality continuum. In augmented reality the real world is augmented with virtual (digital) content; in augmented virtuality the virtual world is augmented with real-world content, for example a real human figure shown inside a virtual environment. The whole space between the fully real and the fully virtual environment is broadly called mixed reality.1
Q023relate_differhorizontal prototype vs vertical prototype?yesA horizontal prototype shows the whole interface at the surface level without functionality: only the look and feel is prototyped, not the interaction, so no real work can be done with it. A vertical prototype represents the interactions or functionalities, and suits the analysis of interactions and features. A single sketch is a horizontal prototype; a series of sketches (a storyboard) can act as a vertical one.42
Q024relate_differWhat is the difference between a within-subject and a between-subject design?yesIn a within-subject (repeated measures) design every participant performs the tasks for all test conditions. In a between-subjects design the tasks for the test conditions are divided among the participants, so not everyone does everything. Within-subject designs are easier to manage but can suffer from the practice effect, which adds noise to the data.46
Q025relate_differHow do rods and cones differ?yesBoth are photoreceptors in the retina. Rods are more numerous in the peripheral region; cones are mainly in the small central region, the fovea. Several rods can share one optic nerve, while each cone can have its own, which aids acuity. Vision with cones only is photopic vision, which gives colour perception; vision with rods only is scotopic vision.27
Q026relate_differTravel and wayfinding - are they the same?yesNo, they are the two components of navigation. Travel (locomotion) is the motor component: the actual movement from one location to another. Wayfinding is about knowing where one is and working out a path to the destination: building a mental model or cognitive map of the surroundings. Without wayfinding support, travel techniques cannot be used efficiently in a complex environment.40
Q027list_kinds_partsWhat are the characteristics of an AR system?yesBy the systemic definition an AR system has three characteristics: it combines real with virtual, it is interactive in real time, and it is registered in 3D. The lecture then summarises four: the real world is enhanced with computer-generated content; the enhancement is registered in 3D (it appears at its appropriate place); the enhancement is experienced through the sense organs; and it supports interaction. A system is AR only if all are satisfied.5
Q028list_kinds_partsWhat are the categories of tracking?yesBy what is tracked there are three categories: tracking the user's sense organs (eyes, ears; in VR mostly head tracking), tracking the user's other body parts (needed when the body is shown in the virtual world, e.g. virtual hands), and tracking the rest of the environment (physical objects around the user). By the position of the tracker there are two types: outside-in and inside-out.16
Q029list_kinds_partsList the types of AR applications.yesBy technology there are two broad categories: marker-based and markerless AR applications. Markerless ones have four subcategories: location-based (a sensor such as GPS gives the user's location), superimposition-based (objects are recognised and content is superimposed), projection-based (light projectors put content on objects or surfaces) and outlining-based (object recognition is used to outline the surroundings, e.g. cars in low light).3
Q030list_kinds_partswhat are the six qualities of immersion?yesExtensive (the range of sensory modalities presented), matching (congruence between modalities, e.g. the view follows head motion), surrounding (how panoramic the cues are, e.g. a wide field of view), vivid (the quality of energy within a modality: resolution, lighting, frame rate, audio bitrate), interactive (the user can change the world and influence future events) and plot informing (the user can follow the story of the experience).8
Q031list_kinds_partsWhat are the phases in the history of VR?yesFour phases: the early era, before digital computers (e.g. Wheatstone's stereoscope of 1838); the formative years, roughly 1950 to 1990 (Sensorama, Sutherland's Sword of Damocles, the term 'virtual reality'); the modern phase, 1991 to 2015 (CAVE, VRML, Oculus Rift); and the growth phase from 2016 onwards, which is the current one.6
Q032list_kinds_partsWhat are the stages of the interactive system development life cycle?yesFeasibility study; requirement gathering, analysis and specification; the design-prototype-evaluate cycle (design, prototyping and early evaluation); code design; coding and implementation; code testing; empirical (usability) study; deployment and maintenance. The life cycle is highly iterative: there are loops between the stages to take in user feedback.32
Q033list_kinds_partsWhat are the stages of the 3D graphics pipeline?yesFive stages: (1) object representation, (2) modeling transformation (local to world coordinates), (3) lighting, (4) the viewing pipeline, with the sub-stages viewing transformation, clipping, hidden surface removal, projection transformation and window-to-viewport transformation, and (5) scan conversion (rendering, rasterization). An implementation need not follow exactly this order.55, 58
Q034list_kinds_partswhat causes cybersickness? list the factorsyesTwo groups. Internal factors (of the user): age, gender, motion sickness susceptibility, physical condition and emotional condition. External factors: hardware (display type, latency, latency jitter, resolution, input technology) and VR content (visual motion, means of locomotion, field of view, type of content, duration, visual complexity).10, 11
Q035list_kinds_partsWhat tracking technologies are used in VR and AR?yesElectromechanical trackers, electromagnetic trackers, acoustic (ultrasonic) trackers, vision-based (optical) tracking with infrared, visible light or 3D structure sensing, inertial trackers (gyroscope, accelerometer, magnetometer), and others: location-based (GPS) and hybrid trackers that combine two or more of the basic methods. Hybrid trackers typically perform better than a single technology.18
Q036where_taughtWhere is the reality-virtuality continuum explained?yesIn lecture 1 (General Intro to XR), from about 15:30: a scale with the real environment at one end and the virtual environment at the other, augmented reality and augmented virtuality in between. The lecture ends with its source, Milgram and Kishino's 1994 paper. Lecture 5 returns to it when defining mixed reality (about 15:00).1, 5
Q037where_taughtWhich lecture explains the matched zone?yesLecture 19 (VR Software), at about 19:50, in the part on matched motion. Lecture 16 (Understanding Tracking I) uses the term earlier and says it was already discussed, but the definition comes in lecture 19; lecture 20 adds that each user has a distinct matched zone.16, 19, 20
Q038where_taughtwhere does the course talk about the simulator sickness questionnaireyesLecture 11 (Cybersickness), at about 20:15, introduces the SSQ as one of the most used questionnaires for measuring cybersickness. It comes back in lecture 44 (among the questionnaires specific to XR) and in lecture 47, where it is used to evaluate the Majuli VR tour.11, 44, 47
Q039where_taughtIn which lecture are Shneiderman's eight golden rules covered?yesLecture 38 (System Design), from about 11:20 to the end of the lecture: the rules are given one by one, starting with 'strive for consistency'.38
Q040where_taughtWhere can I find the part about the blind spot of the eye?yesLecture 27 (Visual Perception & Displays I), near the end, at about 29:00: the region of the retina where the optic nerve leaves holds no photoreceptors, so light falling there is not perceived.27
Q041where_taughtWhere do I learn how to write scripts in Unity?yesLecture 62 (Linking theory to practice), from about 01:30: scripting support in Unity, C# scripts attached to game objects as components, how to create a script, and the anatomy of a script (MonoBehaviour, Start, Update).62
Q042where_taughtWhere is teleportation discussed?yesMainly in lecture 40 (System Design), at about 17:40, as a selection-based locomotion technique. It also appears in lecture 19 (VR Software, about 23:00) as a way to move beyond the matched zone, in lecture 12 (Cybersickness, about 26:40) as a locomotion choice that reduces cybersickness, and in lecture 59, where the Majuli tour uses it to move between information points.12, 19, 40, 59
Q043prerequisitesWhat do I need to know before lecture 16 (Understanding Tracking I)?yesLecture 16 builds on: what VR and AR systems are and how they differ (lectures 4 and 5), including that AR content must be registered in 3D; the idea of pose (position and orientation) and its registration and tracking, raised as an AR challenge in lecture 9; the VR and AR hardware of lectures 13 to 15 (displays, HMDs, sensors and input devices), which the lecture says it continues; and augmented virtuality from lecture 1. One term it uses, the matched zone, is only defined later, in lecture 19.5, 9, 13, 16
Q044prerequisiteswhat should I know before watching lecture 5?yesLecture 5 (Definitions, on AR and MR) continues lecture 4: you need the definition of reality and of virtual reality and the three characteristics of a VR system, since AR is defined and compared in the same way. It also uses the reality-virtuality continuum from lecture 1 for one definition of MR, and the AR system categories from lecture 3 (superimposition).1, 4, 5
Q045prerequisitesWhat do I need before lecture 17?yesLecture 17 continues lecture 16, so you need its content: the three things that can be tracked (sense organs, other body parts, the surrounding environment), inside-out and outside-in tracking, and the four stages of the tracking process (calibration, registration, integration, drift correction), which lecture 17 works through in detail with IMU sensors. Knowing what an HMD is (lecture 14) helps.16, 17
Q046prerequisitesWhat do I need to understand before lecture 51 on the optimal path?yesYou need: cybersickness and that it grows with exposure duration (lectures 10 to 12); experience optimization as minimizing cybersickness, and system-controlled virtual tours (lecture 49); the realistic walking speed model of lecture 50, whose speeds give the travel times; and points of interest, as in the Majuli tour (lecture 41). The lecture also assumes basic graph theory (vertices, weighted edges, the travelling salesman problem), which the course does not teach beforehand.12, 49, 51
Q047prerequisiteswhat concepts do I need before I can understand presence?yesPresence is introduced in lecture 8 right after immersion and against it, so you need: the definition of VR and its characteristics (lecture 4), in particular the immersive experience through computer-generated stimuli; and immersion, the objective, technological side (lecture 8), since presence is the psychological side and depends on immersion.4, 8
Q048prerequisitesWhat do I need to know before drift correction?yesDrift correction is the last of the four tracking steps, so you need what tracking is and the first three steps, calibration, registration and integration (lecture 16): drift correction fixes the error left in the orientation estimated by integration. You also need the IMU sensors (lecture 17): the gyroscope gives angular velocity, and the accelerometer and magnetometer (or a camera) serve as the extra sensor.16, 17
Q049prerequisitesWhat are the prerequisites for this course?yesAccording to the course page: a science or engineering background is preferable; knowledge of software system development, data structures, algorithms and computer graphics is helpful but not mandatory. The course is aimed at UG, PG and PhD students of computer science and allied disciplines, and at software developers interested in extended reality.
Q050lecture_purposeWhat is lecture 1 for?yesLecture 1 (General Intro to XR) introduces the field: the terms XR, AR, VR and MR in broad strokes, the reality-virtuality continuum, why the field is worth learning (market size, the metaverse, the need for skilled manpower), and what the course will cover, including the case study of a VR system for digital heritage preservation.1
Q051lecture_purposeWhat is the point of lecture 4?yesLecture 4 (Definitions) gives a precise definition of virtual reality, starting from the dictionary meanings of 'virtual' and 'reality', and derives the three defining characteristics of a VR system: an artificial environment, an immersive experience through the sense organs, and interactivity. It then uses them to decide whether a system is VR (a CAD system, a video conference, a 3D movie: none is).4
Q052lecture_purposeWhat will I learn in lecture 16?yesLecture 16 (Understanding Tracking I) gives the basic understanding of tracking: why tracking is needed in VR and AR; the three categories of what is tracked (sense organs, other body parts, the surrounding environment); the two ways of placing the trackers, inside-out and outside-in; and the four steps of the tracking process (calibration, registration, integration, drift correction).16
Q053lecture_purposewhat is lecture 12 aboutyesLecture 12 concludes the topic of cybersickness. It finishes the evaluation part (interpretable, black box and hybrid models, and the outputs of evaluation: classes or sickness scores) and then covers mitigation strategies: what developers can do (field of view adjustment, dynamic blur, rest frames, locomotion techniques) and what users can do (limit exposure time, habituation, controlled breathing).12
Q054lecture_purposeWhat is lecture 33 for?yesLecture 33 introduces the case study used in the following lectures to explain the stages of XR system development: the digital heritage preservation of Majuli river island (on the Brahmaputra in Assam). It describes the island, its tangible and intangible heritage, why it needs preserving (floods and erosion), and how immersive technology (a VR tour) can help.33
Q055lecture_purposeWhat does lecture 48 cover?yesLecture 48 is the last part on system implementation. It recaps the interactive system development life cycle and what happens in its stages, and then introduces agile development as the practical way to build interactive systems: short iterations (sprints) of four to six weeks, frequent feedback, methods such as Scrum and Extreme Programming. The life cycle is said to be mainly for understanding the flow. It closes the block on building a system before optimization starts.48
Q056lecture_purposeWhat is the last lecture (63) for?yesLecture 63 gives the concluding remarks: a summary of the course as six modules (introduction; hardware and software; human factors; building XR systems; optimization; linking theory to practice and conclusion) with the lectures of each, the topics the course did not cover (computer vision, cloud computing, machine learning, LLMs and generative AI, IoT, robotics, blockchain), and its aim: a foundation to build on, not mastery of every related discipline.63
Q057cross_lectureWhat did Ivan Sutherland contribute to VR and AR?yesIn the VR history (lecture 6) he built the Sword of Damocles HMD in 1967 and was the first to demonstrate an HMD with head tracking and computer-generated imagery. In the AR history (lecture 7) the same work counts as the first truly computer-generated AR experience: with Bob Sproull he created the first prototype AR system.6, 7
Q058cross_lectureHow is presence defined, and how was it measured for the Majuli tour?yesPresence (lecture 8) is the psychological state in which the user's perception fails to acknowledge the role of the technology: the sense of really being in the virtual world. For the Majuli VR tour it was a key concern (lecture 34) and was measured in the empirical study (lecture 47) with a standard presence questionnaire, the revised version of 2004 (called QEP in the lecture), tailored to the tour, with answers on a 1 to 7 Likert scale; items covered involvement, interface quality, adaptation and immersion, and visual fidelity.8, 34, 47
Q059cross_lectureInteraction comes up in the definitions of both VR and AR. How does each use it?yesFor VR (lecture 4) interactivity is the third defining characteristic: the user's actions partly determine what happens, so the virtual environment changes dynamically with them. For AR (lecture 5) the system must be interactive in real time: the user interacts with the digital content inside the enhanced real-world view, and the result shows immediately. Interactivity is also one of the six qualities of immersion (lecture 8): the user can change the world and influence future events.4, 5, 8
Q060cross_lectureWhich navigation techniques from the design lectures are used in the Majuli VR tour?yesThe design lectures (lecture 40) give walking-based, steering-based, selection-based (e.g. teleportation) and manipulation-based locomotion, plus wayfinding support. The Majuli tour combines them: walking-based navigation within an information point (moving along 360-degree images by selecting arrows), and teleportation or a vehicle metaphor between points on the island. For wayfinding it has a world-in-miniature map of the island and a minimap at each location.40, 41, 59
Q061cross_lectureWhy is tracking a challenge for AR, and how do the tracking lectures deal with it?yesLecture 9 names tracking as a major AR issue: to register virtual content in 3D, the pose (position and orientation) of the viewer relative to a real-world anchor must be determined at the start (registration) and kept up to date as the user moves (tracking). Lecture 16 takes this up: in AR, tracking means following the user's pose and also moving objects of the real world, and it works in four steps (calibration, registration, integration, drift correction); lectures 17 and 18 add the sensors and the technologies, such as vision-based tracking and SLAM.9, 16, 18
Q062cross_lectureWhy does VR need a GPU? I saw it in the hardware part and again later.yesLecture 14 (VR Hardware) names the GPU, with CPU and RAM, as a main part of the processing element: GPUs suit the rendering of the complex scenes of realistic VR, and CPU-only systems may have performance problems. Lecture 54 explains that rendering is given to a dedicated component, the graphics card with its GPU, so the CPU is not used up by it. Lecture 58 gives the reason: graphics operations are highly parallel, CPUs cannot exploit this well, and a GPU has many cores that apply the same instruction to many vertices at once.14, 54, 58
Q063cross_lectureHow does the course use exposure time to control cybersickness?yesIn three places. As a cause: duration is one of the content-related factors (lecture 11). As advice to users (lecture 12): avoid long continuous exposure; studies suggest a VR session of at most about 55 to 70 minutes. As a design method (lectures 51 and 52): the tour is optimised to shorten exposure, first by finding the path that covers all points of interest in the least time, then by setting a time threshold and choosing the path that covers the most important points within it.11, 12, 51, 52
Q064cross_lectureWhat are the modules of the course and which lectures belong to each?yesSix modules, as summarised in lecture 63: (1) introduction, lectures 1 to 12; (2) hardware and software, lectures 13 to 22 (VR and AR hardware 13-15, tracking 16-18, VR and AR software 19-22); (3) human factors, lectures 23 to 31; (4) building XR systems with the Majuli case study, lectures 32 to 48; (5) optimization, lectures 49 to 59 (experience 49-53, resources 54-59); (6) linking theory to practice with Unity, lectures 60 to 62, and the concluding remarks, lecture 63.63
Q065acronym_misspeltwhat is VIMS?yesVIMS stands for visually induced motion sickness. It is another name for cybersickness (CS): a condition that can affect people when they interact with virtual reality technology, with symptoms such as nausea, headache, dizziness and disorientation.10
Q066acronym_misspeltHRTF?yesHRTF stands for head-related transfer function. It models how we receive sound from a location in space; a pair of HRTFs, one per ear, can be used to synthesize binaural sound, so that sound sources can be localised in an XR system. It depends on the size and shape of the head, ears and ear canal, so every person has a unique HRTF; in practice one for an average listener is used.30
Q067acronym_misspeltwhat is an imu sensoryesIMU stands for inertial measurement unit: a composite piece of hardware with three types of sensors, found in many HMDs and mobile phones. The gyroscope captures angular velocity, the accelerometer linear acceleration along the three axes, and the magnetometer the magnetic field strength along the three axes. Tracking with an IMU relies mainly on the gyroscope; the other two help detect and correct drift.17
Q068acronym_misspeltWhat does SLAM stand for and what is it?yesSLAM stands for simultaneous localization and mapping: techniques that create and update a map of the real environment while localizing positions within it, so that tracking is possible in real time. It was originally developed for robot navigation and later adapted for augmented reality; a further optimisation is PTAM (parallel tracking and mapping).18
Q069acronym_misspeltILD and ITD in sound localisation?yesThey are the two binaural cues for localising a sound source. ILD is the interaural level difference: the difference in sound level between the two ears. ITD is the interaural time difference: the difference in the time at which the sound arrives at each ear.30
Q070acronym_misspeltwhat is the go go techneque?yesThe go-go technique is a hand selection technique for selecting distant virtual objects without tiring the arm. It lets the user change the length of the virtual arm: near the user the virtual hand follows the real hand one to one, but as the distance increases, small real hand movements are mapped to larger virtual hand movements, so far objects can be reached.39
Q071acronym_misspeltWhere is Majoli island and why is it in the course?yesMajuli is a river island on the Brahmaputra river in the state of Assam, in Northeast India, considered the world's largest inhabited river island. It is the course's case study: a VR tour was built to preserve its cultural and ecological heritage digitally, since floods and erosion threaten the island, and the tour is used to explain the stages of XR system development.33
Q072acronym_misspeltwho is jaron lenieryesJaron Lanier is the American who proposed the term 'virtual reality' towards the late 1980s, at the end of the formative years of VR history.6
Q073not_coveredHow do I use quaternions to represent the orientation of a headset?noThe course does not cover this. Quaternions are never mentioned. The tracking lectures (16 and 17) describe orientation estimation only in general terms: integrating the gyroscope's angular velocity and correcting drift.16, 17
Q074not_coveredHow does a Kalman filter fuse the IMU and camera data for tracking?noThe course does not cover the Kalman filter. For combining two sensors it gives only one classical approach to drift correction in lecture 17: blending the two orientation estimates with a gain parameter alpha between 0 and 1.17
Q075not_coveredDoes the course explain NeRF or Gaussian splatting for capturing real scenes?noNo. Neither is mentioned. For bringing real places into VR the course uses 360-degree images and videos, drone shots and 3D models built in Autodesk Maya (the Majuli case study), and it names SLAM and computer vision as ways to capture the real world, without detail.19, 34, 44
Q076not_coveredHow do I make a teleport mechanic with Unreal Engine Blueprints?noThe course does not cover this. Unreal Engine is only named as a popular game engine in lecture 20; the practical part of the course (lectures 60 to 62) uses Unity. Teleportation is taught as a locomotion technique (lecture 40), not its implementation in Unreal.20, 40, 63
Q077not_coveredHow are LLMs and generative AI used to build XR applications?noThe course does not cover this. The concluding lecture says so explicitly: LLMs and generative AI were not covered, although they can be used for enhanced interactivity; machine learning, computer vision, cloud computing, IoT, robotics and blockchain are also named as relevant areas left out.63
Q078not_coveredCan you explain how the SIFT algorithm detects features?noThe course does not explain it. SIFT (scale invariant feature transform) is only named in lecture 18, with SURF, BRIEF and BRISK, as a popular method for natural feature tracking; the concluding lecture states that computer vision was not discussed.18, 63
Q079not_coveredWhat is the pass mark for the final exam and how are assignments weighted?noThe lectures and the syllabus do not say. They hold no information on exams, assignment weights or pass marks; this is for the NPTEL course page or the course announcements.
Q080unclearwhat are the three characteristics?yesThe question does not say of what. The course gives three characteristics for two things. A VR system: an artificial environment, an immersive experience through the sense organs, and interactivity (lecture 4). An AR system, by the systemic definition: it combines real and virtual, is interactive in real time, and is registered in 3D (lecture 5; the lecture's own summary has four). A good answer asks which is meant or gives both, labelled.4, 5
Q081unclearhow does it work?yesNothing says what 'it' is. The right response is to ask what the learner means (for example tracking, a head mounted display, the graphics pipeline), perhaps offering a few topics of the course.
Q082unclearWhat are the phases?yesSeveral things in the course have phases: the history of VR (early era, formative years, modern phase, growth phase; lecture 6), the history of AR (experimental, basic research, tools and applications, commercial application; lecture 7), pose determination in AR (registration, then tracking; lecture 9), and the tracking process, whose steps are also called phases (calibration, registration, integration, drift correction; lecture 16). A good answer asks which or lists the candidates briefly.6, 7, 9, 16
Q083unclearwhat is registration?yesThe course uses the word in two related ways. In AR: content is 'registered in 3D' when it appears at its appropriate place in the real-world view, and the registration phase determines the viewer's pose relative to a real-world anchor (lectures 5 and 9). In the tracking process: registration is the second step, estimating the initial position and orientation of the sensor (lecture 16). A good answer gives both senses or asks.5, 9, 16
Q084unclearexplain the pipelineyesMost likely the 3D graphics pipeline of lectures 55 to 58: object representation, modeling transformation, lighting, the viewing pipeline, scan conversion. But 'pipeline' is also used for the viewing pipeline (its fourth stage) and for Unity's render pipelines (built-in, URP, HDRP; lecture 61). A good answer states which pipeline it explains, or asks.55, 61
Q085unclearis it the same as AR?yes'It' is not named. If mixed reality is meant, the course's answer is yes: MR is taken as another term for AR (lecture 5). If augmented virtuality is meant, no: it is the opposite (a virtual world augmented with real content; lecture 1). VR is different too: it replaces the perception of reality. A good answer asks what 'it' is or states the reading it answers.1, 5

The questions of the demo plan, each verified on the rebuilt layer with its time and citations, are in pipeline/continue/DEMO_PLAN.md (12 questions and 3 optional).

Part IV · Using it

Running it

How long a run takes, how to get from a fresh clone to a running viewer, how to run or rerun one stage, the EC2 workflow and the demo. Then the reference: the Python API, every command line, the HTTP routes, the configs and the environment variables. Commands run from pipeline/ unless they say otherwise.

Time estimates

What each stage took on the VR course (63 lectures, 31 hours of video), the hardware and the setting it ran with. These are wall times of whole batches, measured from the logs named in each row.

StageCallsConfig · parallel requestsGPUWall timeSource
0a—code—secondsoutput/logs/qwen_vllm_0a.log
1a6316k thinking · 32RTX PRO 600019 minqwen_vllm_1a.log
1b3716k · 32RTX PRO 6000≈ 8 minstage_1b…/llm/calls.jsonl
2a38616k · 32RTX PRO 60001 h 26 minnotebook, “2a on all 63 lectures”
2c7796k · 32L40S 46 GB3 h 53 minnotebook, “The MVP run”
3a—embeddingsRTX 5070 (local)< 1 minoutput/logs/mvp_run.log
3b4256k · 64RTX PRO 600024 minec2_sync/queue/logs/1.log (local)

4c, 4d—code (embeddings cached)—3 s6.log, 7.log (local)

5 one rewrite round246 + 2466k · 128RTX PRO 600034 min10.log (local)
5 recap re-judge; odd one out rerun (lectures 1, 26)347; 36k · 128; 10k · 128RTX PRO 600022 + 8 min11.log, 12.log (local)
Sum, one pass over the course (plus the 30 min of reruns: ≈ 11.3 h)≈ 10.8 h

Estimate for a 63-lecture course from scratch: about 11 GPU hours on one 96 GB GPU, plus about 25 minutes to set up a fresh instance (mostly model downloads; pipeline/continue/PROMPT.md) and 1–5 minutes per model load. This is the sum of the measured stages above, which ran with different settings: 1a, 1b and 2a with 32 parallel requests and a 16k thinking budget, 2c on a smaller GPU. Running every stage at 128 parallel requests on the 96 GB card would likely be faster; that was not measured. Throughput to plan with (vLLM, Qwen 3.8 27B FP8, RTX PRO 6000): about 1,650–1,850 output tokens per second with 128 requests in flight, about 1,340 with 64, about 45 for one request alone. The lir-construction README measured the same model at 752 tokens per second with 32 requests.

Per question once built: median 4.9 s on a 12 GB RTX 5070, 4.5 s on vLLM; first words after about 3 s.

From a fresh clone to a running viewer

The outputs are in git, so the viewer needs no model run. The full history is about 19 GB; clone the last commit of pipeline/ only (pipeline/demo/README.md):

git clone --depth 1 --filter=blob:none --sparse https://github.com/raghu-raman-b/mtp-implementation.git
cd mtp-implementation && git sparse-checkout set pipeline
cd pipeline
python3 viewer/serve.py nptel_vr              # http://localhost:8765 ; add ?dev=1 for developer mode

Python 3.11 or newer (configs are read with tomllib). The viewer and most stages need only the standard library. The question box needs more:

  • the query embedder: torch and sentence-transformers (the local PC runs torch==2.14.1, sentence-transformers==6.1.0, transformers==5.15.0; Qwen/Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B downloads on first use);
  • a model server: llama.cpp's llama-server and the Gemma 4 12B QAT GGUF (6.72 GB). The local config configs/gemma4_12b_llamacpp.json names this PC's binary, model path and toolbox; on another machine edit binary and model_path, or point QA_CONFIG at another config. On an Ubuntu GPU machine, demo/setup_demo.sh does all of it (Demo).
python3 viewer/serve.py nptel_vr --warm       # loads the layer, the embedder and the model at start
python3 -m qa "What is an HMD?" --lecture 4    # one question from the command line
python3 -m qa --stop                           # stop the model server it started

Running one stage, or the whole course

Each stage is one script with the course name, an optional --only and, for model stages, --llm <config>. Stages run in order; each reads the previous ones' folders. The full chain, as run on the EC2 queue:

python3 scripts/s0a_preprocess.py nptel_vr
python3 scripts/s1a_segmentation.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json
python3 scripts/s1b_verify_segments.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json
python3 scripts/s1c_segment_texts.py nptel_vr
python3 scripts/s2a_fact_extraction.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json
python3 scripts/s2b_verify_facts.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json --no-judge
python3 scripts/s2c_concepts.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k.json
python3 scripts/s3a_candidates.py nptel_vr
python3 scripts/s3b_same.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json
python3 scripts/s3c_registry.py nptel_vr
python3 scripts/s4a_definitions.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json
python3 scripts/s4b_goals.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json
python3 scripts/s4c_lecture_graph.py nptel_vr
python3 scripts/s4d_assemble.py nptel_vr
python3 scripts/s5_activities.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json --judge
python3 scripts/s5_activities.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json --kinds quiz,open_question --judge-rewrite
python3 scripts/s5_activities.py nptel_vr --check

A new course: put its subtitles under input/<course>/input/subs/, its titles.tsv (id, week, type, number, part, title, youtube_id) and a course.json written by hand from its syllabus (only title is required; the prompts use outline and syllabus). Stage 0a already runs on six courses (VR and five other MIT and NPTEL courses); only VR has been run through the whole pipeline.

Rerunning after a prompt change

Every model answer is cached by a hash of its request: the engine, the model path and the request body (prompt, schema, sampling, token limits, tools; for llama.cpp also the thinking budget, a server setting there). So:

  • Edit prompts/<stage>.txt and rerun the stage with the same config: only the changed requests reach the model; the rest are answered from llm/cache.jsonl in seconds. Then rerun the stages after it.
  • A config change that enters the request (model, sampling, max_tokens, think_budget) reruns everything of that stage. launch, attach_wait_s, slots and server flags do not: the 64- and 128-slot 96 GB configs share their hashes with qwen38_27b_vllm_6k.json.
  • 2b's rewriter must use 2a's config: it continues 2a's cached conversation (LLM.lookup).
  • A judge pass over finished activities only adds judge calls: writer requests are identical with or without --judge. The 2026-11-17 rewrite job found its 410 writer answers cached in 4 s.
  • To compare a variant without touching the outputs, write elsewhere: --out DIR exists on 2c, 3a–3c, 4a, 4b and 5; --seed N on 2c, 4a, 4b and 5.

The EC2 workflow

The repository, outputs and caches live on the local PC; the EC2 only serves models, or runs a queue of stages so long runs go on without the local session (pipeline/ec2.md). The same config file is used on both sides: on the EC2 scripts/llm/serve.py starts the server from it; locally the stages attach ("launch": false, waiting up to attach_wait_s, 30 min) and refuse a port that serves another model.

# once per instance (and after every stop: the NVMe model cache is wiped); ~25 min, mostly downloads
rsync -a --exclude __pycache__ --exclude 'input/*/media' --exclude 'output/logs' --exclude '.hf_token' pipeline/ mtp:pipeline/
ssh mtp "mkdir -p pipeline/output/logs && tmux new -d -s setup 'bash ~/pipeline/setup_ec2.sh 2>&1 | tee ~/pipeline/output/logs/setup.log'"

# A. run stages from the local PC against a model server on the EC2
ssh mtp "tmux new -d -s qwen 'cd ~/pipeline && python3 -m scripts.llm.serve configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json'"
ssh -f -N -L 8094:127.0.0.1:8094 -L 8095:127.0.0.1:8095 mtp
python3 scripts/s2a_fact_extraction.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm.json --only 1,4,16

# B. or queue them on the EC2 (each job: "<qwen|sage|none> <command>")
ssh mtp "cd pipeline && tmux new -d -s queue 'QWEN_CFG=configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json bash ~/pipeline/ec2_queue.sh'"
ssh mtp "echo 'qwen python3 -u scripts/s3b_same.py nptel_vr --llm configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json' >> pipeline/queue/jobs.txt"
ssh mtp tail -f pipeline/queue/queue.log
# from the repository root; the 2026-11-17 rebuild ran with configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json
bash pipeline/rebuild_queue.sh configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json    # queues 3b, 3c, 4a, 4b, 4c, 4d
  • ec2_queue.sh runs queue/jobs.txt line by line, starting the server each job needs and stopping the other (each takes about 92% of the GPU); done jobs go to queue/done.txt, logs to queue/logs/<line>.log. A failed job stops the queue until touch queue/resume (skip it) or touch queue/retry. A job can wait for a go-ahead, as the 2026-11-17 rebuild did before 4a: bash -c 'until [ -f queue/go_4a ]; do sleep 20; done'.
  • bash pipeline/continue/progress.sh makes the local tmux session mtp: a status view, the logs, and a sync loop that copies the EC2's output/ and queue/ into ec2_sync/ (gitignored) every 5 minutes. Results are merged into pipeline/output/ (the local cache must be a prefix of the EC2's) and committed.
  • Qwen loads in about 1 minute with its weights in the page cache, 5 cold; Sage in about 5. The 27B FP8 does not fit a 32 GB card (weights about 28 GB); nvidia/Qwen3.8-27B-NVFP4 is the noted fallback.
  • No real model runs except on an instance the user started; plumbing is checked first with fake servers (tests/fake_*.py).

The demo

As of 2026-11-17 · being redesigned

As on main: the viewer and its question box run on one GPU instance, and the laptop reaches it through an SSH port forward. demo/setup_demo.sh sets it all up; demo/check_demo.sh checks it end to end (pipeline/demo/README.md). Another agent is changing this setup to keep only the model on the server (the design).

rsync -a --exclude __pycache__ --exclude 'input/*/media' --exclude 'output/logs' --exclude 'output/*/qa' \
      --exclude 'demo/logs' --exclude '.hf_token' pipeline demo:mtp-implementation/            # about 170 MB
ssh demo "tmux new -d -s setup 'bash ~/mtp-implementation/pipeline/demo/setup_demo.sh 2>&1 | tee ~/setup_demo.log'"
ssh -N -L 8765:127.0.0.1:8765 demo                     # then open http://localhost:8765
ssh demo bash mtp-implementation/pipeline/demo/check_demo.sh
  • setup_demo.sh, about 20 minutes the first time and 2 on a rerun: GPU check; the model cache on the NVMe; system packages; a venv with torch and sentence-transformers while Gemma 4 12B QAT (6.72 GB) and the embedder (1.2 GB) download; llama.cpp built for the GPU's architecture at the commit the PC runs (LLAMA_REF); the demo config filled in; tmux sessions gemma and viewer (with --warm); two test questions. Options: VIEWER_HOST, VIEWER_PORT, LLAMA_REF=master, TORCH_CPU=1, NO_NVME=1, PYTHON=python3.12, HF_TOKEN.
  • check_demo.sh prints PASS / FAIL / SKIP per item: the GPU, packages, binary, model file, embedder, tmux sessions, the model server, the viewer's routes, a Range request on a video, every lecture having a video, and one question answered with citations. On 2026-11-17 on an RTX PRO 6000: ALL PASS; “What is an HMD?” in 2.9 s with 4 citations (analysis/mvp/demo_ec2_2026-11-17/check_demo.txt).
  • demo_tool.py: helpers for both (fill the config, wait for the model, count media, ask, HTTP checks); standard library only.
  • Videos (5.5 GB) are optional: without them each lecture plays from YouTube. --prefer-youtube (or ?yt=1) uses YouTube even when files are there, so video never crosses the tunnel.
  • vLLM instead of llama.cpp: configs/gemma4_12b_vllm_demo.json, benchmarked at median 4.5 s, max 6.6 s on the RTX PRO 6000; start steps in analysis/qa/STATE.md.
  • Demo day: choose a region near the venue and time the tunnel first (time (ssh demo "head -c 2000000 /dev/urandom" | wc -c), under about 2 s); from the user's network to us-east-1 SSH ran at about 40 kB/s. Warm the fact search with one off-stage question (the first use of the embedder took 20–23 s). The walk and the verified questions are in pipeline/continue/DEMO_PLAN.md.

Reference

Python API

Question answering: qa

from qa import answer, answer_stream, warm

r = answer("What is an HMD?", course="nptel_vr", lecture="4")      # lecture: optional
r["answer"]      # text, citations written as "(lecture 4, 6:01)"
r["citations"]   # [{"fact", "lecture", "segment", "start", "end", "text"}]
r["steps"]       # [{"tool", "args", "result_summary"}]
r["covered"]     # True for "COVERED: yes" or "partly"

for ev in answer_stream("What is an HMD?", course="nptel_vr"):
    ...          # {"event": "step", "tool", "args", "result_summary"}   after each tool call
                 # {"event": "draft", "text"}       the answer so far (replaces the previous draft)
                 # {"event": "reset"}               text of a round that ended in tool calls is not the answer
                 # {"event": "done", "result": {...}}   what answer() returns

warm("nptel_vr")  # load the layer and the embedder, start or attach to the model server

Also exported: get_llm(config, think), Knowledge (the layer: Knowledge.load(course)), run_tool(kb, name, args), tool_specs(). Module switches in qa.answer: MAX_ROUNDS = 5 (at most 5 rounds of tool calls, then one answer round), ROUND0_THINK = True, ROUND0_TOOL_CHOICE = "auto". Tool constants in qa.tools: CARD_FACTS = 10, LOOKUP_K = 8, LOW_SCORE_DEFAULT = 0.55, UNKNOWN_K = 5, DEP_MIN_USES = 2.

The LLM client: scripts.llm

from scripts.llm import LLM, LLMConfig, Request

cfg = LLMConfig.load("configs/qwen38_27b_vllm_6k_96gb.json")      # .json or .toml; cfg.with_(slots=8) a changed copy
llm = LLM(cfg).start()            # attach to a running server, or launch one when "launch" is true
req = Request(prompt="...", key="lec_5", schema={...}, check=lambda parsed: [], feedback=True)
res = llm.generate(req, cache_file="out/llm/cache.jsonl", log_file="out/llm/calls.jsonl")
res.ok, res.parsed, res.content, res.reasoning, res.errors, res.input_tokens, res.output_tokens, res.cached
many = llm.generate_many([req, ...], parallel=True)               # all at once; results in request order
llm.lookup(req, cache_file=...)   # a cached answer without sending
llm.stop()                        # stops only a server this LLM started

Request also takes system, followup (more turns), max_tokens, think_budget, sampling, meta, tools, tool_choice; Result has tool_calls, history, finish, attempts, hash, latency_s. Server side: python3 -m scripts.llm.serve <config> [--smoke] starts the server a config describes and keeps it running (Ctrl-C or a hang-up stops it). scripts/llm/fake_server.py answers like vLLM with JSON built from the request's schema, for tests.

Stages as functions

Every stage script has run(), e.g. s1a_segmentation.run(course, llm, lectures=None, parallel=True), s1b_verify_segments.run(course, lectures=None, llm=None), s2c_concepts.run(course, llm, lectures=None, seed=None, out=None), s3a_candidates.run(course, lectures=None, embed_model=…, threshold=0.70, cap=12, …), s4a_definitions.run(course, llm, cap=40, seed=None, out=None), s4c_lecture_graph.run(course, params=None), s5_activities.run(course, llm, only=None, kinds=None, …). scripts.view.Course(course) loads every stage's output into dicts for a notebook (c.sentences["1/0"], c.segments["1/S0"], c.lecture(1)). scripts.embed.Embedder(model, cache, device).embed(texts, instruction=None) returns L2-normalised vectors.

HTTP routes of the viewer

As of 2026-11-17 · being redesigned
RouteAnswers
GET /, /static/…the page; every static file named with ?v=<hash> and sent Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable
GET /api/hometitle, syllabus, course goals, the lectures (key, title, week, goal), the layers present
GET /api/view/<key>what a lecture page needs: segments, sentences, goal, objectives, the graph links into it (before), activities (with unresolved, judge_failed, judge_hide), its concept map, and only the concepts and facts it refers to
GET /api/glossarythe defined concepts (name, other names, definition, where first explained, linked); connecting concepts by name only
GET /api/concept/<C0042>one card: definition, where explained, facts, relationships
GET /api/coursemap, /api/reviewthe lecture graph for the course map; the review cards
GET /api/course, /api/lecture/<key>the older, larger routes (kept)
GET /data/knowledge/<file>, /data/activities/<file>raw layer files (not fact_embeddings.json); 404 when missing
GET /media/<key>the video with HTTP Range; an open range is answered with at most 4 MB; after the first 1 MB sent at twice the bitrate
POST /api/ask{"question", "lecture"} → qa.answer(…), or {"available": false, …} when qa cannot be imported
POST /api/ask_streamthe same, as NDJSON (application/x-ndjson, chunked): one {"event": "step"|"draft"|"reset"} per line as they come, last {"event": "done", "result": …}; the page falls back to /api/ask

Lean routes are built once, kept in memory until a source file changes, gzip-compressed and sent with an ETag (a reload answers 304). From the docstring of viewer/serve.py.

Running the tests

The tests need no GPU: a fake model server answers with JSON built from each request's schema, on a free port, in a temporary copy of the pipeline folder. Each test lists what it would look like if the stage were broken and seeds known errors. Run them from pipeline/; each ends with PASS or the failing checks. Stop the SSH port forward before fake_run.py (it uses the EC2 configs' ports 8094 and 8095), and run them when no other run is writing logs under output/logs/.

python3 tests/fake_run.py          # 2a and 2b on lectures 1, 4, 16 through the EC2 configs: caches, model swap, no output touched
python3 tests/fake_2c.py           # stage 2c: checks, retries with feedback, best attempt kept (44 checks)
python3 tests/fake_registry.py     # stage 3: exact merge, candidates, groups, merge log, relationships rebuilt
python3 tests/fake_knowledge.py    # stages 4a-4d: definitions, goals, lecture graph, the layer and its check
python3 tests/fake_activities.py   # stage 5: every kind, code checks, the judge and rewrite paths, the contract
python3 tests/fake_qa.py           # the tool loop, citations, early exit, streaming, the vLLM config; --layer DIR
python3 tests/viewer_smoke.py      # every route, 304/gzip, versioned static files, Range, /api/ask_stream
Part V · Limits and what's next

Known limits

What is weak in the course as built, from the demo plan's “avoid on stage” list and the status notes, then the fixes planned next, then longer-term work. Each limit names where it shows.

LimitWhere it showsSize
Recap texts the judge failed are still shown (the hiding rule hides recaps only for source talk)4/A2, 9/A7, 30/A2, 30/A4, 30/A8, 36/A2, 36/A4 …222 of 347 texts; 371 failed points
Crosswords with judge-failed clues are shown (a clue cannot leave a grid)51/A21 (6 of 12 clues), 40/A18 (5), 57/A16 (5 of 8), 29/A11 (4), 5/A12 (3 of 5) …82 failed clues
Duplicate concepts left after the mergehead-mounted display / virtual reality headset (both defined, together on 7 lecture maps); interaction / user interaction; iPhone operating system / ios—
Two concepts share an aliasfield of view / horizontal field of view (both “FOV”); “VR” also matches “virtual reality headset”—
Odd “also known as” listspresence (“present”, “presence results”, “presence ratings”); virtual reality system (“system”)—
Connecting-concept cards with odd role sentences“control”, “live-action 180-degree video”, “gigabyte”, “real-world view”, “ios”1,806 connecting concepts
Formulas print as raw LaTeX in answersthe Kalman question printed $\theta_{dk} = \alpha \theta_{gk} + ...$ in 2 of 3 runs—
The first question that uses the fact search loads the embedder20–23 s without --warmonce
Comparisons of several concepts are slow9–19 s—
Selecting a hub concept in the full map view crowds the labels“game engine” in lecture 20—
The fact judge is offthin facts such as 1/S0/F0 stayall 2,006 facts unjudged
The activity judge is not measured against a seed setit only flags—
Relationships only within a segment (D4)links between concepts taught in different segments are never found—
Prerequisite links count any single mention“before lecture 30” is thin: only lecture 29's frequency, plus XR and VR—

From pipeline/continue/DEMO_PLAN.md (“Avoid on stage”) and STATUS.md; counts from activities/checks.json.

Upcoming fixes

Recaps

Show only passing points

222 of 347 recap texts and 371 points failed the judge. Plan: show only the points that pass, and optionally have the model rewrite the text with the judge's reasons.

Crosswords

Drop failed words, lay out again

A failed clue cannot be hidden inside a grid. Plan: drop the words whose clues fail and run the layout again on the rest.

Duplicates

A reviewed merge list

HMD / VR headset, interaction / user interaction, iOS / iPhone OS: merged by a list a person reviews, applied in 3c, so the merge is logged like the others.

Aliases

FOV and odd alias lists

One owner for a shared alias such as FOV; drop aliases like “present” and “system” that are not names of the concept.

Connecting concepts

Show their facts

On a connecting concept's card, show the lecture facts that use it instead of the model's one-sentence role.

Answers

Render formulas

Render LaTeX in answers (KaTeX, bundled with the page so it works offline).

First question

Warm-up

Load the embedder and ask one hidden question at start, so no learner waits 20 s.

Viewer

The redesign

Being done by another agent as of 2026-11-17.

Deployment

Remote-model setup

Only the model on the GPU server; the viewer, layer, videos and tool loop on the learner's machine (design).

Evaluation

Baseline experiments

Plain LLM, RAG over transcripts and graph-only on the same questions, scored for correctness, grounding, citations, “not covered” and time (placeholder).

Future work: what can be improved further

IdeaWhy, and what in the repository points to it
Finish the planned gatesThe block tests of PLAN.md were deferred by the MVP: the fact-judge rerun with the revised prompts (block 1), concept gold (block 2), pair gold (block 3), the scored question set (block 6, draft of 85 questions on a branch).
A judge for every kind, measured firstThe activity judge runs on six kinds and is not yet measured on its seed set; recall, match the pairs and complete the map have no judge. The fact judge needs majority voting or another fix for its 15% flips.
A smaller or distilled answer modelThe answer time is mostly model time (round 0 about 1.6 s, the final answer about 2.6 s at about 60 tokens per second). A smaller model, or one tuned on the course's tool calls, would cut it; tool choice by a small model is the known risk (B17).
Faster decodingAn FP8 Gemma checkpoint on vLLM would roughly double decode speed over BF16 (analysis/qa/STATE.md); speculative decoding is untried; a forced first tool call works on vLLM but needs a guard (it skipped the search on B17).
Batch the whole build1a, 1b, 2a and 2c ran at 32 parallel requests; 4a reached about 1,800 tokens per second at 128. Running every stage at 128 on one GPU, and stages whose inputs are ready side by side, would shorten the 10.8 hours.
Incremental updatesThe cache already skips unchanged requests. A lecture that changes reruns its own segments, facts and concepts; the registry, definitions of the concepts it touches and the graph would then need a targeted rerun rather than a full one.
Relationships across segmentsD4: a later pass over concept pairs that share a lecture but never a segment.
Cross-course concept linkingStage 0a already runs on six courses (VR and five others); one registry over several courses would let a learner follow a concept from one course to another.
Learner modellingReview cards are scheduled per learner in the browser only; quiz answers could feed the schedule and the recall before a lecture.
Multilingual subtitlesThe pipeline trusts human punctuation (0a); other languages need their own splitting rules and prompts in that language or translation of the layer.
Evaluation at scaleMore than one human annotator for segmentation, blind gold for facts and concepts, the question set scored automatically, and the baselines.

Schemas

The activities contract pipeline/schemas/activities.md
# Activities: output contract (MVP, 2026-11-13)

Written by `pipeline/scripts/s5_activities.py` into `pipeline/output/<course>/activities/`; read by the viewer.
All IDs are the knowledge layer's (`knowledge/`, stage 4d): facts `4/S1/F2`, segments `4/S1`, concepts `C0042`,
relationships `R0107`, lectures by key ("4").

`activities/lec_<n>.json`:
```json
{"lecture": "4",
 "activities": [{"id": "4/A0", "kind": "quiz", "scope": "segment", "segment": "4/S1", "title": "...",
                 "data": {...}, "facts": ["4/S1/F0"], "concepts": ["C0003"]}]}
```
`scope` is "segment" (with `segment`) or "lecture" (`segment` null). `facts` and `concepts`: what the activity is built
from (every activity must be answerable from its facts). `data` by `kind`:

| kind | scope | data |
|---|---|---|
| `quiz` | segment | `{"questions": [{"question", "options": [4 strings], "answer": index, "explanation", "facts": [IDs]}]}` |
| `recap` | segment | `{"text": "...", "points": ["..."]}` |
| `recall` | lecture (before it) | `{"items": [{"concept": "C..", "prompt": "...", "answer": "...", "from_lecture": "1"}]}` |
| `open_question` | lecture | `{"questions": [{"question", "reference_answer", "key_facts": [IDs], "concepts": [IDs]}]}` |
| `concept_map` | lecture | `{"nodes": [{"id": "C..", "label": "..."}], "edges": [{"from": "C..", "to": "C..", "label": "...", "relation": "R.."}]}` |
| `complete_map` | lecture | concept_map's `nodes`, `edges` plus `"blanks": [{"edge": index, "hide": "label"\|"from"\|"to", "answer": "...", "options": [strings]}]` |
| `crossword` | lecture | `{"width", "height", "entries": [{"number", "answer": "UPPERCASE LETTERS ONLY", "clue", "row", "col", "direction": "across"\|"down", "concept": "C.."}]}` |
| `match_pairs` | lecture | `{"pairs": [{"left": "term", "right": "short definition", "concept": "C.."}]}` |
| `odd_one_out` | lecture | `{"items": [{"options": [4 strings], "odd": index, "reason", "facts": [IDs]}]}` |
| `put_in_order` | segment or lecture | `{"items": [{"title", "steps": ["in the correct order"], "facts": [IDs]}]}` |

`activities/course.json`: `{"spaced_review": {"cards": [{"concept": "C..", "front": "name", "back": "definition",
"lectures": ["1", "4"]}]}}` (the viewer schedules the cards per learner in the browser).

A kind may be missing for a lecture or segment when its material does not allow it (no steps: no `put_in_order`).

Additions (2026-11-13, requested by the viewer; everything above unchanged):
- Optional per-item `"facts": [fact IDs]` (for time links): on each `concept_map` and `complete_map` edge (the
  relationship's facts), each `crossword` entry and each `match_pairs` pair (the concept's definition facts). Present
  whenever the material gives them; always a subset of the activity's `facts`.
- `complete_map`: a blank that hides `from` or `to` hides a concept that appears on no other edge of the map, so the
  answer is not visible elsewhere (blanks on shared concepts hide the `label` instead).
The knowledge layer's JSON schema pipeline/schemas/knowledge.schema.json
{
 "$comment": "The knowledge layer (output/<course>/knowledge/), checked by scripts/schema_check.py, which supports only the keywords used here. x-ref marks a string that refers to an ID of a kind, x-id a string that defines one; x-key-id and x-key-ref do the same for the keys of an object. Sentence IDs are defined by the first..last range of each segment in lectures/*.json. x-files names the definition each file of the layer is checked against.",
 "x-files": {
  "index.json": "index_file",
  "names.json": "names_file",
  "concepts.json": "concepts_file",
  "relations.json": "relations_file",
  "facts.json": "facts_file",
  "fact_embeddings.json": "embeddings_file",
  "lectures/*.json": "lecture_file",
  "goals.json": "goals_file",
  "lecture_graph.json": "lecture_graph_file",
  "assumed.json": "assumed_file"
 },
 "$defs": {
  "lecture": {
   "type": "string",

Glossary of terms

TermMeaning here
sentencea unit of the subtitles after stage 0a, ID lecture/index, with start and end times when they fall on a cue edge
segmentthe run of sentences in which the learner achieves one sub-goal; ID 30/S4
sub-goalwhat the learner can do after a segment, starting with a verb
objective, lecture goal, course goalthe three levels above the sub-goals (4b): a run of segments, a lecture, several lectures
factone complete point of subject knowledge a segment teaches, with the sentences that state it (its evidence)
segment concepta term named in one segment's facts (2c), with defined true when those facts say what it is
concepta course-wide term after the registry (3), ID C0042; defined or connecting
defined concepta concept the course explains; it has a written definition and is in the glossary (1,316)
connecting concepta concept the course only uses; it has a one-sentence role, stays on maps and cards, not in the glossary (1,806)
relationshipa directed triple subject – label – object stated by facts; labels are free text
lecture linkK → L when lecture L uses a concept K defines; direct, indirect or weak (4c)
hub concepta concept used by more than max(3, 25%) of the lectures; it weighs less in the lecture graph
knowledge layerthe folder knowledge/ stage 4d writes and checks
unitwhat one activity call covers: a segment (quiz, recap) or a lecture (the rest)
code check (hard / soft)a rule checked in code on a model answer; hard drops a still-failing item, soft keeps and flags it
judgea model call that checks finished items against named criteria
unresolvedevery attempt failed a check; the attempt with the fewest problems was kept
request hashthe key of the answer cache: engine, model path and the request body
round (QA)one model call in the tool loop; round 0 chooses tools with thinking on
coveredthe answer's own verdict, from its last line: the course answers the question (yes or partly) or not
F (Fable), O (Opus), U (user)the roles of PLAN.md: Fable plans, writes prompts and gold, reviews; Opus writes scripts, tests and reports; the user decides the design
gatethe end of a block: a report, a review by the block-reviewer agent, then “proceed” or “change”
MVPthe full-course run of 2026-11-13 (tag mvp-2026-11-13), with 2b, 3b and model-written 4a skipped

This page: pipeline/docs/pipeline.html, written for the v3 pipeline as of 2026-11-17 (main at db10844). One file, no network: styles, scripts and diagrams are inline.
